These are schematics of the proved classification, not experimental graphs.}
\label{evo:et:fig:graphs}

Manuscript~03 adds a small test, used for the minimality of witnesses.

\begin{lemma}[A minimality test; manuscript~03]\label{evo:da:lem:minimal}
If an accessible $n$-state DFAO with $k$ outputs has an orbit with more than
$k^{n-1}$ colorings, the original DFAO is minimal.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If two original states were indistinguishable, every orbit coloring would
agree on those states. Only $k^{n-1}$ colorings have that property.
Accessibility and pairwise distinguishability characterize minimality.
\end{proof}

This is the argument Part~I uses after \cref{prop:finite} and Part~II in the
proof of \cref{tor:thm:main}, stated once as a lemma.

\subsection{The two unmixed generator cases}\label{evo:sec:unmixed}

\begin{lemma}[Manuscript~06; also manuscript~03]\label{evo:lem:easy}
For any $a,b:Q\to Q$ and any $\tau:Q\to[k]$, with $M=\langle a,b\rangle$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item if both $a$ and $b$ are permutations, then $|\tau M|\le k^{n-1}$;
\item if both are singular, then
\[
 |\tau M|\le k^n-(k-1)^2k^{n-2}+1.
\]
\end{enumerate}
The second statement is used only for $n\ge3$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A permutation preserves the number of occurrences of each output color.
Within a family with fixed color multiplicities, restriction to any fixed
$n-1$ coordinates is injective: the omitted color is determined by the
multiplicities. This proves (i).

For (ii), choose collision pairs $\{x_a,y_a\}$ and $\{x_b,y_b\}$ for the two
singular transformations. A coloring obtained from a nonempty word is equal
on the collision pair for its rightmost generator. Hence it cannot be a
proper coloring of the graph consisting of these one or two edges. Two
distinct edges form a forest, with exactly $(k-1)^2k^{n-2}$ proper colorings;
this holds whether they share a vertex or are disjoint. If the selected edges
coincide, there are even more proper colorings. Only the empty word, producing
$\tau$, can contribute a proper coloring.
\end{proof}

Manuscript~03 proves the same two bounds, as deficits
$\defi\ge(k-1)k^{n-1}$ and $\defi\ge(k-1)^2k^{n-2}-1$, by the same argument
(``after specifying the first $n-1$ positions, the last one is determined if
completion is possible''). Part~I's entries \eqref{eq:Dperm} and
\eqref{eq:Dsing} are the same two cases; the first uses the balanced
multinomial $M(n,k)$, which is at most $k^{n-1}$ by (i).

\begin{remark}[Manuscript~01's version]\label{evo:et:rem:unmixed}
Manuscript~01 states the unmixed cases with a weaker two-permutation bound
and adds the nonsurjective case: if both generators are permutations, the
coloring-orbit deficit is at least $k^{n-1}$ (after fixing the values at
$n-1$ vertices, at most $k-1$ choices at the last vertex can have the
prescribed count of a chosen color, so the orbit has at most $(k-1)k^{n-1}$
colorings); if both are singular, it is at least $(k-1)^2k^{n-2}-1$, by the
argument above; and a nonsurjective output map has deficit at least
$k^n-(k-1)^n$, because all reached colorings use at most the $k-1$ colors
appearing initially (Part~I's \eqref{eq:Dnon}). All three lower bounds grow
faster than $\lambda_k^n$, since $k>\lambda_k$.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Bounds on permutation orders}\label{evo:sec:order}

\begin{lemma}[Elementary permutation-order bound; manuscripts~06 and~03]\label{evo:lem:order}
A permutation on $r$ points has order at most $3^{r/3}$, including order one
when $r=0$. In the cross-cycle case, with the notation of \eqref{eq:diffcount},
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:crossorder}
 \ord(p)\le duv\,3^{r/3};
\end{equation}
in the same-cycle case, $\ord(p)\le m\,3^{r/3}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The order divides the product of the cycle lengths. Each positive integer $j$
satisfies $j\le3^{j/3}$: check $j=1,2,3$ and note that $\log x/x$ is
decreasing for $x\ge3$. Multiplication gives the bound. The active cycles have
least common multiple $\lcm(du,dv)=duv$ in the cross-cycle case, and length
$m$ in the same-cycle case. Multiply their order by the residual bound.
\end{proof}

\noindent\emph{Second proof of $j\le3^{j/3}$ (manuscript~03).} Check
$j=1,2,3$, and use $j+3\le3j$ for $j\ge2$ to induct in steps of three, with
$j=4$ checked directly. Manuscript~03 then uses the crude forms
$\Pi\le n3^{r/3}$ (one cycle) and $\Pi\le n^23^{r/3}$ (two cycles), which it
calls intentionally crude and uses only after an exponential separation has
made their precision irrelevant.

The bound $3^{r/3}$ is Part~II's $J(r)\le3^{r/3}$ (\cref{tor:lem:product}).
No asymptotic estimate for the maximal order of a permutation is required; in
particular manuscript~06's proof does not depend on a precise estimate for
Landau's function. Manuscript~01 uses a different, asymptotic bound.

\begin{lemma}[Subexponential order bound; manuscript~01]\label{evo:et:lem:order}
Uniformly over all permutations $p$ of $n$ objects,
\[
 \log\ord(p)=O(\sqrt n\log n).
\]
In particular, for every fixed real $\sigma>1$, $\ord(p)=o(\sigma^n)$ uniformly
in $p$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Put $L=\lceil\sqrt n\rceil$. The least common multiple of the cycle lengths
at most $L$ divides $L!$. There are at most $n/(L+1)$ longer cycles, each of
length at most $n$. Thus
\[
 \ord(p)\le L!\,n^{n/(L+1)}.
\]
Taking logarithms proves the assertion. This deliberately avoids using a
sharp asymptotic formula for Landau's function.
\end{proof}

Manuscript~01's finite certificates use instead the exact product bound:
every permutation of $r$ points has order at most $J(r)$ (Part~II's
\eqref{tor:eq:J}, manuscript~01's $A(r)$), because the least common multiple
of its cycle lengths is at most their product. Manuscript~01 proves the
closed form of $J$ as Part~II does (a part at least five can be replaced by
three and its remainder to increase the product; ones are removed in a
maximizing partition of an integer at least two, except that $r=1$ must be
retained separately; three twos are improved by two threes; a leftover four
can be retained or replaced by two twos), and its independent checker
computes $J(r)=\max_{1\le j\le r}jJ(r-j)$ without the formula, as Part~II's
does.

\section{The exact lower witness from the two-generation theorem}\label{evo:sec:witness}

Parts~I--II and manuscripts~06 and~03 cite Davies's Theorem~3 and
Corollary~3 for attainment. Manuscript~01 isolates the external algebraic
ingredient more narrowly, as the Holzer--K\"onig two-generation theorem, and
proves exactly which colorings the resulting monoid gives. This section is
manuscript~01's. Fix $1<a<b$, $\gcd(a,b)=1$, and $Q=X\sqcup Y$ with $|X|=a$,
$|Y|=b$; let $p$ cycle $X$ and $Y$ separately.

\begin{definition}\label{evo:et:def:U}
The monoid $U_{a,b}$ consists of the powers of $p$, together with all
transformations $t:Q\to Q$ such that
\[
 t(X)\cap t(Y)\ne\varnothing
 \quad\text{and}\quad Y\not\subseteq t(Q).
\]
\end{definition}

The published two-generator theorem says that this monoid is generated by $p$
and one singular transformation \cite[Theorem~8]{HolzerKoenig2004}; Davies
records the theorem and explicit generators \cite[Section~3]{Davies2017}. The
explicit generator is recorded in Davies's account of Krawetz's construction
\cite{Krawetz2003}. Number the states $0,\ldots,a+b-1$, with
$X=\{0,\ldots,a-1\}$. Start with the identity array, change $s(0)$ to $a$ and
$s(a+b-1)$ to $0$, and, when $a$ is odd, interchange $s(1)$ and $s(2)$. This is
the witness of Part~II's \cref{tor:sec:witnesses} and of Part~I's
\texttt{u\_witness}, and the convention of the verification code. The theorem
is used as an imported theorem, not as a computational inference.

\begin{lemma}[Projection saturates improper colorings]\label{evo:et:lem:saturation}
Suppose $\tau:Q\to\D$ is surjective, its palettes on $X$ and $Y$ are
disjoint, and two distinct points of $Y$ have the same output. Then
\begin{equation}\label{evo:et:eq:saturation}
 \tau U_{a,b}
 =\{c:c\text{ is improper for }K_{a,b}\}
   \;\sqcup\;\{\tau\circ p^j:j\ge0\}.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Every nonpermutation member of $U_{a,b}$ identifies a cross pair, so its
projected coloring is improper. Every power of $p$ preserves the disjoint
palettes and gives a proper coloring.

Conversely, choose a point $z\in Y$ whose output has another representative
in $Y$. Choose one representative $q_c\ne z$ for every output color $c$. For
any improper coloring $h$, define $t(q)=q_{h(q)}$. There is a cross pair with
the same $h$-value, hence the same $t$-value, and $z\notin t(Q)$. Therefore
$t\in U_{a,b}$ and $\tau\circ t=h$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Maximum proper period]\label{evo:et:lem:properperiod}
If $p$ has two cycles of coprime lengths $a\le b$, the orbit length of a
proper $k$-coloring of $K_{a,b}$ is at most $G_k(a,b)$. For $k\ge4$, this
bound is attained whenever both cycles can be given primitive words using
disjoint palettes of at least two colors. For $k=3$ and $b\ge2$, it is
attained by a constant word on the shorter cycle and a primitive two-color
word on the longer.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Every coloring orbit length divides $\lcm(a,b)=ab$. With at most three colors,
disjoint nonempty palettes force at least one cycle to be monochromatic. Its
coloring period is one, so the full period divides the length of the other
cycle and is at most $b$. A cyclic word in which one color occurs exactly
once has its full length as period. Using such words on both cycles, when
possible, gives period $ab$ by coprimality.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Attainable exact formula]\label{evo:et:prop:witness}
For $n\ge7$ and $3\le k<n$, there is a minimal binary $n$-state DFAO with
\[
 |\tau\langle p,s\rangle|=k^n-\Bip_k(a_n,b_n)+G_k(a_n,b_n).
\]
In particular, $D_k(n)\le D^*_k(n)$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For $k=3$, use one color on $X$ and two on $Y$, the latter in a primitive word
with a repeated color. For $k\ge4$, put
\[
 i=\min(a_n,k-2),\qquad j=k-i.
\]
Then $2\le i\le a_n$ and $2\le j\le b_n-1$. Give $X$ exactly $i$ colors and
$Y$ the other $j$, using a primitive word on each. Such a word of length $L$
with $t\ge2$ prescribed colors is obtained by writing each color once and
filling the remaining positions with the last color; the first color occurs
exactly once. The bound $j\le b_n-1$ ensures a repeated color on $Y$. Apply
\cref{evo:et:lem:saturation,evo:et:lem:properperiod}.

The automaton is accessible: $p$ reaches every state of $X$ from $0$, and
$s(0)=a_n$ enters $Y$, where $p$ reaches every remaining state. It is minimal
as well. For distinct $q,r$, color $r$ with one color and all other states
with another. This coloring separates $q$ and $r$ and is improper for
$K_{a_n,b_n}$, since both parts have at least two vertices and therefore have
a cross pair avoiding $r$. By \cref{evo:et:lem:saturation}, it equals
$\tau\circ t$ for a word transformation $t$. The corresponding continuation
distinguishes $q,r$.
\end{proof}

This is Davies's lower bound, Part~I's $L(n,k)$ at the nearest split, with
the saturation step proved and only the generation theorem imported. It also
discharges part of the ``lower bound as a separate obligation'' of Part~II's
formalization plan (\cref{tor:sec:formal}): given generation of $U_{a,b}$, the
saturation identity is elementary.

\section{Strict balancing of complete bipartite colorings}\label{evo:sec:balance}

A structural comparison still has infinitely many possible side sizes. The
next result removes that freedom exactly rather than approximately. A leading
exponential estimate cannot by itself select a unique split when $k$ is even:
a whole interval of proportions has the same leading term
(\cref{evo:sec:plateau}). Manuscripts~06 and~01 therefore both prove an exact
balancing inequality, by different routes; both are printed.

\subsection{Order-two total positivity of Stirling numbers}

\begin{lemma}[Manuscript~06]\label{evo:lem:stirlingtp}
For $1\le r\le s$ and $1\le i<j$,
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:stirlingtp}
 S(r,i)S(s,j)\ge S(r,j)S(s,i).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
We first prove log-concavity of every row. Define the Touchard polynomial
$T_r(x)=\sum_iS(r,i)x^i$ (unrelated to Part~I's $T_{r,k}$). The Stirling
recurrence gives
\[
 T_{r+1}(x)=x\bigl(T_r(x)+T_r'(x)\bigr).
\]
Inductively, $T_r$ has $r$ distinct real roots, one at zero and the others
negative. The base case is $T_1=x$. If the claim holds for $T_r$, the function
$e^xT_r(x)$ has a critical point in each interval between successive roots.
It also has one to the left of the leftmost root: it tends to zero as
$x\to-\infty$, is zero at that root, and is nonzero in between, so an interior
extremum exists. Thus $T_r+T_r'$ has at least $r$ distinct negative roots.
Since its degree is $r$, these are all its roots. Multiplication by $x$
supplies the new simple root at zero.

For completeness, real nonpositive roots imply the needed coefficient
log-concavity without invoking an unproved property of the Stirling array.
Every derivative of $T_r$ also has real nonpositive roots, by Rolle's
theorem. For a polynomial $g$ with such roots and $x>0$,
\[
 g'(x)^2-g(x)g''(x)
   =g(x)^2\sum_{\rho:g(\rho)=0}\frac1{(x-\rho)^2}\ge0,
\]
where roots are counted with multiplicity. Apply this to $g=T_r^{(i-1)}$ and
let $x\downarrow0$. It yields
\[
 (i!\,S(r,i))^2\ge (i-1)!\,S(r,i-1)\,(i+1)!\,S(r,i+1),
\]
and hence ordinary log-concavity $S(r,i)^2\ge S(r,i-1)S(r,i+1)$. On the
positive support, the ratios $S(r,i-1)/S(r,i)$ are therefore nondecreasing in
$i$.

Now compare adjacent rows using the recurrence:
\begin{align*}
&S(r,i)S(r+1,j)-S(r,j)S(r+1,i)\\
&\quad=(j-i)S(r,i)S(r,j)
 +S(r,i)S(r,j-1)-S(r,j)S(r,i-1)\ge0.
\end{align*}
The ratio observation proves the final inequality whenever the displayed
entries are positive. At the support boundary, the zero entries make the same
inequality immediate. Finally, for fixed $i<j$ with positive entries, these
adjacent-row inequalities say that $S(r,j)/S(r,i)$ is nondecreasing as the row
index increases. Iterating from $r$ to $s$ gives \eqref{evo:eq:stirlingtp};
the remaining zero cases again have the required sign.
\end{proof}

\noindent\emph{Second proof (manuscript~01).} Manuscript~01 proves row
log-concavity without real-rootedness.

\begin{lemma}[Stirling rows are log-concave; manuscript~01]\label{evo:et:lem:logconcavity}
For $n\ge1$, the positive support of $S(n,i)$ is $1\le i\le n$, and
$S(n,i)^2\ge S(n,i-1)S(n,i+1)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The support assertion follows from the interpretation by set partitions. For
a nonnegative log-concave sequence $(x_i)$ with no internal zeros, put
$y_i=ix_i+x_{i-1}$. Extend the sequence by zero outside its support. Direct
expansion gives
\begin{align*}
 y_i^2-y_{i-1}y_{i+1}
 ={}&(i^2-1)(x_i^2-x_{i-1}x_{i+1})+x_i^2\\
 &+(i+1)(x_ix_{i-1}-x_{i-2}x_{i+1})
   +(x_{i-1}^2-x_{i-2}x_i).
\end{align*}
For $i\ge1$, all terms are nonnegative. The middle product inequality follows
from monotonicity of adjacent ratios on the positive support; zero boundary
cases are immediate. Starting with the row $S(1,1)=1$, the recurrence
$S(n+1,i)=iS(n,i)+S(n,i-1)$ proves the assertion by induction.
\end{proof}

With this, manuscript~01 derives \eqref{evo:eq:stirlingtp} as follows. If
$j>r$, the right side is zero; if $i>r$, both sides are zero. Otherwise all
relevant entries up to row $s$ are positive. For $1\le t\le m$,
\[
 \frac{S(m+1,t)}{S(m,t)}=t+\frac{S(m,t-1)}{S(m,t)}.
\]
By \cref{evo:et:lem:logconcavity}, the second term is nondecreasing in $t$,
while the first is strictly increasing. Multiplying these row-to-row ratios
from $m=r$ to $s-1$ shows that $S(s,t)/S(r,t)$ is nondecreasing in $t$, which
is \eqref{evo:eq:stirlingtp}. When $r=s$, the assertion is equality.

\subsection{Moving a vertex away from balance}

\begin{theorem}[Strict bipartite balancing; manuscript~06]\label{evo:thm:balance}
For integers $k\ge3$ and $2\le a\le b$,
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:balance}
 \Bip_k(a-1,b+1)>\Bip_k(a,b).
\end{equation}
Thus, among positive side sizes with a fixed sum, $\Bip_k$ is strictly
increasing with the distance from balance.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Start with $K_{r,s}$, where $r=a-1$ and $s=b$, and designate one new vertex.
Attaching it to the side of size $s$ produces $K_{a-1,b+1}$; attaching it to
the side of size $r$ produces $K_{a,b}$.

Suppose a proper coloring of the old graph uses $i$ colors on its first side
and $j$ on its second. These color sets are disjoint. The numbers of choices
for the new vertex are respectively $k-i$ and $k-j$, so the difference is
$j-i$. The number of old colorings with these color-class counts is
$\fall{k}{i+j}S(r,i)S(s,j)$. Pairing the contribution $(i,j)$ with $(j,i)$
gives
\begin{align}
&\Bip_k(a-1,b+1)-\Bip_k(a,b)\notag\\
&=\sum_{\substack{1\le i<j\\i+j\le k}}
 (j-i)\fall{k}{i+j}
 \bigl(S(r,i)S(s,j)-S(r,j)S(s,i)\bigr).
 \label{evo:eq:balancedifference}
\end{align}
Every term is nonnegative by \cref{evo:lem:stirlingtp}. The term $i=1$,
$j=2$ is positive: $s>r\ge1$, $S(t,1)=1$, and $S(t,2)=2^{t-1}-1$. Its
coefficient is nonzero because $k\ge3$.
\end{proof}

Retaining the pair $(1,2)$ quantitatively gives manuscript~01's form; its
proof is the same extension count, read from the other side.

\begin{theorem}[Quantitative strict balancing; manuscript~01]\label{evo:et:thm:balance}
For $k\ge3$ and positive integers $a,b$ with $b\ge a+2$,
\begin{equation}\label{evo:et:eq:balance}
 \Bip_k(a,b)-\Bip_k(a+1,b-1)
 \ge k(k-1)(k-2)\bigl(2^{b-2}-2^{a-1}\bigr)>0.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
First compare extensions of a proper coloring of $K_{a,c}$ to $K_{a+1,c}$ and
$K_{a,c+1}$. If its left and right palettes have sizes $i,j$, the respective
numbers of extensions are $k-j$ and $k-i$. The number of such colorings is
$\fall{k}{i+j}S(a,i)S(c,j)$. Consequently
\[
 \Bip_k(a+1,c)-\Bip_k(a,c+1)
 =\sum_{i+j\le k}(i-j)\fall{k}{i+j}S(a,i)S(c,j).
\]
Pairing the summands with indices $(i,j)$ and $(j,i)$, for $i<j$, gives
\[
 (i-j)\fall{k}{i+j}\bigl(S(a,i)S(c,j)-S(a,j)S(c,i)\bigr).
\]
When $c\ge a$, every such expression is nonpositive by
\cref{evo:lem:stirlingtp}. Take $c=b-1>a$ and retain only the pair
$(i,j)=(1,2)$. Since $S(t,1)=1$ and $S(t,2)=2^{t-1}-1$, reversing the
comparison gives precisely \eqref{evo:et:eq:balance}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Explicit optimization of the lower construction; manuscript~06]\label{evo:cor:split}
For $n\ge7$ and $k\ge4$, among all $a,b$ satisfying $1<a<b$, $a+b=n$ and
$\gcd(a,b)=1$, the quantity
\[
 k^n-\Bip_k(a,b)+ab
\]
is uniquely maximized, up to interchanging sides, by \eqref{evo:eq:split}.
This optimization conclusion holds without any large-$n$ hypothesis.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Moving toward balance strictly decreases $\Bip_k$ and strictly increases
$ab$. The nearest admissible split therefore uniquely maximizes the
expression. For odd $n$, consecutive integers give the nearest split. For
$n=2h$ with $h$ even, the pair $h-1,h+1$ is coprime because its common
divisor divides two and both terms are odd. For $h$ odd, $h-1,h+1$ is not
coprime, whereas $h-2,h+2$ is: the common divisor divides four and both terms
are odd. The equal split is not admissible for $n\ge7$.
\end{proof}

(In this proof, as in Part~II's \cref{tor:lem:optimize}, $h$ is $\lfloor
n/2\rfloor$, not $\lfloor k/2\rfloor$.) Manuscript~06 also uses the nearest
coprime split for component sizes $s\ge2$; the same formula applies, except
that $s=2$ has split $(1,1)$. For this auxiliary use, sides of size one are
allowed.

Manuscript~01 proves the same optimization for every $k\ge3$, with Part~I's
period term $G_k$.

\begin{corollary}[Balancing with the period correction; manuscript~01]\label{evo:et:cor:balanceT}
For fixed $n$ and $k\ge3$, the quantity $\Bip_k(a,n-a)-G_k(a,n-a)$,
$1\le a<n-a$, strictly decreases when $a$ moves toward $n/2$ without crossing
it.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
For $k\ge4$, the product $a(n-a)$ increases under balancing, so subtracting
it strengthens \cref{evo:et:thm:balance}. For $k=3$, the subtracted value
$n-a$ decreases by one, but the right side of \eqref{evo:et:eq:balance} is at
least six. Thus the net change still has the required strict sign.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Closest coprime split; manuscript~01]\label{evo:et:lem:coprime}
For $n\ge7$, $(a_n,b_n)$ is the unique unordered coprime pair closest to
equality with sum $n$. It minimizes $\Bip_k(a,b)-G_k(a,b)$ among coprime
positive pairs $a<b$, for every $k\ge3$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For odd $n$, the two consecutive integers $(n-1)/2,(n+1)/2$ are coprime. For
$n=4t$, the equally sized pair is not coprime, while $2t-1,2t+1$ has greatest
common divisor dividing two and consists of odd numbers. For $n=4t+2$, the
equal pair is not coprime; the next pair $2t,2t+2$ is even; and $2t-1,2t+3$
has greatest common divisor dividing four and consists of odd numbers. This
gives the listed minimal differences. The minimization follows from
\cref{evo:et:cor:balanceT}, even though some intervening pairs are not
coprime.
\end{proof}

Manuscript~03 gives the same admissibility argument for the nearest split,
without a number-theoretic search. At $k=3$ this is Part~II's
\cref{tor:lem:optimize}, which proves it with the
explicit decrement $3(2^{b-1}-2^a)-1$.

\section{Palette expansions and uniform estimates}\label{evo:sec:palettes}

\subsection{Exact counting formulas}

\begin{proposition}[Manuscripts~06 and~03]\label{evo:prop:palette}
For $a,b\ge1$,
\begin{align}
\Bip_k(a,b)
 &=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\fall{k}{i}S(a,i)(k-i)^b,
 \label{evo:eq:positivepalette}\\
\Bip_k(a,b)
 &=\sum_{\substack{i,j\ge1\\i+j\le k}}
 (-1)^{k-i-j}\frac{k!}{i!\,j!\,(k-i-j)!}\,i^aj^b.
 \label{evo:eq:signedpalette}
\end{align}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For \eqref{evo:eq:positivepalette}, partition the first side into its $i$
nonempty color classes, assign $i$ distinct colors to them, and color the
second side with any of the other $k-i$ colors. (This is Part~I's
\eqref{eq:bipoly}; manuscript~01 writes the same sum up to $i\le
\min(a,k-1)$, the term $i=k$ being zero.)

For \eqref{evo:eq:signedpalette}, split the $k$ available colors into three
labeled sets $I,J,U$. Allow colors in $I$ on the first side and colors in $J$
on the second, and assign weight $(-1)^{|U|}$. A coloring using a color on
both sides is never counted. For a proper coloring, each actually used color
is forced into its correct palette, whereas an unused color may be put into
$I,J,U$ with weights $1,1,-1$. Its total contribution is therefore $1+1-1=1$.
Summing over palette sizes gives the formula. Terms with an empty $I$ or $J$
contribute zero because both sides are nonempty.
\end{proof}

\noindent\emph{Second proof of \eqref{evo:eq:signedpalette} (manuscript~03).}
A single color contributes $e^x+e^y-1$ to the exponential generating
function: its vertices may occur on the first side, the second side, or
neither, but not both. Expanding $(e^x+e^y-1)^k$ and taking the coefficient
of $x^ay^b/(a!\,b!)$ proves \eqref{evo:eq:signedpalette}. The zero exponents
disappear because $a,b>0$.

Manuscript~03 records two further exact forms. With
$\sur_j(m)=j!\,S(m,j)=\sum_{q=0}^j(-1)^q\binom jq(j-q)^m$ the number of onto
maps from an $m$-set to a prescribed $j$-set ($m,j\ge1$), counting the colors
used on the first part gives
\begin{equation}\label{evo:da:eq:palette1}
 \Bip_k(a,b)=\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\binom ki\sur_i(a)(k-i)^b,
\end{equation}
which is \eqref{evo:eq:positivepalette} again, and counting the exact palettes
on both parts gives the nonnegative expansion
\begin{equation}\label{evo:da:eq:palette2}
 \Bip_k(a,b)=
 \sum_{\substack{i,j\ge1\\i+j\le k}}
 \frac{k!}{i!\,j!\,(k-i-j)!}\sur_i(a)\sur_j(b).
\end{equation}
Manuscript~01's \cref{evo:et:lem:IE} below is \eqref{evo:da:eq:palette1} with
$\sur_i$ expanded by inclusion--exclusion. The signed formula is a finite
exponential sum; in particular, its subtractions cannot be discarded in exact
computations: counting disjoint \emph{allowed} palettes without
inclusion--exclusion overcounts colorings that do not use every allowed color
(manuscript~06). The positive formula \eqref{evo:da:eq:palette2} is best for
lower bounds, the signed formula \eqref{evo:eq:signedpalette} makes the
bounded-imbalance asymptotics transparent, and using them for their
respective purposes avoids a potentially invalid lower bound obtained by
discarding negative terms (manuscript~03).

\subsection{Constants for the effective theorem}

Fix an integer $k\ge4$ for the remainder of this subsection and the next
section, and define
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:constants}
\begin{gathered}
 \hat H=\lfloor k^2/4\rfloor,\qquad \lambda_k=\sqrt{\hat H},\\
 \hat M=\begin{cases}\hat H-1,&k\text{ even},\\ \hat H-2,&k\text{ odd},\end{cases}
 \qquad \mu=\sqrt{\hat M},\qquad \hat\theta=\mu/\lambda_k<1,\\
 \hat C=\begin{cases}\binom{k}{k/2},&k\text{ even},\\
                 2\binom{k}{(k-1)/2},&k\text{ odd},\end{cases}
 \qquad \hat B=k^23^k.
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
We always have $\hat C\ge6$, $\lambda_k\ge2$, and $k/\lambda_k\ge2$.

\begin{lemma}[Uniform near-balanced estimate; manuscript~06]\label{evo:lem:nearbalanced}
Let $a+b=s$, $a,b\ge1$, and write $\delta=(b-a)/2$. If $|\delta|\le2$, then
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:paletteerror}
\bigl|\Bip_k(a,b)-\mathcal L_k(s,\delta)\bigr|\le\hat B\mu^s,
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:leadingpalette}
\mathcal L_k(s,\delta)=
\begin{cases}
 \hat C\lambda_k^s,&k\text{ even},\\
 \hat C\lambda_k^s\cosh(\delta\,\omega_h),&k=2h+1.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A term in \eqref{evo:eq:signedpalette} has exponential factor
$i^aj^b=(ij)^{s/2}(j/i)^\delta$. Among positive $i,j$ with $i+j\le k$, the
maximum of $ij$ is $\hat H$. For even $k=2h$, it is attained only at $(h,h)$;
its coefficient is $\hat C$. For odd $k=2h+1$, it is attained at $(h,h+1)$ and
$(h+1,h)$, each with coefficient $\hat C/2$. These terms give
\eqref{evo:eq:leadingpalette}.

Every remaining product $ij$ is at most $\hat M$. For even $k$, the next
possible product is at most $h^2-1$. For odd $k=2h+1$ with $h\ge2$, either
$i+j=k$ and the next product is at most $(h-1)(h+2)=\hat H-2$, or
$i+j\le k-1$ and the product is at most $h^2\le\hat H-2$. Also
$|(j/i)^\delta|\le k^2$ in the stated range. The sum of all absolute
multinomial coefficients is at most $3^k$, by the multinomial theorem. This
proves \eqref{evo:eq:paletteerror}.
\end{proof}

Let $\hat S=\hat S(k)$ be the least integer $\hat S\ge4$ satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:Sdefinition}
 64\hat B^2\hat M^{\hat S}\le\hat C^2\hat H^{\hat S}.
\end{equation}
Equivalently, $\hat B\hat\theta^{\hat S}\le\hat C/8$. The definition is
effective using only integer multiplication and comparison, and such an
$\hat S$ exists because $\hat M<\hat H$.

\begin{corollary}[Manuscript~06]\label{evo:cor:uniformpalette}
For $s\ge\hat S$,
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:balancedlower}
 \Bip_k(\lfloor s/2\rfloor,\lceil s/2\rceil)\ge\frac78\hat C\lambda_k^s.
\end{equation}
For $n\ge\max(7,\hat S)$,
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:nearupper}
 \Bip_k(a_n,b_n)<\frac32\hat C\lambda_k^n.
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
In the balanced split, $|\delta|\le1/2$, and the leading expression in
\eqref{evo:eq:leadingpalette} is at least $\hat C\lambda_k^s$. Subtract the
error $\hat C\lambda_k^s/8$.

For the nearest coprime split, $|\delta|\le2$. In the even-output case, the
upper coefficient is at most $1+1/8=9/8$. In the odd-output case, $h\ge2$ and
\[
 \cosh(2\omega_h)\le\cosh(2\log(3/2))=\frac{97}{72}.
\]
Adding the error coefficient gives $97/72+1/8=53/36<3/2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[A bound with no size restriction; manuscripts~06, 01 and~03]\label{evo:lem:twopalettes}
For every $k\ge3$ and all $u,v\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:twopalettes}
 \Bip_k(u,v)\ge2\lambda_k^{u+v}.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Fix a partition of the colors into sets of sizes $h=\lfloor k/2\rfloor$ and
$k-h$. Color the two sides from these palettes, and also count the colorings
with the palettes exchanged. The two families are disjoint, since both sides
are nonempty and the palettes are disjoint. Their total is
$h^u(k-h)^v+(k-h)^uh^v\ge2[h(k-h)]^{(u+v)/2}$.
\end{proof}

All three manuscripts prove \cref{evo:lem:twopalettes} by this argument
(06: Lemma~4.5, 01: Lemma~5.1, 03: first part of Lemma~7.1); manuscript~06
states it for $k\ge4$, manuscripts~01 and~03 for $k\ge3$.

\subsection{Sharpened palette bounds for fixed $k$ (manuscripts~01 and~03)}

Manuscripts~01 and~03 need asymptotic lower bounds valid for unbounded
imbalance. For fixed $j$, the union bound on a missing color gives
\begin{equation}\label{evo:et:eq:ontoestimate}
 j^m-j(j-1)^m\le\sur_j(m)\le j^m;
\end{equation}
for $j=1$ and $m\ge1$ this reads $\sur_1(m)=1$.

\begin{lemma}[Sharpened lower bounds; manuscript~01, also manuscript~03]\label{evo:et:lem:paletteLB}
Let $u,v\to\infty$, with $k\ge3$ fixed and $h=\lfloor k/2\rfloor$. If $k=2h$,
\begin{equation}\label{evo:et:eq:evenLB}
 \Bip_k(u,v)\ge(1-o(1))\binom kh\lambda_k^{u+v}.
\end{equation}
If $k=2h+1$,
\begin{equation}\label{evo:et:eq:oddLB}
 \Bip_k(u,v)\ge(1-o(1))\,2\binom kh\lambda_k^{u+v}
 \cosh\!\left(\frac{(v-u)\,\omega_h}2\right).
\end{equation}
The $o(1)$ tends to zero whenever $\min(u,v)\to\infty$, with no assumption on
the ratio $u/v$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
In the even case, count colorings using exactly a chosen $h$-color palette on
the left and exactly its complement on the right. These choices are disjoint
as the chosen palette varies, so they give $\binom kh\sur_h(u)\sur_h(v)$.
Apply \eqref{evo:et:eq:ontoestimate}.

In the odd case, use every color and the two possible palette-size pairs
$(h,h+1)$ and $(h+1,h)$. Their combined count is
$\binom kh\bigl(\sur_h(u)\sur_{h+1}(v)+\sur_{h+1}(u)\sur_h(v)\bigr)$. Each
factor has relative error tending to zero with the smaller side. Factoring
out $\lambda_k^{u+v}$ gives \eqref{evo:et:eq:oddLB}.
\end{proof}

Manuscript~03 proves the same bounds (its Lemma~7.1, in the form
$\Bip_{2h+1}(u,v)\ge(\binom kh-o(1))\lambda_k^{u+v}(t_h^{(v-u)/2}+t_h^{-(v-u)/2})$,
where $t_h^{z}+t_h^{-z}=2\cosh(z\omega_h)$) by retaining in
\eqref{evo:da:eq:palette2} the term $(h,h)$, respectively $(h,h+1)$ and
$(h+1,h)$; since $\sur_j(m)/j^m\to1$ uniformly over the finite set $1\le j\le
k$, the relative errors depend only on $\min(u,v)$, which proves the
uniformity.

\begin{lemma}[Near-balanced asymptotics; manuscript~01]\label{evo:et:lem:witnessasym}
For $a+b=n\to\infty$ and bounded $|a-b|$, the lower bounds
\eqref{evo:et:eq:evenLB} and \eqref{evo:et:eq:oddLB} are asymptotic
equalities. Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{evo:et:eq:witnessasym}
 \frac{D^*_k(n)}{\lambda_k^n}=
 \begin{cases}
 \binom kh+o(1),&k\text{ even},\\
 2\binom kh\cosh(\delta_n\omega_h)+o(1),&k\text{ odd}.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Every proper coloring admits a partition of the full $k$-color alphabet into a
nonempty left palette and a nonempty right palette. It may admit several if
some colors are unused, but this gives the upper bound
\begin{equation}\label{evo:et:eq:paletteUB}
 \Bip_k(a,b)\le\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\binom ki i^a(k-i)^b.
\end{equation}
For bounded imbalance, each summand has exponential base $\sqrt{i(k-i)}$ in
$n$. The unique largest product is $h^2$ for even $k$; for odd $k$, the two
largest products are $h(h+1)$, from $i=h,h+1$. All remaining products are
strictly smaller. The sharpened lower bounds match these largest summands.
Finally, $G_k(a_n,b_n)\le n^2$ is negligible relative to $\lambda_k^n$.
\end{proof}

(In manuscript~01's own normalization, $\delta_n\in\{1,2,4\}$ and the cosine
argument is $\lambda\delta_n/2$ with $\lambda=\log((m+1)/m)$; this is
$\delta_n\omega_h$ in ours.)

\begin{lemma}[Bounded-imbalance asymptotics; manuscript~03]\label{evo:da:lem:balancedasymp}
If $a+b=n$ and $|a-b|$ is bounded, then for some $\rho_k<\lambda_k$,
\begin{align}
\Bip_{2h}(a,b)&=\binom{2h}{h}h^n+O_k(\rho_k^n),\label{evo:da:eq:evenwitnessasymp}\\
\Bip_{2h+1}(a,b)&=\binom{2h+1}{h}\lambda_k^n
 \bigl(t_h^{(b-a)/2}+t_h^{-(b-a)/2}\bigr)+O_k(\rho_k^n).
\label{evo:da:eq:oddwitnessasymp}
\end{align}
The implied constants may depend on the fixed bound on $|a-b|$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
In \eqref{evo:eq:signedpalette}, the largest possible product $ij$ under
$i+j\le k$ is $\lfloor k^2/4\rfloor$. The maximizing pair is $(h,h)$ for even
$k$, and the two pairs $(h,h+1)$, $(h+1,h)$ for odd $k$. All their
coefficients are positive and have magnitude $\binom kh$. Every other product
is strictly smaller. Bounded imbalance means
$i^aj^b=(ij)^{n/2}(j/i)^{(b-a)/2}$, with a bounded final factor. There are
only finitely many terms, so the rest have a strictly smaller exponential
base. For $k=3$ the rest reduce to the constant $-6$.
\end{proof}

By \cref{evo:et:prop:witness} (or Davies's Corollary~3) and
\cref{evo:da:lem:balancedasymp}, the leading terms of
\eqref{evo:da:eq:evenwitnessasymp}--\eqref{evo:da:eq:oddwitnessasymp} are
already upper bounds for the optimal deficit to first order. The remaining
work, in manuscript~03's words, is an upper bound on reversal complexity,
equivalently a matching lower bound on the deficit, uniform over all pairs of
transformations. \Cref{evo:lem:nearbalanced} is the effective version of
\cref{evo:et:lem:witnessasym,evo:da:lem:balancedasymp} for $|\delta|\le2$.

\section{The effective eventual theorem}\label{evo:sec:eventual}

This section and the next are manuscript~06's. Retain \eqref{evo:eq:constants}
and \eqref{evo:eq:Sdefinition}, with $k\ge4$ fixed.

\subsection{The complete integer definition of the threshold}

Define
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:Ndef}
\begin{split}
 \hat L&=\min\{\ell\ge0:2^\ell\ge2\hat C\},\\
 \hat K&=k(k-1)(k-2),\qquad \beta_k=\hat K^{1/3},\\
 \hat U&=\min\{u\ge3:\hat K^{2u}\ge(2\hat C)^6\hat H^{3u}\},\\
 N(k)&=\max\{k+1,\,7,\,\hat L(\hat S+1),\,\hat U\}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
The condition defining $\hat U$ is equivalent to
$(\beta_k/\lambda_k)^{\hat U}\ge2\hat C$. All the threshold-defining tests are
exact integer inequalities. In particular, rounding a numerical logarithm is
not part of the theorem's specification. The shipped
\path{data/05-quartic-threshold-thresholds.json} lists the constants for
$4\le k\le16$ under the keys \texttt{H}, \texttt{M}, \texttt{C}, \texttt{B},
\texttt{L}, \texttt{S}, \texttt{U}, \texttt{N}; in the write phase they were
recomputed from \eqref{evo:eq:constants}, \eqref{evo:eq:Sdefinition} and
\eqref{evo:eq:Ndef} and agree in all $13$ rows.

\subsection{Separating incomplete or disconnected cross-cycle graphs}

Call a cross-cycle graph \emph{full} if it is a single $K_{a,b}$ on all $n$
vertices, with $\gcd(a,b)=1$; in the notation of \eqref{eq:diffcount},
fullness means $d=1$, $r=0$.

\begin{lemma}\label{evo:lem:badcross}
If $n\ge\hat L(\hat S+1)$ and a cross-cycle graph is not full, then
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:badcross}
 P_G(k)\ge\frac74\hat C\lambda_k^n.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Write $n=d(u+v)+r$, $d\ge1$, $r\ge0$. First suppose $d+r\ge\hat L$.
\Cref{evo:lem:twopalettes} gives
\[
 \frac{P_G(k)}{\lambda_k^n}
 =\left(\frac{\Bip_k(u,v)}{\lambda_k^{u+v}}\right)^d
     \left(\frac{k}{\lambda_k}\right)^r
 \ge2^{d+r}\ge2\hat C.
\]
This is stronger than required.

Now suppose $d+r<\hat L$, and put $s=u+v$. Since $r<\hat L$ and $d<\hat L$,
$n\ge\hat L(\hat S+1)$ implies $s=(n-r)/d\ge\hat S$. Strict balancing, without
any coprimality requirement for the intermediate balanced split, and
\eqref{evo:eq:balancedlower} imply
\[
 \Bip_k(u,v)\ge\Bip_k(\lfloor s/2\rfloor,\lceil s/2\rceil)
          \ge\frac78\hat C\lambda_k^s.
\]
If $d=1$, nonfullness forces $r\ge1$, and the normalized coloring count is at
least $(7\hat C/8)\cdot2=7\hat C/4$. If $d\ge2$, it is at least
$(7\hat C/8)^2\ge7\hat C/4$, because $\hat C\ge6$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[A quantitative nonfull-graph loss]\label{evo:cor:nonfullgap}
For $n\ge N(k)$, any mixed pair admitting a nonfull cross-cycle collision
graph has orbit size strictly less than $k^n-D^*_k(n)$, by at least
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:nonfullpenalty}
 \left(\frac{\hat C}4-1\right)\lambda_k^n
\end{equation}
as a real-valued lower bound on the difference.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
By the collision bound and \cref{evo:lem:order}, its deficit from $k^n$ is at
least $P_G(k)-3^{n/3}$. Since $3^{n/3}<\lambda_k^n$ and
$\Bip_k(a_n,b_n)<(3\hat C/2)\lambda_k^n$, subtracting the optimal candidate
deficit $D^*_k(n)=\Bip_k(a_n,b_n)-a_nb_n$ gives a difference strictly larger
than the right side of \eqref{evo:eq:nonfullpenalty}. It is positive because
$\hat C\ge6$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Separating the remaining graph types}

\begin{lemma}[Manuscript~06]\label{evo:lem:betabounds}
For $k\ge4$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item every same-cycle collision graph has at least $\beta_k^n$ proper
colorings;
\item $(k-1)^2k^{n-2}\ge\beta_k^n$ for $n\ge3$;
\item $k^n-k^{n-1}\ge\beta_k^n$ for $n\ge1$;
\item $\beta_k>\lambda_k$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Set $q=k-1$. Then $\beta_k^3=q^3-q$, so $\beta_k<q<k$. For an even component
length $\ell$, its coloring count $q^\ell+q$ exceeds $\beta_k^\ell$. For odd
$\ell\ge3$,
\[
 q^\ell-q=q^\ell(1-q^{1-\ell})\ge q^\ell(1-q^{-2})^{\ell/3}=\beta_k^\ell.
\]
Indeed $1-q^{1-\ell}\ge1-q^{-2}$ and raising a number in $(0,1)$ to a power at
least one can only decrease it. Multiplication over components and the factor
$k^r\ge\beta_k^r$ from isolates prove (i).

At $n=3$, (ii) is $k(k-1)^2\ge k(k-1)(k-2)$; increasing $n$ multiplies the
left side by $k$ and the right side by $\beta_k<k$. For (iii),
$(k-1)k^{n-1}>\beta_k^n$ follows from $k-1>\beta_k$ and $k>\beta_k$. Finally,
using $\hat H\le k^2/4$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:betaratio}
 \frac{\beta_k^3}{\lambda_k^3}
 =\frac{k(k-1)(k-2)}{\hat H^{3/2}}
 \ge8\left(1-\frac1k\right)\left(1-\frac2k\right)\ge3.
\end{equation}
\end{proof}

For $n\ge\hat U$, the definition of $\hat U$ gives
$\beta_k^n\ge2\hat C\lambda_k^n$. The possible correction $\ord(p)$ in the
same-cycle case is less than $\lambda_k^n$. The possible correction one in
the two-singular case is at most $\lambda_k^n$ as well. Thus every deficit in
these cases exceeds $(3\hat C/2)\lambda_k^n$, because $\hat C\ge6$. The same
conclusion holds, without a correction, in the two-permutation case. Each is
strictly larger than the candidate $D^*_k(n)$.

\noindent\emph{Second routes to (i) and (iv).} Manuscript~01 proves (i) and
(iv) for every $k\ge3$ (its Lemma~6.3, with $\beta_k$ called $\gamma_k$): for
odd $L\ge3$, $C_L(k)^{1/L}=(k-1)(1-(k-1)^{1-L})^{1/L}\ge(k-1)(1-(k-1)^{-2})^{1/3}
=\beta_k$; for even $L\ge4$, $C_L(k)^{1/L}\ge k-1>\beta_k$; for $L=2$,
$[k(k-1)]^{1/2}\ge\beta_k$; isolated vertices contribute $k\ge\beta_k$; and
$\beta_k>k/2\ge\lambda_k$, since after cubing the strict inequality is
$8(k-1)(k-2)>k^2$, valid for all $k\ge3$. Manuscript~03 proves
$s^L+(-1)^Ls\ge(s^3-s)^{L/3}=\beta_k^L$ for $s=k-1\ge2$, $L\ge2$, by the
argument of its three-output case (\eqref{evo:da:eq:cyclebeta}), and
$\beta_k>\lambda_k$ by checking $(1-1/k)(1-2/k)>1/8$, true at $k=3$ and
increasing thereafter, because $\lambda_k\le k/2$. At $k=3$,
$\beta_3=6^{1/3}$, and (i) is Part~II's \eqref{tor:eq:Clower}.

\subsection{Completion of the exactness proof}

\begin{theorem}[Eventual exactness with explicit threshold; manuscript~06]\label{evo:thm:eventual}
For $k\ge4$ and $n\ge N(k)$ as defined in \eqref{evo:eq:Ndef},
\eqref{evo:eq:main} holds.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Consider any binary transformation pair on $n$ points. The two-permutation
and two-singular cases are strictly below the candidate optimum by
\cref{evo:lem:easy} and the preceding estimates. In the mixed case, select any
collision pair of the singular map. A same-cycle graph is strictly excluded by
\cref{evo:lem:betabounds}; a nonfull cross-cycle graph is strictly excluded by
\cref{evo:cor:nonfullgap}.

The only remaining possibility is that the permutation has exactly two
cycles, of coprime lengths $a,b$ summing to $n$, and the collision graph is
$K_{a,b}$. Now $\ord(p)=ab$, so
\[
 |\tau\langle p,s\rangle|\le k^n-\Bip_k(a,b)+ab
                         \le k^n-\Bip_k(a_n,b_n)+a_nb_n,
\]
where the last inequality follows from strict balancing. This argument also
covers a cycle of length one: such a split is farther from balance than
\eqref{evo:eq:split}. It proves the universal upper bound.

For attainability, use the published lower-bound theorem
\cite[Theorem~3 and Corollary~3]{Davies2017}: for $4\le k<n$ and a coprime
split $1<a<b$, there is an accessible binary DFAO with reverse complexity
$k^n-\Bip_k(a,b)+ab$. Apply it to $(a_n,b_n)$.

We must also check the original-state minimality in the definition of
$R_2(n,k)$, since accessibility alone is not minimality. Because
$n\ge\hat L$ and $k/\lambda_k\ge2$, we have
$\hat C(\lambda_k/k)^n\le\hat C2^{-n}\le1/2$. Equation \eqref{evo:eq:nearupper}
gives $\Bip_k(a_n,b_n)<3k^n/4$, so the attained reverse complexity is greater
than $k^n/4\ge k^{n-1}$. If the original automaton minimized to fewer than $n$
states, the elementary coloring construction would bound its reverse
complexity by $k^{n-1}$, a contradiction. The witness therefore has original
state complexity exactly $n$ (\cref{evo:da:lem:minimal}).
\end{proof}

\subsection{A uniform quartic bound}

\begin{corollary}[Manuscript~06]\label{evo:cor:quartic}
For every $k\ge4$, $N(k)\le3k^4$. Consequently \eqref{evo:eq:main} holds
whenever $k\ge4$ and $n\ge3k^4$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
First, $\hat C\le2^{k+1}$, hence $\hat L\le k+2$. Since $\hat M\le\hat H-1$,
\[
 \log(1/\hat\theta)=\tfrac12\log(\hat H/\hat M)\ge\frac1{2\hat H}.
\]
For $k\ge4$ we have
\[
 \log(8\hat B)=\log8+2\log k+k\log3\le3k.
\]
For example, the three summands are bounded respectively by $3k/4$, $k$, and
$5k/4$; the middle inequality follows from $2\log k\le k$ for $k\ge4$. Thus
$S_0=6\hat Hk$ satisfies $\hat B\hat\theta^{S_0}\le1/8\le\hat C/8$, and
\[
 \hat S\le6\hat Hk\le\tfrac32k^3.
\]
Therefore
\[
 \hat L(\hat S+1)\le(k+2)(\tfrac32k^3+1)\le3k^4,
\]
using $k+2\le3k/2$ and $\tfrac32k^3+1\le2k^3$.

By \eqref{evo:eq:betaratio}, $\beta_k/\lambda_k\ge3^{1/3}$. At $u=2(k+2)$,
\[
 (\beta_k/\lambda_k)^u\ge3^{2(k+2)/3}>2^{k+2}\ge2\hat C,
\]
so $\hat U\le2(k+2)$. The remaining entries $k+1,7$ in the maximum defining
$N(k)$ are plainly at most $3k^4$.
\end{proof}

The constants were chosen to make a fully explicit comparison easy to audit,
not to optimize the threshold. In particular, the argument deliberately uses
an elementary exponential order bound instead of a sharper bound on
permutation orders.

\begin{remark}[Part~I's structural bound in the same range; added in the merge]\label{evo:rem:UL}
Part~I asks in \cref{sec:conclusion} to prove or refute $U(n,k)=L(n,k)$ for
$n\ge7$, $3\le k<n$; Part~II's \cref{tor:rem:UL} proves it for $k=3$. It also
holds on manuscript~06's exactness range: for $k\ge4$ and either $n\ge N(k)$
or $4\le k\le7$, $\max(7,k+1)\le n$ (with $k<n$),
\[
 D(n,k)=D^*_k(n),\qquad U(n,k)=L(n,k)=R_2(n,k)=k^n-D^*_k(n).
\]
Indeed $L(n,k)=k^n-D^*_k(n)$ by \cref{evo:cor:split}, and Part~I's collection
contains the entry \eqref{eq:Dbip} at $(a,b)=(a_n,b_n)$, $d=1$, $r=0$, which
is $\Bip_k(a_n,b_n)-H_0(a_nb_n)=D^*_k(n)$; so $D(n,k)\le D^*_k(n)$. Conversely
every entry is at least a quantity that manuscript~06 bounds below by
$D^*_k(n)$. Entry \eqref{eq:Dsing} is \cref{evo:lem:easy}(ii); entry
\eqref{eq:Dperm} is at least $k^n-k^{n-1}$, because $M(n,k)\le k^{n-1}$ by
\cref{evo:lem:easy}(i); in \eqref{eq:Dcycle},
$H_{n-m}(m)\le m\gamma_{n-m}\le m\,3^{(n-m)/3}$; in \eqref{eq:Dbip}, where
$u=a/d$, $v=b/d$ are coprime with sum $s$, $T_{r,k}(a,b)=H_r(\lcm(a,b))\le
duv\gamma_r$, $\Bip_k(u,v)\ge\Bip_k(u_s,v_s)$ and $uv\le u_sv_s$ for the
nearest coprime split $(u_s,v_s)$ of $s$ (\cref{evo:thm:balance}), so the entry
is at least manuscript~06's compressed quantity \eqref{evo:eq:finitecross}.
For $n\ge N(k)$ the proofs of
\cref{evo:lem:badcross,evo:cor:nonfullgap,evo:lem:betabounds} bound these
quantities from below by numbers exceeding $D^*_k(n)$, except the entries with
$d=1$, $r=0$, which strict balancing handles; below $N(k)$, for $4\le k\le7$,
the finite verification of \cref{evo:sec:finite} checks exactly the
compressed quantities. The one entry manuscript~06 does not treat is the
nonsurjective \eqref{eq:Dnon}: for $n\ge N(k)$,
$k^n-(k-1)^n\ge n(k-1)^{n-1}\ge(k-1)^n>\beta_k^n\ge2\hat C\lambda_k^n>D^*_k(n)$
(using $n\ge k+1$ and $n\ge\hat U$), and for $4\le k\le7$ below $N(k)$ the
inequality $k^n-(k-1)^n>D^*_k(n)$ was checked in the write phase for all
$2{,}217$ rows by exact integer arithmetic. So $D(n,k)\ge D^*_k(n)$. This
remark is the merge's reading of manuscript~06's comparisons against Part~I's
definitions; the manuscripts do not state it, and for $n\le30$ it agrees with
Part~I's \path{data/finite_range_bounds.csv} (\cref{evo:sec:rowcheck}).
\end{remark}

\section{Finite exact closure for four through seven outputs}\label{evo:sec:finite}

\subsection{What must actually be checked}

The analytic theorem leaves finitely many $n$ for each fixed $k$. It is
unnecessary to enumerate all pairs of transformations. The graph
classification and strict balancing reduce the upper-bound verification to
integer inequalities indexed by graph parameters.

For any component total $s\ge2$, let $(u_s,v_s)$ be its nearest positive
coprime split, allowing $(1,1)$ for $s=2$, and write
$\Bip_k(u_s,v_s)$ for its coloring count (manuscript~06's $C_{k,s}$). Let
$\gamma_r=\lfloor3^{r/3}\rfloor$. For each $n$, set $D=D^*_k(n)$. The
following list is exhaustive:
\begin{align}
 k^n-k^{n-1}&\ge D,
 \label{evo:eq:finiteperm}\\
 (k-1)^2k^{n-2}-1&\ge D,
 \label{evo:eq:finitesing}\\
 \Bip_k(u_s,v_s)^{d}k^r-du_sv_s\gamma_r&\ge D,
 \quad s\ge2,\ d\ge1,\ r=n-ds\ge0,
 \label{evo:eq:finitecross}\\
 \bigl((k-1)^\ell+(-1)^\ell(k-1)\bigr)^{m/\ell}k^{n-m}-m\gamma_{n-m}&\ge D,
 \quad 2\le m\le n,\ \ell\mid m,\ \ell\ge2.
 \label{evo:eq:finitesame}
\end{align}

To justify the compression in \eqref{evo:eq:finitecross}, fix $s,d,r$ and
vary over all coprime pairs $u+v=s$. Strict balancing minimizes
$\Bip_k(u,v)$ at $(u_s,v_s)$, while $uv$ is maximized there. Thus
$\Bip_k(u,v)^dk^r-duv\gamma_r$ is minimized at that same split. No pair is
lost. Equation \eqref{evo:eq:finitesame} includes every possible displacement
class inside a cycle. The factor $\gamma_r$ is a valid bound because a
residual permutation order is an integer and at most $3^{r/3}$.

For $s=n$, $d=1$, $r=0$, \eqref{evo:eq:finitecross} is the distinguished
identity $\Bip_k(a_n,b_n)-a_nb_n=D$. All other comparisons in the verified
ranges are \emph{strict}. This extra fact extends the structural rigidity
theorem (\cref{evo:thm:rigidity}) as well as the maximum-value theorem.

\subsection{Two implementations with different arithmetic routes}

The primary program, \path{code/05-quartic-threshold-theorem_checks.py},
computes $\Bip_k$ by the signed palette formula, uses the explicit residue
formula for the split, and computes $\gamma_r$ by integer binary search. The
independent program, \path{code/05-quartic-threshold-independent_check.py},
does not import the primary program or read its computed tables. It instead
uses the positive Stirling formula, finds the split by a descending gcd scan,
and enumerates active sizes and divisors rather than component sizes and
multiplicities.

The independent program also uses a different residual-order comparison.
Given a coloring count $X$, target deficit $D$, and active period factor $t$,
it first checks
\[
 X-D>t\,3^{\lceil r/3\rceil}.
\]
If needed, it can check the sharper real-order inequality by cubing:
$(X-D)^3>t^33^r$ with $X-D>0$. In the reported runs, the coarser test already
succeeded for every excluded mixed case; no cubic fallback was needed. Thus
the second implementation verifies inequalities at least as strong as the
corresponding primary exclusions, rather than merely reproducing rounded
roots.

Both programs raise exceptions on any failed comparison or unexpected
equality. Neither depends on Python's optional assertion statements. The
independent program also checks that the attained candidate exceeds $k^{n-1}$
in every finite row, completing the original-state minimality argument below
the analytic threshold.

\midrule

5 & 6 & 39 & 8 & 240 & $7$--$239$ & 233 & 238,946\\
6 & 6 & 158 & 8 & 954 & $7$--$953$ & 947 & 5,022,662\\
7 & 8 & 104 & 10 & 840 & $8$--$839$ & 832 & 3,804,474\\

\label{evo:tab:closure}
\end{table}

There were zero failed inequalities and zero unexpected equalities. There was
precisely one distinguished equality per row. The primary program's smallest
strict excluded margins, taken over its entire range for each $k$, were
respectively $1265$, $9285$, $41615$, and $327855$. The per-row CSV files
(\path{data/05-quartic-threshold-finite_k4.csv} to \path{..._k7.csv}) retain
the target deficit, strict margin, and closest excluded case, not only an
overall success flag.

\begin{theorem}[Computer-assisted all-$n$ slices; manuscript~06]\label{evo:thm:smallk}
For $4\le k\le7$ and $n\ge\max(7,k+1)$,
\[
 R_2(n,k)=k^n-\Bip_k(a_n,b_n)+a_nb_n.
\]
Moreover, in these ranges every collision-graph case other than a full
nearest-coprime two-cycle graph is strictly suboptimal.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For $n\ge N(k)$ this is \cref{evo:thm:eventual} and its strict exclusions.
Below $N(k)$, the finite ranges in \cref{evo:tab:closure} cover every
remaining integer $n$ under consideration. The two implementations verify
\eqref{evo:eq:finiteperm}--\eqref{evo:eq:finitesame}, with strictness outside
the distinguished case. Their mathematical sufficiency was proved above. The
published lower witness supplies equality, and the separate $k^{n-1}$ test
ensures its original minimality.
\end{proof}

This is a computer-assisted proof with explicit finite obligations. It is not
a proof-assistant certificate, and agreement between two programs does not
make them independent of the common mathematical reduction. The programs do,
however, separate several potential implementation failures: counting
formula, split selection, loop indexing, and order-bound arithmetic.

The supplementary program, \path{code/05-quartic-threshold-supplementary_checks.py},
checks $1{,}183$ signed-versus-positive palette identities, $1{,}999$

enumerates every permutation and collision pair on up to five vertices,
checking $1{,}364$ collision-graph coloring counts by literal three-color
assignments. Twenty-seven small matching-spectrum enumerations test the
rank-sensitive formulas of \cref{evo:sec:rank}. These are regression checks,
not substitutes for the proofs of the identities. The program is not a third
independent implementation of the full proof: it imports the primary
chromatic routine when comparing that formula to an independently written
positive formula.

\section{Finite certificates for \texorpdfstring{$n\le100$, $k\le12$}{n <= 100, k <= 12}}\label{evo:sec:certificates}

This section is manuscript~01's. Its infinite argument
(\cref{evo:sec:separation}) uses a subexponential permutation-order bound and
a subsequence contradiction; its finite computations use a different bound,
weaker asymptotically but especially easy to check in integers. They close a
large initial region directly; they do not replace the infinite proof.

\subsection{A finite universal bound}

For a same-cycle collision, write the support size as $m=dL$, $L\ge2$, and
put $r=n-m$. The permutation order is at most $mJ(r)$, so the deficit is at
least
\begin{equation}\label{evo:et:eq:finitecycle}
 C_L(k)^dk^r-mJ(r).
\end{equation}
For a collision across cycles $a\le b$, write $d=\gcd(a,b)$ and $r=n-a-b$. The
analogous lower bound is
\begin{equation}\label{evo:et:eq:finitebip}
 \Bip_k(a/d,b/d)^dk^r-V_k(a,b,d)J(r),
 \qquad
 V_k(a,b,d)=
 \begin{cases}
 b,&k=3\text{ and }d=1,\\
 \lcm(a,b),&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
To justify the improvement when $k=3$, $d=1$: a proper coloring of $K_{a,b}$
has one monochromatic side, so its period on the two selected cycles is at
most $b$. The period on the remaining vertices divides the residual
permutation order, and the total period is at most the product of those
bounds. When the initial coloring is improper, subtracting this possible
proper period is still a valid, weaker bound. (This is Part~I's
\eqref{eq:periodimprove} with $H_r$ relaxed to a product with $J(r)$, as in
Part~II's \eqref{tor:eq:crossperiod}.)

\begin{proposition}[Finite certificate principle; manuscript~01]\label{evo:et:prop:finite}
Let $D^{\mathrm{cert}}_k(n)$ be the minimum of the three bounds of
\cref{evo:et:rem:unmixed}, all values of \eqref{evo:et:eq:finitecycle} with
$m=dL\le n$ and $L\ge2$, and all values of \eqref{evo:et:eq:finitebip} with
$1\le a\le b$, $a+b\le n$. Then every coloring orbit has defect at least
$D^{\mathrm{cert}}_k(n)$. If $n\ge7$, $3\le k<n$, and
\begin{equation}\label{evo:et:eq:certmin}
 D^{\mathrm{cert}}_k(n)=D^*_k(n),
\end{equation}
then $D_k(n)=D^*_k(n)$ for that particular pair $(n,k)$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The minimum includes a valid bound for every case of the generator
classification and every possible collision-orbit graph. This proves the
universal lower bound. The explicit accessible minimal witness has defect
$D^*_k(n)$ by \cref{evo:et:prop:witness}, so equality of the two integer
bounds proves the second assertion.
\end{proof}

Some terms in the minimum could be negative for other parameter ranges; this
only makes the bound weaker, never invalid. No positivity assumption on the
certificate expressions is used. The principle is Part~I's
\cref{prop:finite} with Part~I's exact residual order sets $H_r$ replaced by
the product bound $J(r)$, and with Part~I's two-permutation entry
\eqref{eq:Dperm} replaced by the weaker $k^{n-1}$.

\subsection{The certified finite region}

Both programs independently obtained
\begin{equation}\label{evo:et:eq:region}
 D^{\mathrm{cert}}_k(n)=D^*_k(n)
 \quad\text{for every }7\le n\le100,
 \qquad3\le k\le\min(12,n-1).
\end{equation}
There are $919$ such pairs. For every row,
\path{data/03-eventual-finite_certificates.csv} records
$n,k,a_n,b_n,D^*_k(n),D^{\mathrm{cert}}_k(n)$ (columns
\texttt{candidate\_defect}, \texttt{universal\_lower\_bound}) and a minimizing
graph type. These are finite exhaustive checks of \emph{graph-parameter
bounds}, not exhaustive enumerations of all $n$-state automata. The latter
would be vastly larger and is unnecessary for this certificate principle.

The result extends Part~I's finite range (\cref{prop:finite}, $n\le30$) in the
state variable, but does not establish a new record for each individual value.
Some of the small values, including the example in \cref{evo:et:ex:n8k5},
were already computed in Part~I or in Davies's experiments. No claim of
priority for the finite table is made. For $4\le k\le7$ the region lies inside
manuscript~06's closure; for $8\le k\le12$ it is the only settled part of the
window below $N(k)$ ($N(8),\ldots,N(12)=2688,1863,5364,3883,10483$,
\cref{evo:tab:thresholds}).

\begin{example}[A worked finite optimum; manuscript~01]\label{evo:et:ex:n8k5}
For $n=8$ and $k=5$, the selected split is $(a,b)=(3,5)$. Direct evaluation
of \eqref{evo:eq:positivepalette} gives
\[
 \Bip_5(3,5)=5\cdot4^5+20\cdot3\cdot3^5+60\cdot2^5=21620.
\]
The two primitive cycle words return $ab=15$ proper colorings, so the
candidate defect is $21605$ and the candidate maximum is
$5^8-21605=369020$. The finite certificate proves that this candidate is the
actual maximum, without needing to know whether $8\ge N_\exists(5)$. This
value is already established in Part~I (\cref{sec:correction}, which
corrects Davies's printed $368020$); it is an explanatory example, not a newly
claimed numerical record.
\end{example}

\subsection{Separation of the producer and checker}

The producer \path{code/03-eventual-verify.py} evaluates Stirling numbers by
their recurrence, uses the explicit maximum-product formula for $J$, and uses
the residue formula for the proposed split. Its separate finite-verification
tasks also check the two chromatic formulas, strict balancing, collision
graphs, actual witness orbits, and recurrences.

The checker \path{code/03-eventual-check_certificates.py} imports none of the
producer's code. It computes complete-bipartite color counts by
inclusion--exclusion, computes residual product bounds by an integer-partition
dynamic program, and finds the closest coprime split by direct enumeration
rather than by residue cases. It then enumerates the full parameter minimum
independently for each CSV row. All $919$ rows were accepted, with zero
rejected rows.

This is implementation independence, not logical independence of the
underlying theorem: both programs check the finite certificate principle of
\cref{evo:et:prop:finite}. Neither is a proof assistant, and agreement between
them cannot validate an incorrect mathematical statement shared by both. The
proof of the principle is therefore included in full rather than hidden
behind the programs.

\subsection{Recorded checks and their meaning}

The producer recorded $42960$ successful checks, grouped as follows.

Candidate residue-recurrence instances & 416\\
\midrule
Total & 42960\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The $24$ witness checks are three checks for each of eight automata:
\[
 (n,k)\in\{(7,3),(8,3),(9,3),(10,3),(7,4),(8,4),(7,5),(8,5)\}.
\]
Their complete breadth-first searches visited $605713$ coloring states in
total. Each search finishes the entire orbit; none reports a truncated partial
orbit as complete. The program's explicit state limit is a guard that raises
an error, not a stopping rule that manufactures a result.

The $120$ graph tests and $120$ automaton-bound tests use a recorded,
deterministic random seed and small state spaces where literal enumeration is
possible. They are regression tests, not an exhaustive classification of all
automata in their size ranges. The exact infinite balancing proof and the
structural exclusion argument do not depend on their success.

\subsection{Comparison with Parts~I and~II and between the manuscripts}\label{evo:sec:rowcheck}

In the write phase the shipped tables were compared by exact integer
arithmetic with each other and with $D^*_k(n)$ computed from
\eqref{evo:eq:positivepalette}. All $919$ rows of
\path{data/03-eventual-finite_certificates.csv} have
$D^{\mathrm{cert}}_k(n)=D^*_k(n)$ equal to the formula and the split
$(a_n,b_n)$. All $372$ rows of Part~I's \path{data/finite_range_bounds.csv}
have \texttt{upper} $=$ \texttt{lower} $=k^n-D^*_k(n)$ and $(\ell,m)=(a_n,b_n)$;
$219$ of them ($k\le12$) are also rows of manuscript~01's table, and $95$
($4\le k\le7$) rows of manuscript~06's. Manuscript~06's $2{,}217$ rows have
the split $(a_n,b_n)$, and their \texttt{target\_gap} equals $D^*_k(n)$ in
all $1{,}025$ rows with $n\le300$ that were recomputed; $375$ of them are also
rows of manuscript~01's table, with equal values. The $23$ values of
\path{data/05-quartic-threshold-sample_values.csv} equal $k^n-D^*_k(n)$. The
$25$ rows of manuscript~03's table (\cref{evo:da:tab:finite}) equal
$D^*_3(n)=D_n$ and Part~II's rows. These are consistency checks of
independently written computations, not part of any proof.

\section{A second route: eventual exactness for every fixed \texorpdfstring{$k\ge3$}{k >= 3}}\label{evo:sec:separation}

This section is manuscript~01's, the base of its argument. It proves the
exact formula for every fixed $k\ge3$ and all sufficiently large $n$, without
an explicit threshold. For $k\ge4$, \cref{evo:thm:eventual} supersedes it
with $N(k)\le3k^4$; for $k=3$, Part~II's \cref{tor:thm:main} gives every
$n\ge7$. It remains a genuinely different route: a compactness argument over
graph parameters instead of explicit estimates, valid for every fixed
$k\ge3$ (not uniformly in $k$), and it does not presume that a singular
generator has rank $n-1$. Manuscript~01 stresses that Davies's two binary
questions, nonattainment of $k^n$ (Part~I) and exactness of the two-cycle
lower bound for $n\ge7$, are not the same question: the second requires
controlling virtually all colorings, not merely exhibiting a missing one.
Manuscript~01 also does not assert that the transition monoid of every
extremal DFAO must equal a particular $U_{a,b}$: it proves an exact
coloring-saturation criterion (\cref{evo:et:thm:extremizers}), and monoid
classification is a stronger question.

\begin{theorem}[Eventual exact optimum; manuscript~01]\label{evo:et:thm:main}
For each fixed integer $k\ge3$, there is an integer $N_\exists(k)$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{evo:et:eq:main}
 D_k(n)=D^*_k(n),\qquad
 R_2(n,k)=k^n-\Bip_k(a_n,b_n)+G_k(a_n,b_n)
\end{equation}
for all $n\ge N_\exists(k)$. One may require $N_\exists(k)\ge\max(7,k+1)$.
The same maximum is obtained if one maximizes the coloring-orbit size over
\emph{all} transformation pairs and all $k$-valued output maps, without first
requiring accessibility, minimality, or surjectivity.
\end{theorem}

The last sentence matters: the upper bound is genuinely universal, whereas
the equality witness is an actual accessible minimal DFAO
(\cref{evo:et:prop:witness}). No tacit passage from an abstract monoid to a
realizable minimal automaton is needed. The proof of \cref{evo:et:thm:main}
does not depend on the correctness of the repository's other mathematical
projects; manuscript~01 reproves the needed graph and counting facts, and its
one non-elementary imported assertion is the published fact that $U_{a,b}$
is two-generated for $1<a<b$, $\gcd(a,b)=1$ \cite[Theorem~8]{HolzerKoenig2004}.

\begin{proposition}[Structural separation; manuscript~01]\label{evo:et:prop:separation}
Fix $k\ge3$. For all sufficiently large $n$, any transformation pair and
output map whose defect is at most $D^*_k(n)$ has, after possibly exchanging
its generators, one permutation $p$ and one singular map $s$. Moreover, $p$
consists of exactly two coprime cycles. Every collision pair of $s$ connects
those two cycles.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Suppose there are arbitrarily large exceptions, and select a sequence of them
with $n\to\infty$. By \cref{evo:et:lem:witnessasym}, their defects are
$O_k(\lambda_k^n)$. The unmixed and nonsurjective cases are excluded by
\cref{evo:et:rem:unmixed}. Choose any collision pair of the singular
generator. By the collision bound and \cref{evo:et:lem:order}, its graph
satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{evo:et:eq:Pboundseq}
 \frac{P_G(k)}{\lambda_k^n}\le\frac{D^*_k(n)}{\lambda_k^n}+o(1).
\end{equation}
The same-cycle case is excluded by \cref{evo:lem:betabounds}(i),(iv) in
manuscript~01's form for $k\ge3$ (\cref{evo:sec:eventual}). We therefore have
$G=dK_{u,v}\sqcup rK_1$, $n=d(u+v)+r$, with integers $d\ge1$, $r\ge0$. From
\cref{evo:lem:twopalettes}, with $\varrho_k=k/\lambda_k$,
\begin{equation}\label{evo:et:eq:boundeddr}
 \frac{P_G(k)}{\lambda_k^n}\ge2^d\varrho_k^r.
\end{equation}
Thus $d$ and $r$ are bounded. Pass to a subsequence on which both are
constant, and, where needed, $n\bmod4$ is constant.

Both $u$ and $v$ tend to infinity on this subsequence. Indeed, if one stays
bounded, take a further subsequence on which it is constant. Color that part
with one color and the other part freely with the remaining $k-1$. This gives
at least $k(k-1)^{\max(u,v)}$ colorings per component. Since
$k-1>\lambda_k$, the normalized total tends to infinity, contradicting
\eqref{evo:et:eq:Pboundseq}.

If $k$ is even, let $C=\binom{k}{k/2}$. The sharpened palette lower bound
\eqref{evo:et:eq:evenLB} and boundedness of $d$ give
$P_G(k)/\lambda_k^n\ge(1-o(1))C^d2^r$. The witness limit in
\eqref{evo:et:eq:witnessasym} is $C$. Since $C\ge6$, either $d\ge2$ or $r\ge1$
gives a strictly larger limiting lower bound. Therefore $d=1$, $r=0$.

Now suppose $k=2h+1$ is odd. Write $C=\binom kh$ and $c=\cosh\omega_h$. Then
\[
 0<\omega_h\le\log2,\qquad 1<c\le\frac54,
 \qquad\varrho_k=2\cosh(\omega_h/2).
\]
By \eqref{evo:et:eq:oddLB},
\begin{equation}\label{evo:et:eq:oddsep}
 \frac{P_G(k)}{\lambda_k^n}\ge
 (1-o(1))(2C)^d\varrho_k^r
 \cosh^d\!\left(\frac{(v-u)\,\omega_h}2\right).
\end{equation}
The witness constant is at most $2C\cosh(2\omega_h)\le2C\cdot17/8$. If
$d\ge2$, the lower coefficient is at least $(2C)^2$, larger than this because
$C\ge3$. If $r\ge2$, it is at least $2C\varrho_k^2\ge8C$, also larger. It
remains to exclude the single delicate case $d=1$, $r=1$.

If $n$ is odd, its witness constant is $2C\cosh(\omega_h/2)$, whereas
\eqref{evo:et:eq:oddsep} is at least $2C\varrho_k=4C\cosh(\omega_h/2)$, already
larger. If $n$ is even, then $u+v=n-1$ is odd, so $|v-u|\ge1$. The factor after
$2C$ on the right of \eqref{evo:et:eq:oddsep} is at least
\[
 \varrho_k\cosh(\omega_h/2)=2\cosh^2(\omega_h/2)=1+c.
\]
For $n\equiv0\pmod4$, the witness factor is $c<1+c$. For $n\equiv2\pmod4$, it
is $\cosh(2\omega_h)=2c^2-1$, and
\begin{equation}\label{evo:et:eq:smallmargin}
 (1+c)-(2c^2-1)=2+c-2c^2\ge\frac18>0
 \qquad(1\le c\le5/4).
\end{equation}
Again there is a strict separation. Hence $d=1$, $r=0$ also for odd $k$.

Since $d=\gcd(a,b)$ and $r=n-a-b$, these conclusions say that $p$ has exactly
two coprime cycles. The collision pair was arbitrary. If an exceptional pair
lay on one cycle for arbitrarily large $n$, choose that pair in the preceding
argument and obtain the same-cycle contradiction. Thus every collision pair
connects the two cycles, once $n$ is sufficiently large. This contradicts the
assumed sequence of exceptions and proves the proposition.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why the odd-$k$ margin is necessary; manuscript~01]\label{evo:et:rem:margin}
Merely observing that an isolated vertex multiplies a coloring count by $k$
is not enough. Removing that vertex also changes $n\bmod4$ and can improve
the available coprime imbalance from four to one. Inequality
\eqref{evo:et:eq:smallmargin} compares both effects on the same scale.
Omitting it would leave a genuine hole in the structural argument,
particularly at $k=3$. Manuscript~06's effective proof avoids the issue by
bounding every nonfull graph by $\tfrac74\hat C\lambda_k^n$
(\cref{evo:lem:badcross}), and manuscript~03's by the explicit gap
\eqref{evo:da:eq:oddgap}.
\end{remark}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{evo:et:thm:main}]
For each $n$ there are finitely many transformation pairs and output maps, so
the maximum of their coloring-orbit sizes exists. By
\cref{evo:et:prop:witness}, the minimum defect among them is at most
$D^*_k(n)$. For fixed $k$ and sufficiently large $n$,
\cref{evo:et:prop:separation} applies to an optimizer. Its permutation has
exactly two coprime cycles, say of lengths $a<b$, and any collision pair
produces $K_{a,b}$ on the whole state set.

The proper-period bound of \cref{evo:et:lem:properperiod} sharpens the
chromatic bound to
\[
 D\ge\Bip_k(a,b)-G_k(a,b).
\]
This holds even if $\tau$ is improper, because then the true subtraction is
zero and the displayed bound is weaker. By \cref{evo:et:lem:coprime},
\[
 D\ge\Bip_k(a_n,b_n)-G_k(a_n,b_n)=D^*_k(n).
\]
The opposite inequality comes from the witness, so equality holds. Since the
witness is accessible and minimal, the unrestricted algebraic maximum and the
automata maximum coincide.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Criterion for eventual extremizers; manuscript~01]\label{evo:et:thm:extremizers}
For fixed $k\ge3$ and sufficiently large $n$, a transformation pair and output
map attain the unrestricted maximum if and only if, after exchanging the
generators when necessary, the following hold:
\begin{enumerate}
\item The permutation generator has two cycles of lengths $a_n,b_n$.
\item The output map is proper for the corresponding $K_{a_n,b_n}$ and its
permutation orbit has length $G_k(a_n,b_n)$.
\item Every improper coloring of that complete bipartite graph is reachable.
\end{enumerate}
For every maximizing pair, each nontrivial kernel class of the singular
generator has size two and meets both cycles.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Necessity of the two-cycle structure follows from
\cref{evo:et:prop:separation}. A split other than $(a_n,b_n)$ would make the
lower defect bound strictly larger by
\cref{evo:et:cor:balanceT,evo:et:lem:coprime}. Equality in the chromatic bound
requires both full reachability of improper colorings and the maximum allowed
number of proper ones. The latter forces $\tau$ itself to be proper and have
the stated period.

Conversely, the three properties give exactly
$k^n-\Bip_k(a_n,b_n)+G_k(a_n,b_n)$ reached colorings, which is the maximum by
\cref{evo:et:thm:main}.

Finally, \cref{evo:et:prop:separation} prohibits a kernel pair within either
cycle. A kernel class of three or more points would contain such a pair by
the pigeonhole principle. Any nontrivial class is therefore a cross pair.
\end{proof}

Manuscript~01 notes that this criterion does not force the singular map to
have exactly one collision pair: several disjoint cross pairs are not excluded
by the kernel argument, and whether full improper-coloring saturation permits
them is a separate algebraic problem (its question on rank and kernel
structure, \cref{evo:q:rank}). That problem is now answered: at $k=3$, for
every $n\ge7$, by Part~II's \cref{tor:thm:rigidity}(ii), and for $k\ge4$ on
manuscript~06's exactness range by \cref{evo:thm:rigidity}(ii) below, whose
proof exploits exactly the full improper saturation of (3). Both show rank
$n-1$ with one cross kernel pair.

\section{A third route: uniform separation and sharp constants}\label{evo:sec:sharp}

This section is manuscript~03's fixed-$k$ theory. Its separation step is a
second route to the exclusions of \cref{evo:et:prop:separation}, organized as
a compactness lemma with explicit quantifiers; its conclusions are the sharp
first-order asymptotics for every fixed $k\ge3$ (printed once, as
\cref{evo:thm:firstorder}), eventual exactness for fixed odd $k$, and
near-optimal rigidity. Manuscript~03 distinguishes three statements that can
otherwise be confused: proving $R_2(n,k)<k^n$ is nonattainment (Part~I);
determining the first-order behavior of $k^n-R_2(n,k)$ is a sharp deficit
asymptotic; proving an exact formula for $R_2(n,k)$ is an extremal theorem.
It reaches the third level for all $n\ge7$ when $k=3$, eventually for each
fixed odd $k$, and the second level for every fixed $k\ge3$. The meaning of
``fixed'' is essential: none of its asymptotic or eventual statements is
uniform in a number of outputs growing with $n$, and none settles the
monoid-size problem on the diagonal $k=n$. In its words, the execution of two
checkers adds reproducibility, not a second source of mathematical
authority.

\subsection{The cyclic correction cannot conceal a large graph count}

Fix $k\ge3$. For a two-cycle graph, \eqref{evo:eq:twopalettes} and
\cref{evo:lem:order} give
\[
 P_G(k)\ge2^d\lambda_k^{n-r}k^r,\qquad \Pi\le n^23^{r/3},
\]
where $\Pi$ bounds the reachable proper colorings of the pure orbit.
Consequently, with $\eta=\min\{\lambda_k,k/3^{1/3}\}>1$, we have the uniform
bound
\begin{equation}\label{evo:da:eq:uniformperiod}
 \frac{\Pi}{P_G(k)}\le\frac{n^2}{2\eta^n}\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
No assumption that $d$, $r$, or the imbalance is bounded was used. Also
\begin{equation}\label{evo:da:eq:drbound}
 \frac{P_G(k)}{\lambda_k^n}\ge2^d(k/\lambda_k)^r\ge2^{d+r}.
\end{equation}
For one-cycle graphs, $s^L+(-1)^Ls\ge(s^3-s)^{L/3}=\beta_k^L$ for $s=k-1\ge2$,
$L\ge2$, and $\beta_k>\lambda_k$ (\cref{evo:sec:eventual}), so
\[
 P_G(k)\ge\beta_k^{n-r}k^r\ge\beta_k^n,\qquad
 \frac{\Pi}{P_G(k)}\le\frac{n}{\min\{\beta_k,k/3^{1/3}\}^n}\longrightarrow0.
\]
Every one-cycle collision graph forces a deficit exponentially larger than
$\lambda_k^n$. The two easy generator cases have the same exclusion property
by \cref{evo:lem:easy}.

\begin{lemma}[Reduction of bounded normalized deficits; manuscript~03]\label{evo:da:lem:compact}
Consider a sequence of binary DFAOs with fixed output size $k$ and
$n\to\infty$, whose deficits are $O(\lambda_k^n)$. For all sufficiently large
$n$ the generators are mixed. Choose any collision of the singular generator
for each $n$. Its endpoints must lie on different permutation cycles. The
associated $d+r$ is bounded. Along any subsequence on which $d,r$ are fixed,
both reduced cycle lengths $u=a/d$ and $v=b/d$ tend to infinity.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The easy and one-cycle cases were just excluded. In a two-cycle case,
\eqref{evo:da:eq:uniformperiod} and the collision obstruction show
$\defi\ge(1-o(1))P_G(k)$. Thus $P_G(k)=O(\lambda_k^n)$, and
\eqref{evo:da:eq:drbound} bounds $d+r$.

Suppose, after passing to a fixed-$d,r$ subsequence, that $u$ is bounded.
Make its part monochromatic and use any of the other $k-1$ colors on the
opposite part. This gives $\Bip_k(u,v)\ge k(k-1)^v$. Since $dv=n-r-du$ and
$du$ stays bounded,
\[
 \frac{P_G(k)}{\lambda_k^n}
 \ge\frac{k^{d+r}(k-1)^{dv}}{\lambda_k^n}\longrightarrow\infty,
\]
because $k-1>\lambda_k$. This is a contradiction. Interchanging the parts
handles bounded $v$. If $u$ or $v$ failed to tend to infinity it would have a
bounded subsequence, already excluded.
\end{proof}

This lemma is the reason pointwise asymptotics suffice in the next step. An
arbitrary sequence of prospective counterexamples has a subsequence with
fixed $d,r$ and with both reduced lengths tending to infinity. There are no
hidden exceptional graphs with growing numbers of components or with a
bounded side.

\subsection{Even output size}

Let $k=2h\ge4$, so $\lambda_k=h$, and $C=\binom{2h}{h}$. The lower witness has
deficit $(C+o(1))h^n$. Take any sequence of optimal automata and apply
\cref{evo:da:lem:compact}. Along a subsequence with fixed $d,r$,
\eqref{evo:et:eq:evenLB} implies
\begin{equation}\label{evo:da:eq:evengraphconstant}
 \frac{\defi}{h^n}\ge(1-o(1))(C-o(1))^d\,2^r.
\end{equation}
For $d\ge2$ this has lower limit at least $C^2>C$; for $r\ge1$ it has lower
limit at least $2C>C$. Hence such parameter values cannot occur infinitely
often for optimal automata. The only possibility is $d=1$, $r=0$, where the
lower limit in \eqref{evo:da:eq:evengraphconstant} is $C$. This proves the
even case of \cref{evo:thm:firstorder} by comparison with the witness.

More formally, if the asserted liminf bound failed, a sequence violating it by
a fixed positive amount would admit the fixed-parameter subsequence above and
contradict \eqref{evo:da:eq:evengraphconstant}. The same argument shows that
any sequence whose deficit is asymptotic to the optimum eventually has only
spanning coprime two-cycle collision graphs.

\subsection{Odd output size: a strict separation of every other shape}

Let $k=2h+1$, $x=h(h+1)$, $\lambda_k=\sqrt x$, $C=\binom{2h+1}{h}$,
$t_h=(h+1)/h$, and consider $z\mapsto t_h^z+t_h^{-z}=2\cosh(z\omega_h)$,
strictly increasing on $(0,\infty)$. The lower witness has normalized deficit
$2C\cosh(\delta_n\omega_h)+o(1)$. Its coefficient is at most $(17/4)C$, since
$1<t_h\le2$ and $\delta_n\le2$.

Take a hypothetical infinite sequence of optimal or asymptotically optimal
automata with a competing graph. Pass to a subsequence with fixed $d,r$ and
fixed residue class of $n$ modulo four; both $u,v$ tend to infinity by
\cref{evo:da:lem:compact}. We now compare its lower-limit coefficient with the
witness coefficient.

If $d\ge2$, the inequality $2\cosh\ge2$ and \eqref{evo:et:eq:oddLB} give a
coefficient at least $(2C)^d(k/\lambda_k)^r$, which is at least
$4C^2>(17/4)C$. If $d=1$, $r\ge2$, the coefficient is at least
$2C(k/\lambda_k)^2\ge8C>(17/4)C$. These are strict gaps.

Suppose $d=1$, $r=1$. If $n$ is odd, $a+b=n-1$ is even, so the best possible
coefficient is at least $2Ck/\lambda_k$. The witness coefficient for odd $n$
is
\[
 2C\cosh(\omega_h/2)=C\Bigl(\sqrt{t_h}+\frac1{\sqrt{t_h}}\Bigr)=Ck/\lambda_k,
\]
exactly half that lower bound. If $n$ is even, $a+b$ is odd, and the
coefficient is at least
\[
 C(k/\lambda_k)\cdot2\cosh(\omega_h/2)=C(k/\lambda_k)^2=C(4+1/x).
\]
The larger of the even-$n$ witness coefficients is
$2C\cosh(2\omega_h)=C(2+4/x+1/x^2)$. Their difference is
\begin{equation}\label{evo:da:eq:oddgap}
 C(2-3/x-1/x^2)>0\qquad(x\ge2).
\end{equation}
Again there is a strict gap, including at $h=1$.

The only remaining case is $d=1$, $r=0$. Then $\gcd(a,b)=1$ and $a+b=n$.
Writing $z=|b-a|/2$, admissibility implies $z\ge\delta_n$. Hence
\eqref{evo:et:eq:oddLB} gives a normalized lower limit at least
$2C\cosh(\delta_n\omega_h)$. If the split is not the nearest one, the lattice
of possible imbalances gives $z\ge\delta_n+1$. Since $\cosh$ is strictly
increasing on positive arguments, this gives another fixed positive gap for
the chosen residue class. If $z$ is unbounded the inequality is stronger
still.

These comparisons prove the odd case of \cref{evo:thm:firstorder}. They also
prove something stronger: for all sufficiently large $n$, every optimal
automaton must have the nearest coprime two-cycle permutation. Otherwise a
subsequence of counterexamples would fall into one of the strictly separated
cases. The witness's error tends to zero, whereas the appropriate separation
is a fixed positive number for fixed $k$ and residue class.

\subsection{From asymptotic separation to exact odd-output optimality}

\begin{theorem}[Eventual exactness for odd output size; manuscript~03]\label{evo:da:thm:odd}
For each fixed odd $k\ge3$ there is an integer $N_\exists(k)$ such that, for
$n\ge N_\exists(k)$,
\begin{equation}\label{evo:da:eq:oddexact}
 R_2(n,k)=k^n-\Bip_k(a_n,b_n)+G_k(a_n,b_n).
\end{equation}
One may take $N_\exists(3)=7$ (\cref{evo:da:thm:main3}). Manuscript~03 does
not compute a numerical $N_\exists(k)$ for $k\ge5$; the proof is an eventual
separation argument.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For an optimal automaton in the surviving graph class, the graph count is
exactly $\Bip_k(a_n,b_n)$. The proper cyclic orbit has size at most
$a_nb_n$ when $k\ge5$, and at most $b_n$ when $k=3$. Therefore the collision
bound gives the exact upper bound
$R_2(n,k)\le k^n-\Bip_k(a_n,b_n)+G_k(a_n,b_n)$. The established lower witness
(Davies's Corollary~3, manuscript~03's Proposition~2.3) attains the
right-hand side, and it is minimal by \cref{evo:da:lem:minimal} once its orbit
exceeds $k^{n-1}$, which holds for all sufficiently large $n$ with $k$ fixed
by the estimates above. \Cref{evo:da:thm:main3} supplies $N_\exists(3)=7$.
\end{proof}

The argument does not merely infer an exact equality from a first-order
asymptotic. It first forces the optimizer into one particular finite-parameter
graph class by a strict constant gap; within that class it uses an exact
chromatic count and an exact available cyclic correction. Manuscript~03 calls
this distinction indispensable. For odd $k\ge5$ the theorem is now a special
case of \cref{evo:thm:eventual} (with $N(k)$ explicit, and every
$n\ge\max(7,k+1)$ when $k=5,7$); its proof is a third route.

\subsection{Near-optimal structural rigidity}

\begin{corollary}[Manuscript~03]\label{evo:da:cor:nearoptimal}
Fix $k\ge3$. Suppose a sequence of binary $n$-state DFAOs, with transitions
$p_n,s_n$ and outputs $\tau_n$, satisfies
$\defi(\tau_n;p_n,s_n)/D_k(n)\to1$. For all sufficiently large $n$, one
generator is a permutation with exactly two coprime cycles, and the other is
singular and injective on each cycle separately. If $k$ is odd, the two
lengths are exactly the nearest split \eqref{evo:eq:split} for all
sufficiently large $n$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Repeat the constant-gap arguments, which apply equally to a sequence with
relative error tending to zero. A selected collision must span the two coprime
cycles. Any collision within one of those cycles would give the exponentially
more expensive one-cycle obstruction, a contradiction. The odd-output
imbalance gap forces the nearest split. No assertion that the same
restricted structure suffices for near-optimality is made.
\end{proof}
