For even $k$ the corollary does not force the nearest split, and it cannot:
\cref{evo:da:thm:plateau} exhibits a whole interval of asymptotically optimal
splits within the witness family. Manuscript~06's exact rigidity
(\cref{evo:thm:rigidity}) forces the nearest split for exact maximizers at
every $k\ge4$ in its range.

\section{A second proof of Part~II's three-output theorem}\label{evo:sec:three}

Manuscript~03's headline is Part~II's \cref{tor:thm:main}, proved
independently: an analytic argument for $n\ge32$ and an exact certificate of
$3{,}717$ structural inequalities for $7\le n\le31$. Part~II's proof is
analytic with six displayed base cases (\cref{tor:sec:small}). Both are
kept; this section is the second proof.

\begin{theorem}[Exact three-output complexity; manuscript~03]\label{evo:da:thm:main3}
For every integer $n\ge7$,
\begin{equation}\label{evo:da:eq:main3}
 R_2(n,3)=3^n-\Bip_3(a_n,b_n)+b_n,\qquad \Bip_3(a,b)=3(2^a+2^b-2).
\end{equation}
The proof for $n\ge32$ is mathematical and does not depend on an enumerated
range. For $7\le n\le31$ it uses the complete exact certificate of
\cref{evo:da:sec:finite}.
\end{theorem}

This is \eqref{tor:eq:main}: $(a_n,b_n)=(A_n,B_n)$ and
$\Bip_3(a_n,b_n)-b_n=D_n$. The formula for $\Bip_3$ is Part~I's
\eqref{eq:threebip}; manuscript~03 reproves it: if the first part is
monochromatic there are $3\cdot2^b$ choices; if it uses exactly two colors
there are $3(2^a-2)$ choices, and the second part is forced to use the
remaining color.

\subsection{A finite structural reduction}

\begin{proposition}[Finite structural reduction for three outputs; manuscript~03]\label{evo:da:prop:finitebound}
For a fixed $n\ge3$, a valid lower bound for every individual three-output
deficit is the minimum of the following finite list:
\begin{align}
&2\cdot3^{n-1},\qquad4\cdot3^{n-2}-1;\label{evo:da:eq:candidates1}\\
&\bigl(2^{m/d}+(-1)^{m/d}2\bigr)^d3^{n-m}-H_{n-m}(m),
 \quad 2\le m\le n,\ d\mid m,\ d<m;\label{evo:da:eq:candidates2}\\
&\Bip_3(a/d,b/d)^d3^r-
 \begin{cases}
 \max\{H_r(a),H_r(b)\},&d=1,\\
 H_r(\lcm(a,b)),&d>1,
 \end{cases}\label{evo:da:eq:candidates3}
\end{align}
where $1\le a\le b$, $a+b\le n$, $d=\gcd(a,b)$, and $r=n-a-b$. Every pair of
transformations is covered; no transitivity, rank, or accessibility
hypothesis is omitted from this upper-bound reduction.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The two generators are either both permutations, both singular, or one of
each. Apply \cref{evo:lem:easy} in the first two cases. In the mixed case
choose any collision of the singular map, classify it by \eqref{eq:samecount}
or \eqref{eq:diffcount}, and use the collision obstruction and the
three-color period restriction, which is Part~I's \eqref{eq:periodimprove}:
in the two-cycle case with $d=1$, at least one part of a proper coloring of
$K_{a,b}$ is monochromatic, so the period on the occupied vertices divides
$a$ or $b$, and the remaining $r$ vertices contribute the order of a
permutation on them; if the initial coloring is not proper, the bound is
automatic. The listed parameter ranges contain its type.
\end{proof}

This is Part~I's collection \eqref{eq:Dnon}--\eqref{eq:Dbip} at $k=3$, with
the two-permutation entry $3^n-M(n,3)$ replaced by the weaker $2\cdot3^{n-1}$
and without the nonsurjective entry, which manuscript~03 does not list
(a nonsurjective map has deficit at least $3^n-2^n$, Part~II's
\cref{tor:lem:unmixed}, far above the target). Manuscript~03 remarks that it
is not legitimate to replace $b$ by $ab$ in the $d=1$ entry and expect a
sharp three-output answer: the discrepancy is only polynomial, but the exact
extremal formula retains precisely that polynomial correction.

\subsection{An analytic proof beyond the cutoff}

This subsection proves \cref{evo:da:thm:main3} for every $n\ge32$. Set
$\Lambda_n=2^{n/2}$. The desired minimum deficit is $\Bip_3(a_n,b_n)-b_n$.
From \eqref{evo:eq:split},
\begin{equation}\label{evo:da:eq:targetsize}
\Bip_3(a_n,b_n)=
\begin{cases}
(9/\sqrt2)\Lambda_n-6,&n\text{ odd},\\
(15/2)\Lambda_n-6,&n\equiv0\pmod4,\\
(51/4)\Lambda_n-6,&n\equiv2\pmod4.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
We prove that every graph other than a spanning, coprime $K_{a,b}$ forces a
deficit strictly larger even than $\Bip_3(a_n,b_n)$. Throughout,
\cref{evo:lem:order} gives $\Pi\le n3^{r/3}$ in the one-cycle case and
$\Pi\le n^23^{r/3}$ in the two-cycle case.

\emph{Excluding one-cycle collision graphs.} Put $\beta_3=6^{1/3}$. For every
$L\ge2$,
\begin{equation}\label{evo:da:eq:cyclebeta}
 2^L+(-1)^L2\ge\beta_3^L.
\end{equation}
For even $L$ this follows from $2>\beta_3$. For odd $L\ge3$,
$2^L-2\ge2^L(3/4)\ge2^L(3/4)^{L/3}=6^{L/3}$. Consequently
$P_G(3)\ge\beta_3^{n-r}3^r$. Since $3^{2/3}>\beta_3$ and $\beta_3^n\ge n$ in
the range in question,
\begin{equation}\label{evo:da:eq:cycleexclude}
P_G(3)-\Pi
\ge3^{r/3}\left[\beta_3^n
 \left(\frac{3^{2/3}}{\beta_3}\right)^r-n\right]
\ge\beta_3^n-n.
\end{equation}
For $n\ge12$,
\[
 \left(\frac{\beta_3}{\sqrt2}\right)^n\ge
 \left(\frac{\beta_3}{\sqrt2}\right)^{12}=\frac{81}{4},
 \qquad \frac n{\Lambda_n}\le1.
\]
Hence $\beta_3^n-n\ge(77/4)\Lambda_n>(51/4)\Lambda_n\ge\Bip_3(a_n,b_n)$. This
already excludes all one-cycle graphs for $n\ge12$. (Part~II's
\eqref{tor:eq:Clower} is the same bound $C_L\ge6^{L/3}$, proved by
induction.)

The easy generator bounds also exceed $\Bip_3(a_n,b_n)$ for $n\ge32$. For
instance, $3/\sqrt2>2$, so each of $2\cdot3^{n-1}$ and $4\cdot3^{n-2}-1$
dominates $(51/4)\Lambda_n$ in that range. Their ratios to $\Lambda_n$ are
increasing, and the comparison at $n=32$ is immediate.

\emph{A uniformly small cyclic correction for two-cycle graphs.} Write
$a=du$, $b=dv$, $r=n-d(u+v)$. For $u,v\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{evo:da:eq:p3rough}
 \Bip_3(u,v)=3(2^u+2^v-2)\ge3\cdot2^{(u+v)/2}\ge2\cdot2^{(u+v)/2}.
\end{equation}
Indeed $2^u+2^v\ge2\cdot2^{(u+v)/2}$ and $2^{(u+v)/2}\ge2$. Thus
\[
 P_G(3)\ge2^d2^{(n-r)/2}3^r,\qquad
 \frac{\Pi}{P_G(3)}\le
 \frac{n^2}{2^d\Lambda_n}\left(\frac{\sqrt2}{3^{2/3}}\right)^r
 \le\varepsilon_n:=\frac{n^2}{2\Lambda_n}.
\]
The sequence $\varepsilon_n$ decreases for $n\ge32$, and
$\varepsilon_{32}=1/128$. It follows that throughout this range
\begin{equation}\label{evo:da:eq:127}
 P_G(3)-\Pi\ge\frac{127}{128}P_G(3).
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Manuscript~03]\label{evo:da:lem:nonspan3}
For $n\ge32$, a two-cycle graph with $(d,r)\ne(1,0)$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{evo:da:eq:nonspanbounds}
P_G(3)\ge
\begin{cases}
9\sqrt2\,\Lambda_n-18,&n\text{ odd},\\
(27/2)\Lambda_n-18,&n\text{ even}.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The slightly stronger first inequality in \eqref{evo:da:eq:p3rough} gives
$P_G(3)\ge3^d\Lambda_n(3/\sqrt2)^r$. For $d\ge3$ the coefficient is at least
$27$. For $d=2$, $r\ge1$ it is at least $27/\sqrt2$. For $d=1$, $r\ge2$ it is
at least $27/2$. Each suffices.

If $d=2$, $r=0$, put $w=u+v=n/2\ge4$. The arithmetic--geometric mean
inequality gives
\[
\frac{P_G(3)}{\Lambda_n}
=\left(\frac{\Bip_3(u,v)}{2^{w/2}}\right)^2
\ge(6-6/2^{w/2})^2\ge(9/2)^2=81/4,
\]
which again suffices. This case has even $n$.

The remaining case is $d=1$, $r=1$. Then $P_G(3)=9(2^a+2^b-2)$ and
$a+b=n-1$. Among integer splits of a fixed sum, $2^a+2^b$ is smallest at a
balanced split. If $n$ is odd this yields $9\sqrt2\,\Lambda_n-18$; if $n$ is
even it yields $(27/2)\Lambda_n-18$.
\end{proof}

For even $n$, \eqref{evo:da:eq:127} and \eqref{evo:da:eq:nonspanbounds} imply
a deficit strictly exceeding the largest even target in
\eqref{evo:da:eq:targetsize}, since
\begin{equation}\label{evo:da:eq:evenmargin}
\frac{127}{128}\left(\frac{27}{2}\Lambda_n-18\right)
-\left(\frac{51}{4}\Lambda_n-6\right)
=\frac{165}{256}\Lambda_n-\frac{759}{64}>0.
\end{equation}
At $\Lambda_n=2^{16}$ this margin is $2702601/64$; it increases with
$\Lambda_n$. For odd $n$, the analogous difference is
\[
 \frac{127}{128}(9\sqrt2\,\Lambda_n-18)
 -\left(\frac9{\sqrt2}\Lambda_n-6\right)
 =\frac{567}{64\sqrt2}\Lambda_n-\frac{759}{64}>0
\]
in the same range. All excluded graph types therefore force a deficit larger
than $\Bip_3(a_n,b_n)$, not merely larger than $\Bip_3(a_n,b_n)-b_n$.

\emph{Optimizing the only remaining graph.} The sole remaining possibility is
$r=0$, $d=1$: two coprime cycles covering all $n$ states, and graph $K_{a,b}$.
Assume $a\le b$. By the three-color period restriction, its deficit is at
least $F_n(a,b)=3(2^a+2^b-2)-b$. If $a\ge2$ and $a\le b$, moving one vertex
from the smaller part to the larger part changes this quantity by
\begin{equation}\label{evo:da:eq:monotone3}
 F_n(a-1,b+1)-F_n(a,b)=3(2^b-2^{a-1})-1>0.
\end{equation}
It is therefore strictly minimized over coprime splits by the closest such
split, namely \eqref{evo:eq:split} (Part~II's \cref{tor:lem:optimize} is the
same computation). The published lower witness supplies an attaining
automaton, and \cref{evo:da:lem:minimal} applies: the finite certificate and
the analytic comparisons give $\Bip_3(a_n,b_n)-b_n<2\cdot3^{n-1}$ for every
$n\ge7$. This proves \cref{evo:da:thm:main3} for every $n\ge32$.

\subsection{The exact finite certificate}\label{evo:da:sec:finite}

The infinite proof leaves exactly the $25$ integers $7\le n\le31$. The
certificate is not a sample of automata. It enumerates the entire finite list
of \cref{evo:da:prop:finitebound} for each of these integers. The sum of the
list lengths is $3{,}717$. Each number is evaluated in integer arithmetic, and
in every row the smallest bound is exactly $\Bip_3(a_n,b_n)-b_n$. Moreover,
the only minimizing graph parameters are $r=0$, $d=1$, $(a,b)=(a_n,b_n)$.

\emph{Why the order calculation is exhaustive.} The order of a permutation is
the least common multiple of its cycle lengths. Enumerating every
nondecreasing partition of $r$ into positive integers therefore produces
precisely $\mathcal O_r$. The producer uses that description. The independent
checker instead uses the recurrence
\begin{equation}\label{evo:da:eq:ordersdp}
 \mathcal O_0=\{1\},\qquad
 \mathcal O_r=\bigcup_{j=1}^r\{\lcm(j,o):o\in\mathcal O_{r-j}\}.
\end{equation}
For one inclusion, append a $j$-cycle to a permutation on $r-j$ points. For
the other, remove any cycle from a permutation on $r$ points. Repeated
partitions cause no difficulty because the result is a set of orders. Taking
the maximum in \eqref{eq:H} now evaluates the exact period bound, not a
numerical approximation to a Landau function.

The independent implementation also changes the chromatic calculation: it
reconstructs $\Bip_3(a,b)$ by inclusion--exclusion for onto maps instead of
using the closed form. It enumerates one-cycle cases by component length
rather than component count, and two-cycle cases by total occupied size
rather than first cycle length. It compares every parameter record and
integer value with the producer's certificate. Finally, it finds the optimal
coprime split directly, without calling the nearest-split formula.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}rrrr@{}}
\caption{The complete finite range of manuscript~03 (the shipped
\texttt{data/04-deficit-asymptotics-finite\_table.tex}, input verbatim). Each
row records its minimizing deficit $D_3(n)=D_n$ and the number of structural
cases checked.}\label{evo:da:tab:finite}\\
\toprule
$n$ & $(a_n,b_n)$ & $D_3(n)$ & Cases\\
\midrule\endfirsthead
\toprule $n$ & $(a_n,b_n)$ & $D_3(n)$ & Cases\\\midrule\endhead
\midrule\multicolumn{4}{r}{Continued on next page}\endfoot
\bottomrule\endlastfoot
\input{data/04-deficit-asymptotics-finite_table.tex}
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

\emph{What the certificate proves, and what it does not.} By
\cref{evo:da:prop:finitebound}, every automaton's deficit is at least one of
the listed structural bounds and therefore at least their minimum. The
computed minimum is the target, and the established lower witness attains the
corresponding number of states. This proves the exact answer for each checked
$n$; with the analytic tail it proves \cref{evo:da:thm:main3} for all $n\ge7$.
This is a computer-assisted finite step in a mathematical proof. It depends on
the displayed reduction, the elementary order recurrence, the correctness of
the two programs, and their execution. It is not a proof in Lean, and two
implementations written during one investigation are not two external
referees. The machine-readable certificate
(\path{data/04-deficit-asymptotics-finite_certificate.json}), producer,
independent checker, and their recorded outputs are shipped so these
obligations can be examined and rerun. The rows agree with Part~II's
\path{data/02-three-output-structural_checks.csv} and, for $n\le30$, with
Part~I's table (\cref{evo:sec:rowcheck}).

\subsection{Residue formulas and concrete examples}

Expanding \eqref{evo:da:eq:main3} gives manuscript~03's deficit formula
\begin{equation}\label{evo:da:eq:closed3}
D_3(n)=
\begin{cases}
9\cdot2^{(n-1)/2}-(n+1)/2-6,&n\text{ odd},\\
\frac{15}{2}\,2^{n/2}-n/2-7,&n\equiv0\pmod4,\\
\frac{51}{4}\,2^{n/2}-n/2-8,&n\equiv2\pmod4,
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
which is Part~II's \eqref{tor:eq:deficitpiece}. The linear subtractions are
part of the exact theorem; they do not arise from an asymptotic
approximation. Manuscript~01 writes the same law on classes modulo four:
with $n=4t+r$,
\begin{equation}\label{evo:et:eq:D3residues}
\begin{aligned}
 D_3(4t)&=\tfrac{15}{2}4^t-2t-7, &
 D_3(4t+1)&=9\cdot4^t-2t-7,\\
 D_3(4t+2)&=\tfrac{51}{2}4^t-2t-9, &
 D_3(4t+3)&=18\cdot4^t-2t-8.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The half-integer coefficients in the even classes cause no integrality
problem: the admissible values of $t$ have $4^t$ divisible by two.
Equivalently, on the odd integers, $D_3(2t+1)=9\cdot2^t-t-7$. (Manuscript~01
states these for $D_3$ eventually and for $D^*_3$ throughout $n\ge7$; with
Part~II they hold for $D_3$ at every $n\ge7$. The write phase checked
\eqref{evo:et:eq:D3residues} and \eqref{evo:da:eq:closed3} against $D^*_3(n)$
for $7\le n\le199$.) Manuscript~01 adds that the normalized deficit has three
limits, $9/\sqrt2$, $15/2$ and $51/4$, on odd $n$, $n\equiv0$ and
$n\equiv2\pmod4$ (Part~II's \cref{tor:cor:asymptotic}), so there is no single
constant $c$ with $D_3(n)\sim c(\sqrt2)^n$ along all integers, and replacing
the assertion by merely $D_3(n)=\Theta((\sqrt2)^n)$ would lose genuine
arithmetic information.

At $n=7$, the split is $(3,4)$, the proper-coloring count is $66$, and
$R_2(7,3)=2187-66+4=2125$. At $n=8$, the split is $(3,5)$, giving
$R_2(8,3)=6561-114+5=6452$. At $n=10$, the split is $(3,7)$ rather than
$(4,6)$, because the latter is not coprime; the deficit is $395$. At $n=11$
the deficit drops to $276$. Thus $D_3(n)$ itself need not be monotone, despite
its exponential scale (as Part~II also notes in \cref{tor:sec:witnesses}).
These values follow from the proved formula, not from interpreting a random
search as exhaustive.

\subsection{Equality and rigidity for three outputs}

\begin{theorem}[Necessary structure of every optimizer; manuscript~03]\label{evo:da:thm:rigid3}
Let $n\ge7$. If a binary three-output DFAO has reversal complexity
$R_2(n,3)$, then, after possibly interchanging the two input letters:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item One letter is a permutation $p$, and the other is singular, $s$.
\item $p$ has exactly two cycles, of lengths $a_n$ and $b_n$.
\item The restriction of $s$ to each of these two cycles is injective.
\item The output coloring is constant on the smaller cycle, uses the other two
colors on the larger cycle, and its cyclic word on the larger cycle has least
period $b_n$.
\item Every coloring that uses some color on both cycles belongs to the
reversal orbit.
\end{enumerate}
Conversely, an accessible automaton with the stated two-cycle permutation,
the coloring in (iv), and a singular generator whose orbit contains every
coloring in (v), attains $R_2(n,3)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The bounds for two permutations and two singular maps are strictly larger
than the optimal deficit in both the certified and analytic ranges. Thus (i)
holds. Choose any collision of $s$. The only structural parameters that can
attain the minimum are a spanning coprime cross graph on the nearest split;
this is the strict conclusion of the analytic tail and the recorded
uniqueness check in \cref{evo:da:sec:finite}. It forces (ii). If $s$ had a
collision within one cycle, applying the obstruction to that collision would
give a strictly larger deficit. This proves (iii).

For the resulting graph $K_{a_n,b_n}$, equality in the obstruction requires
exactly $b_n$ proper colorings in the orbit and all $3^n-\Bip_3(a_n,b_n)$
improper colorings in the orbit. In particular $\tau$ is proper. A proper
surjective three-coloring has a monochromatic part. If the larger part were
monochromatic, the cyclic orbit would have size at most $a_n<b_n$. Thus the
smaller part is monochromatic, and the larger has least period $b_n$. This
proves (iv) and (v).

Conversely, (iv) gives $b_n$ distinct proper colorings under powers of $p$.
Condition (v) supplies all the improper ones. Their union has the cardinality
in \eqref{evo:da:eq:main3}, which is the universal maximum. The original
automaton is minimal by \cref{evo:da:lem:minimal}.
\end{proof}

Manuscript~03 stresses that condition (v) is a reachability condition, not a
simple classification of all possible singular generators; injectivity on the
two blocks alone is not asserted sufficient, and determining which singular
maps satisfy the remaining condition is a genuine next problem (its
\cref{evo:q:classify}; Part~II's \cref{tor:q:singular}). Part~II's
\cref{tor:thm:rigidity} is stronger: it adds that $s$ has rank $n-1$ with
exactly one cross double fiber, and its (iv) is \eqref{tor:eq:saturated}.
Manuscript~03's converse (sufficiency given (v)) is the equality case
Part~II's proof also uses; Part~II's \cref{tor:ex:nonsufficient} and
manuscript~06's \cref{evo:ex:nonsaturation} show that (i)--(iv) alone do not
imply (v).

\section{Rigidity of maximizing automata}\label{evo:sec:rigidity}

This section and the next are manuscript~06's. Let $\mathcal E$ denote the
proved exactness range for $k\ge4$:
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:exactrange}
\mathcal E=\{(n,k):k\ge4,
  \ n\ge N(k)\ \text{or}\
  [4\le k\le7\text{ and }n\ge\max(7,k+1)]\}.
\end{equation}
The first part of this range is analytic; the additional finite portions carry
the computational status stated in \cref{evo:sec:finite}.

\begin{theorem}[Necessary structure at the maximum; manuscript~06]\label{evo:thm:rigidity}
Suppose $(n,k)\in\mathcal E$ and a binary DFAO attains $R_2(n,k)$. After
interchanging the two letters if necessary, its transition maps are $p,s$ with
the following properties:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $p$ is a permutation with exactly two cycles $X,Y$, of lengths
$a_n,b_n$;
\item $s$ has rank $n-1$, and its unique nontrivial kernel class consists of
one point of $X$ and one point of $Y$;
\item $\tau(X)\cap\tau(Y)=\varnothing$ and the pure-$p$ orbit of $\tau$ has
size $a_nb_n$;
\item every coloring improper on $K_{a_n,b_n}$ is reached.
\end{enumerate}
These are necessary conditions. Conditions (i)--(iii) alone are not
sufficient.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The strict exclusions show that exactly one letter is a permutation and one
is singular. Select any collision pair of the singular map. The same strict
exclusions force its graph to be full, so the permutation has exactly two
coprime cycles. Strict balancing forces their lengths to be $a_n,b_n$,
proving (i).

No two points in the same cycle can be identified by $s$, because selecting
such a collision pair would produce a strictly excluded same-cycle graph.
Therefore every kernel class has at most two points, and every class of size
two has one point in each cycle. Distinct nontrivial classes form a cross
matching.

If there were at least two such classes, every word containing $s$ would
produce a coloring with at least two disjoint monochromatic cross edges: write
the word as $u\circ s\circ p^j$ and pull the two kernel pairs back by $p^j$.
There exist colorings with exactly one monochromatic cross edge. For instance,
give one chosen cross pair a common color and use disjoint other palettes on
the two remaining sides. This uses at most three colors and is possible since
$k\ge4$. Such a coloring cannot arise from a word containing $s$. On the other
hand, equality in the basic collision bound requires $\tau$ to be proper;
hence all pure-$p$ colorings are proper and cannot supply this missing
improper coloring. The maximum is impossible when there are two or more
kernel pairs. Thus there is exactly one, proving (ii).

Finally, in the full graph case the upper bound is
$k^n-\Bip_k(a_n,b_n)+a_nb_n$. To attain it, the proper part must contain all
$a_nb_n$ possible pure-orbit states and the improper part must contain all
$k^n-\Bip_k(a_n,b_n)$ improper states. Properness is preserved by $p$, so
$\tau$ must be proper. This proves (iii) and (iv).
\end{proof}

This is the $k\ge4$ counterpart of Part~II's \cref{tor:thm:rigidity}, whose
(ii) is proved the same way, with a coloring that has exactly one
monochromatic cross pair (compare \cref{tor:prop:rankpenalty}); at $k\ge4$ the proper output has a full period
$a_nb_n$ instead of Part~II's $B_n$. Manuscript~01's
\cref{evo:et:thm:extremizers} gives (i), the proper-output condition and (iv)
for every fixed $k\ge3$ and large $n$, and shows that together they are also
sufficient; (ii) is where manuscript~06 goes further.

\begin{remark}[Manuscript~06]\label{evo:rem:rigidity}
The theorem constrains the actual two letter transformations, not merely an
abstract monoid isomorphic to their transition monoid. The singular map's
unique collision is forced to be a cross-cycle collision. Its image and its
action away from the collision still contain information not determined by
the theorem.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}[Rank one deficiency and a full output period are not enough; manuscript~06]\label{evo:ex:nonsaturation}
Use states $0,\ldots,6$ and define, in image-list notation,
\[
\begin{aligned}
p&=(1,2,0,4,5,6,3),\\
s&=(0,1,2,0,4,5,6),\\
\tau&=(0,0,1,2,2,2,3).
\end{aligned}
\]
The permutation has cycles $X=\{0,1,2\}$ and $Y=\{3,4,5,6\}$. The map $s$ has
rank six and its only collision is the cross pair $\{0,3\}$. The output
palettes are disjoint, and the output words on the two cycles have least
periods three and four. Thus the pure orbit has size twelve.

Nevertheless, $X$ is invariant under both transformations and $s$ is the
identity there. Every reachable coloring restricts to one of the three
rotations of $(0,0,1)$ on $X$. Many improper colorings fail this necessary
condition and are unreachable. In fact, the supplied literal orbit calculation
returns $564$ reachable colorings (\path{data/05-quartic-threshold-supplementary.json}),
far below the optimum $15\,472$.

This is not an artifact of inaccessible or equivalent original states. Taking
initial state $3$ gives accessibility: rotate through $Y$, then apply $s$ to
reach $X$, and rotate through $X$. States in different blocks are already
distinguished by their outputs, and states in the same block are
distinguished by powers of $p$, because their cyclic output words are
primitive. Thus the original DFAO is minimal.
\end{example}

The example identifies the missing issue in a complete extremizer
classification: rank, cycle shape, and initial coloring do not by themselves
guarantee that the singular words cover the entire improper family. It is the
$k=4$ analogue of Part~II's \cref{tor:ex:nonsufficient}, with the invariant
block on the shorter cycle instead of the longer.

\section{A rank-sensitive matching obstruction}\label{evo:sec:rank}

The proof of rank rigidity uses only two kernel pairs. Keeping the entire
matching produces a stronger family of instance-sensitive upper bounds. This
result does not require a large-$n$ hypothesis.

\subsection{The matching statistic on output colorings}

Suppose $p$ has two coprime cycles $X,Y$ of lengths $a,b$, and all nontrivial
kernel classes of $s$ are disjoint cross pairs. Let their number be $t\ge1$,
so $\rk(s)=a+b-t$. For a coloring $c$, write
\[
 a_\gamma=|\{x\in X:c(x)=\gamma\}|,\qquad
 b_\gamma=|\{y\in Y:c(y)=\gamma\}|.
\]
The graph of monochromatic cross edges is a disjoint union
$\bigsqcup_\gamma K_{a_\gamma,b_\gamma}$. Its maximum matching size is
therefore
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:nustatistic}
 \nu(c)=\sum_{\gamma=1}^k\min(a_\gamma,b_\gamma).
\end{equation}
Define
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:Wdefinition}
 W_{k,t}(a,b)=\#\{c:X\sqcup Y\to[k]:\nu(c)<t\}.
\end{equation}
The case $t=1$ is exactly $W_{k,1}(a,b)=\Bip_k(a,b)$.

\begin{theorem}[Matching obstruction hierarchy; manuscript~06]\label{evo:thm:matching}
Under the preceding assumptions,
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:matchingbound}
 |\tau\langle p,s\rangle|\le k^{a+b}-W_{k,t}(a,b)+ab.
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:matchingegf}
 W_{k,t}(a,b)=a!\,b!\,[x^ay^bz^{<t}]
 \left(\sum_{i,j\ge0}\frac{x^iy^j}{i!\,j!}z^{\min(i,j)}\right)^k,
\end{equation}
where $[z^{<t}]$ means summing the coefficients of $z^0,\ldots,z^{t-1}$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A word containing $s$ can be written $u\circ s\circ p^j$. The $t$ distinct
kernel pairs, pulled back by $p^j$, remain disjoint cross pairs and are
monochromatic in the resulting output coloring. Hence that coloring has
$\nu\ge t$. All reachable colorings with $\nu<t$ must therefore come from
powers of $p$, contributing at most $ab$ possibilities. This proves
\eqref{evo:eq:matchingbound}.

For \eqref{evo:eq:matchingegf}, a single output color used $i$ times on $X$
and $j$ times on $Y$ contributes $\min(i,j)$ to $\nu$. The two sides are
labeled, so the exponential generating factor is
$x^iy^jz^{\min(i,j)}/(i!\,j!)$. Multiply the factors for the $k$ named colors,
extract the desired sizes and matching range, and multiply by $a!\,b!$. Only
finitely many terms can affect a specified coefficient, so the formal
power-series calculation has no convergence assumption.
\end{proof}

The hierarchy is monotone: increasing $t$ increases the excluded family
$\{\nu<t\}$. It uses the actual kernel pattern rather than only one collision.
It remains an upper bound, not a claim that every coloring with $\nu\ge t$ is
reachable. It carries out, for two coprime cycles, the refinement Part~I
proposes in \cref{sec:conclusion} (``using more than one collision'').

\subsection{The first exact correction beyond proper colorings}

\begin{proposition}[Manuscript~06]\label{evo:prop:firstmatching}
For $a,b\ge1$ and $k\ge2$,
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:W2}
 W_{k,2}(a,b)=\Bip_k(a,b)+\Omega_{k,1}(a,b),
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:M1}
 \Omega_{k,1}(a,b)
 =k\sum_{\substack{1\le i\le a,\ 1\le j\le b\\\min(i,j)=1}}
       \binom ai\binom bj\Bip_{k-1}(a-i,b-j).
\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:oneedgepenalty}
 \Omega_{k,1}(a,b)\ge kab\,\Bip_{k-1}(a-1,b-1).
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The condition $\nu=1$ says that exactly one color is used on both sides, and
for that color the smaller of its two multiplicities is one. Choose that
color, choose its $i$ vertices on $X$ and $j$ vertices on $Y$, and then color
the remaining graph properly with the other $k-1$ colors. This is a disjoint
classification and gives \eqref{evo:eq:M1}. The subcase $i=j=1$ gives
\eqref{evo:eq:oneedgepenalty}; it counts exactly the colorings with one
monochromatic cross edge.
\end{proof}

For an otherwise correctly shaped candidate whose singular map has at least
two cross kernel pairs, the loss relative to the full one-collision bound is
at least the explicit integer in \eqref{evo:eq:oneedgepenalty}. In the proved
exactness range with $a=a_n$, $b=b_n$, this is a quantitative stability
statement relative to the actual optimum. At $k=3$,
$kab\,\Bip_2(a-1,b-1)=3ab\cdot2=6ab$ for $a,b\ge2$, which is Part~II's
\cref{tor:prop:rankpenalty}.

\begin{remark}[Different obstructions live on different scales; manuscript~06]\label{evo:rem:scales}
The nonfull-graph penalty in \eqref{evo:eq:nonfullpenalty} is on the scale
$\lambda_k^n$. The one-edge family in \eqref{evo:eq:oneedgepenalty}, at
near-balanced side sizes, is governed instead by the palette counts for $k-1$
colors. This distinction suggests more than one asymptotic scale in the
near-maximum spectrum. It does not identify the second-largest attainable
state complexity: a matching obstruction need not be sharp for any given
singular transformation.
\end{remark}

\section{Exact exponential expansions of the optimum}\label{evo:sec:asymptotics}

The formula of \cref{evo:thm:eventual} identifies the optimal deficit from
the trivial bound with a finite palette sum minus a quadratic term. It
therefore gives considerably more information than its exponential growth
rate alone. Throughout this section $k$ is fixed and $n\to\infty$; the
constants in $O_k(\cdot)$ may depend on $k$, and $\delta_n\in\{\tfrac12,1,2\}$
is as in \eqref{evo:eq:split}.

\subsection{The first-order law, three times}

All three manuscripts prove the sharp first-order deficit for every fixed
$k$; it is printed once.

\begin{theorem}[Sharp defect asymptotics; manuscripts~01, 03 and~06]\label{evo:thm:firstorder}
Fix $k\ge3$, $h=\lfloor k/2\rfloor$. If $k=2h$ is even, then
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:evenfirst}
 D_k(n)\sim\binom{2h}{h}h^n.
\end{equation}
If $k=2h+1$ is odd, then
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:oddfirst}
 \frac{D_k(n)}{\lambda_k^n}
 =2\binom{2h+1}{h}\cosh(\delta_n\omega_h)+o(1),
\end{equation}
so for odd $k$ the three residue classes of $n$ (odd, $\equiv0$, $\equiv2$
modulo four) have three distinct leading constants. In either case
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:deficitroot}
 \lim_{n\to\infty}D_k(n)^{1/n}=\sqrt{\lfloor k^2/4\rfloor}=\lambda_k,
\end{equation}
and the relative deficit from $k^n$ has exponential base $\lambda_k/k$.
\end{theorem}

\emph{Three proofs.} (1)~Manuscript~01 substitutes the eventual equality
$D_k=D^*_k$ (\cref{evo:et:thm:main}) into \cref{evo:et:lem:witnessasym}; the
displayed leading constants are positive and bounded above and below
independently of $n$, giving \eqref{evo:eq:deficitroot}. In its own
normalization the odd law reads $D_k(n)/s_k^n=2\binom km\cosh(\lambda\delta_n/2)
+o(1)$ with $\lambda=\log((m+1)/m)$ and $\delta_n\in\{1,2,4\}$.
(2)~Manuscript~03 obtains the even law from the compactness argument of
\cref{evo:sec:sharp} (it does not need exactness for even $k$), and the odd law
together with \cref{evo:da:thm:odd}; in its normalization
$D_{2h+1}(n)\sim\binom{2h+1}{h}[h(h+1)]^{n/2}(t_h^{\delta_n}+t_h^{-\delta_n})$.
Taking $n$th roots gives \eqref{evo:eq:deficitroot}. (3)~Manuscript~06 derives
the sharper expansions of \cref{evo:thm:evenasymptotic,evo:thm:oddasymptotic}
below from \cref{evo:thm:eventual} (for $k\ge4$; at $k=3$ Part~II's
\cref{tor:cor:asymptotic} gives $9/\sqrt2$, $15/2$, $51/4$, the case $h=1$ of
\eqref{evo:eq:oddfirst}). The three normalizations agree: $2\binom kh\cosh
(\delta_n\omega_h)=\binom kh(t_h^{\delta_n}+t_h^{-\delta_n})$ and manuscript~06's
$\hat C=2\binom kh$ for odd $k$; the write phase checked this numerically for
$4\le k\le11$ and all three values of $\delta_n$. For odd $k$ the three
constants are distinct: this is an arithmetic oscillation in the leading
constant, not an error term caused by rounding a saddle point; it comes from
the coprimality requirement on two nearly equal cycle lengths
(manuscript~01).

\subsection{Even output counts: the residue effect is subleading}

\begin{theorem}[Manuscript~06]\label{evo:thm:evenasymptotic}
Let $k=2h\ge4$, and define
\[
 C_0=\binom{2h}{h},\qquad
 C_1=\frac{(2h)!}{(h-1)!\,(h+1)!},\qquad
 \mu_1=\sqrt{h^2-1},\qquad
 \xi_1=\sqrt{\max\{h(h-1),h^2-4\}}.
\]
Then $\xi_1<\mu_1<h$ and
\begin{align}
 k^n-R_2(n,k)
 &=C_0h^n
   +2C_1\mu_1^n
      \cosh\!\left(\delta_n\log\frac{h+1}{h-1}\right)\notag\\
 &\qquad-a_nb_n+O_k(\xi_1^n).
 \label{evo:eq:evenexpansion}
\end{align}
In particular, $(k^n-R_2(n,k))/h^n\to C_0$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The maximal product in the signed palette formula is $h^2$, from $(h,h)$.
The next largest product is $h^2-1$, from $(h-1,h+1)$ and its reversal; both
terms have coefficient $C_1$ and positive sign. Their sum is the
hyperbolic-cosine term displayed above.

All remaining pairs with total $2h$ have product at most $h^2-4$. Pairs with
smaller total have product at most $h(h-1)$. The bounded values of
$\delta_n$ keep every ratio factor uniformly bounded, so the remaining finite
sum is $O_k(\xi_1^n)$. Subtract $a_nb_n$ and use eventual exactness. The
inequalities between the exponential bases are immediate for $h\ge2$.
\end{proof}

Thus the mod-four displacement of the nearest coprime split does not change
the leading coefficient for even $k$, but it does affect the next exponential
scale. (For even $k$, $\mu_1=\mu$ of \eqref{evo:eq:constants}; the expansion
answers manuscript~03's question on second-order even asymptotics,
\cref{evo:q:evensecond}.)

\begin{example}[A closed formula for four outputs; manuscripts~06 and~01]\label{evo:ex:k4formula}
Specializing the signed palette sum gives the exact identity
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:k4P}
 \Bip_4(a,b)=6\cdot2^{a+b}+4(3^a+3^b)-12(2^a+2^b)+12.
\end{equation}
Therefore for every $n\ge7$,
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:k4exact}
 4^n-R_2(n,4)
 =6\cdot2^n+4(3^{a_n}+3^{b_n})-12(2^{a_n}+2^{b_n})+12-a_nb_n.
\end{equation}
The first nonconstant residue-dependent term has base $\sqrt3$, whereas the
leading deficit has base two. This is an exact formula, not a truncated
approximation.
\end{example}

Manuscript~01 derives the same identity \eqref{evo:eq:k4P} by expanding the
three nonzero terms of \eqref{evo:eq:positivepalette}, or by onto-map
inclusion--exclusion, notes that it is symmetric, as a complete-bipartite
coloring count must be, and states \eqref{evo:eq:k4exact} eventually; with
\cref{evo:thm:smallk} it holds for every $n\ge7$. Its leading constant is
always six, with no parity oscillation at the $2^n$ scale; parity still
affects the exponentially smaller terms and the polynomial correction, which
is why a leading-term calculation would not have sufficed to identify the
exact maximizing coprime split (manuscript~01). (The identity was checked in
the write phase for $1\le a,b\le11$.)

\subsection{Odd output counts: three leading constants}

\begin{theorem}[Manuscript~06]\label{evo:thm:oddasymptotic}
Let $k=2h+1\ge5$ and $\hat C=2\binom{2h+1}{h}$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:oddexpansion}
 k^n-R_2(n,k)
 =\hat C\lambda_k^n\cosh(\delta_n\omega_h)
   -a_nb_n+O_k\bigl((h(h+1)-2)^{n/2}\bigr).
\end{equation}
Consequently, the normalized deficit $(k^n-R_2(n,k))/\lambda_k^n$ has exactly
three subsequential limits,
\begin{equation}\label{evo:eq:oddlimits}
 \hat C\cosh\!\left(\tfrac12\omega_h\right),\qquad
 \hat C\cosh(\omega_h),\qquad
 \hat C\cosh(2\omega_h),
\end{equation}
corresponding respectively to odd $n$, $n\equiv0\pmod4$, and
$n\equiv2\pmod4$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The leading terms and error estimate are \cref{evo:lem:nearbalanced}.
Eventual exactness permits their direct use for the optimal deficit. The error
base is strictly smaller than $\lambda_k$, and $a_nb_n/\lambda_k^n\to0$. All
three residue classes occur infinitely often, and their limiting constants
are distinct because $\omega_h>0$ and $\cosh$ is strictly increasing on
positive arguments. There are no other limits because every integer belongs
to one of these three classes.
\end{proof}

Manuscript~06 proves \eqref{evo:eq:deficitroot} from these two theorems: the
leading coefficients are positive and bounded above and below for fixed $k$,
all errors have smaller exponential base, and the polynomial correction is
negligible. The threshold theorem is uniform in $k$, while these expansion
statements are formulated for fixed $k$. One should not silently replace $k$
by an arbitrary function of $n$ inside an $O_k$ term. A genuinely uniform
expansion in a growing-output regime would require tracking its constants and
the ratio between successive palette products.

\subsection{Eventual recurrences and rational generating functions}

This subsection is manuscript~01's. For fixed $k$, the residue subsequences
of the optimal deficit are eventually exponential polynomials, hence satisfy
constant-coefficient linear recurrences, which can be written down before any
initial values are computed.

\begin{lemma}[Finite exponential expansion; manuscript~01]\label{evo:et:lem:IE}
For $a,b\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{evo:et:eq:IE}
 \Bip_k(a,b)=\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\binom{k}{i}(k-i)^b
       \sum_{j=0}^{i}(-1)^{i-j}\binom{i}{j}j^a.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Choose the exact $i$-color palette on the left in $\binom ki$ ways. By
inclusion--exclusion, the number of onto maps to that palette is
$\sum_{j=0}^i(-1)^{i-j}\binom ij j^a$. Each right-hand vertex then has $k-i$
choices. Sum over $i$.
\end{proof}

Define the finite set of positive integer products
$\mathcal P_k=\{uv:u,v\ge1,\ u+v\le k\}$, and let $\mathsf E$ be the forward
shift on a sequence indexed by $t$: $(\mathsf EF)(t)=F(t+1)$.

\begin{theorem}[An explicit eventual annihilator; manuscript~01]\label{evo:et:thm:recurrence}
Fix $k\ge3$ and $r\in\{0,1,2,3\}$. The sequence $F_r(t)=D_k(4t+r)$ is
eventually annihilated by
\begin{equation}\label{evo:et:eq:annihilator}
 \mathcal A_k(\mathsf E)=(\mathsf E-1)^{e_k}
       \prod_{q\in\mathcal P_k\setminus\{1\}}(\mathsf E-q^2),
 \qquad e_3=2,\quad e_k=3\quad(k\ge4).
\end{equation}
The same recurrence holds for $D^*_k(4t+r)$ whenever all of the indices
involved are in its witness range. Minimality of the displayed annihilator is
not asserted for arbitrary $k$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Within one residue class, $a_{4t+r}$ and $b_{4t+r}$ each increase by two when
$t$ increases by one. The nonzero exponential terms in \eqref{evo:et:eq:IE}
have the form $j^a(k-i)^b$, with $j\ge1$, $k-i\ge1$, and $j+(k-i)\le k$. They
therefore multiply by $[j(k-i)]^2$ under $\mathsf E$. Equal products can be
combined; one factor $\mathsf E-q^2$ kills their combined contribution. The
subtracted period is linear in $t$ when $k=3$, and quadratic when $k\ge4$. It
is killed by $(\mathsf E-1)^2$ or $(\mathsf E-1)^3$, respectively; these
factors also kill constant exponential terms corresponding to $q=1$. The
eventual equality $D_k=D^*_k$ completes the proof.
\end{proof}

For example, when $k=3$ the annihilator is
$(\mathsf E-1)^2(\mathsf E-4)=\mathsf E^3-6\mathsf E^2+9\mathsf E-4$, so every
residue subsequence eventually satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{evo:et:eq:rec3}
 F_r(t+3)=6F_r(t+2)-9F_r(t+1)+4F_r(t).
\end{equation}
With Part~II this holds for every $t$ with $4t+r\ge7$; it is the order-three
form, in the step $t$, of Part~II's order-12 recurrence
\eqref{tor:eq:recurrence12} (whose characteristic polynomial in $z^4$ is
$(z^4-1)^2(z^4-4)$). For $k=4$ the annihilator is
$(\mathsf E-1)^3(\mathsf E-4)(\mathsf E-9)(\mathsf E-16)$. The code records
exact expanded coefficients for $3\le k\le10$
(\path{data/03-eventual-recurrences.json}) and checks $416$ recurrence
instances on the explicit candidate sequences. This does not supply missing
initial values of the actual optimum before the eventual threshold; for
$4\le k\le7$ \cref{evo:thm:smallk} now supplies them from $n=\max(7,k+1)$.

\begin{corollary}[Rationality on residue classes; manuscript~01]\label{evo:et:cor:rational}
For fixed $k\ge3$ and $r\in\{0,1,2,3\}$, the ordinary generating function of
the sequence $D_k(4t+r)$, with any convention for the finitely many indices
where $4t+r<k$, is rational. Its denominator divides
\[
 (1-z)^{e_k}\prod_{q\in\mathcal P_k\setminus\{1\}}(1-q^2z).
\]
The generating function of the full sequence $D_k(n)$ is rational as well,
with a denominator dividing the expression obtained by replacing $z$ by $z^4$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Multiply each residue generating function by the displayed denominator. The
recurrence makes all sufficiently high coefficients vanish, leaving a
polynomial. Changing finitely many initial values changes the generating
function by a polynomial, so does not spoil rationality. Interleaving the four
residues gives $\sum_{r=0}^3z^rG_r(z^4)$, again rational.
\end{proof}

This is an existence theorem for a rational generating function, not an
explicit numerator for the unknown finite initial segment. It concerns the
fixed-$k$ optimum, not a generating function where $k$ varies with $n$. At
$k=3$ it is Part~II's rationality statement in \cref{tor:cor:recurrence}.

\section{Even outputs: an interval of asymptotically optimal proportions}\label{evo:sec:plateau}

This section is manuscript~03's. For odd $k$, changing the cycle imbalance
changes the leading coefficient. For even $k$, both balanced palettes have
size $h$, so the main term is $\binom{2h}{h}h^{a+b}$ regardless of the
imbalance. Smaller palette terms only become relevant at a specific boundary.
This produces an interval, not a unique balanced ratio. Fix $k=2h\ge4$ and
define
\begin{equation}\label{evo:da:eq:threshold}
 \theta_h=\frac{\log((h+1)/h)}{\log((h+1)/(h-1))},\qquad 0<\theta_h<\frac12.
\end{equation}
The upper inequality is equivalent to $h^2>h^2-1$.

\begin{theorem}[The even-output interval criterion; manuscript~03]\label{evo:da:thm:plateau}
Let $a+b=n\to\infty$, $1<a<b$, $\gcd(a,b)=1$, and use the established
$U_{a,b}$ witness with $2h$ outputs. Its deficit is asymptotic to the global
optimum $\binom{2h}{h}h^n$ if and only if
\begin{align}
\Phi_n&=a\log\frac h{h-1}-b\log\frac{h+1}h\longrightarrow+\infty,
\label{evo:da:eq:X}\\
\Psi_n&=b\log\frac h{h-1}-a\log\frac{h+1}h\longrightarrow+\infty.
\label{evo:da:eq:Y}
\end{align}
Equivalently,
\begin{equation}\label{evo:da:eq:interiormargins}
 a-\theta_hn\longrightarrow+\infty,
 \qquad (1-\theta_h)n-a\longrightarrow+\infty.
\end{equation}
In particular, any sequence of admissible coprime splits with
$a/n\to\theta\in(\theta_h,1-\theta_h)$ is asymptotically optimal.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The witness deficit is $\Bip_{2h}(a,b)-ab$. The polynomial term is negligible
relative to $h^n$, so it suffices to characterize
$\Bip_{2h}(a,b)/h^n\to C=\binom{2h}{h}$.

First suppose this convergence holds. The monochromatic-side lower bound
$\Bip_{2h}(a,b)\ge2h(2h-1)^{\max(a,b)}$ implies that both $a$ and $b$ are
bounded below by a positive multiple of $n$: indeed
$\max(a,b)/n\le\log h/\log(2h-1)+o(1)<1$. The $(h,h)$ term in the positive
expansion \eqref{evo:da:eq:palette2} is therefore $(C+o(1))h^n$. The
neighboring full-palette terms $(h-1,h+1)$ and $(h+1,h-1)$, divided by $h^n$,
are positive constant multiples of $(1+o(1))e^{-\Phi_n}$ and
$(1+o(1))e^{-\Psi_n}$. Since the whole normalized sum tends to $C$, both must
tend to zero. This proves necessity.

Conversely assume \eqref{evo:da:eq:X}--\eqref{evo:da:eq:Y}. Both lengths are
again linear in $n$, by \eqref{evo:da:eq:interiormargins}. Consider the
logarithm
\[
 f(i)=a\log i+b\log(2h-i),\qquad 0<i<2h.
\]
It is strictly concave. The two neighboring differences from $f(h)$ are
$-\Phi_n$ and $-\Psi_n$. Concavity implies that every other integer $i\ne h$
has $f(i)-f(h)\to-\infty$: along either direction, successive increments are
no larger than the first negative increment. Thus every full-palette term
other than $(h,h)$ is $o(h^n)$.

If a term in \eqref{evo:da:eq:palette2} uses fewer than $2h$ colors, increase
one of its two palette sizes until the total is $2h$. Because both $a,b$ are
linear in $n$, its pure exponential is smaller than that full-palette
exponential by an exponentially vanishing factor. All full-palette
exponentials are $O(h^n)$ by the previous paragraph. There are only finitely
many palette pairs, so all terms with unused colors are $o(h^n)$ as well. The
onto-map errors vanish, leaving precisely $Ch^n$. Finally,
\eqref{evo:da:eq:interiormargins} follows by expanding $\Phi_n$ and $\Psi_n$
with $a+b=n$ and factoring out $\log((h+1)/(h-1))$.
\end{proof}

For $k=4$ the left boundary is $\theta_2=\log(3/2)/\log3\approx0.369$. The
interval is nontrivial and persists as $n$ grows. The theorem is a first-order
assertion about the established witness family. It does not imply that every
split in the interval is exactly optimal, or that every singular map with the
appropriate two-cycle permutation realizes the witness orbit. Indeed, for
$4\le k\le7$ and every $n\ge\max(7,k+1)$, and for every $k\ge4$ once $n\ge
N(k)$, only the nearest split is exactly optimal (\cref{evo:cor:split} with
\cref{evo:thm:main}); manuscript~06's second-order term
\eqref{evo:eq:evenexpansion} is what separates the splits inside the
interval.

\begin{remark}[Boundary behavior; manuscript~03]\label{evo:da:rem:boundary}
If $\Phi_n$ tends to a finite real number rather than $+\infty$, the
neighboring palette $(h-1,h+1)$ contributes a nonvanishing extra constant at
scale $h^n$. If $\Phi_n\to-\infty$, that contribution dominates the
balanced-palette scale. Thus the interval criterion identifies the precise
first-order boundary, not merely a convenient sufficient central region.
\end{remark}

\section{Reproducible checks and their limits}\label{evo:sec:verification}

\subsection{What each manuscript ran}

All programs of the three manuscripts use only the Python standard library,
exact integers, and explicit exceptions rather than \texttt{assert}
statements, so Python's optimization flag does not disable their checks. None
performs network access or contains downloaded certificates. Their recorded
outputs were produced with Python~3.13.5 (the environment records say so); the
write phase reran all of them on scratch copies (\cref{evo:sec:commands}).

\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Manuscript~06:} three programs with the file prefix
\texttt{code/05-quartic-threshold-}: the primary
\texttt{theorem\_\allowbreak checks.py}, the independent
\texttt{independent\_\allowbreak check.py}, and
\texttt{supplementary\_\allowbreak checks.py}; $2\times9{,}247{,}046$
comparisons in $2{,}217$ rows for $k=4,\ldots,7$, with zero failures and zero
unexpected equalities, and the supplementary counts of \cref{evo:sec:finite}.
\item \emph{Manuscript~01:} the producer \path{code/03-eventual-verify.py}
and the checker \path{code/03-eventual-check_certificates.py}; $42{,}960$
producer checks, $919/919$ certificate rows accepted, eight BFS witnesses with
$605{,}713$ states.
\item \emph{Manuscript~03:} the producer
\path{code/04-deficit-asymptotics-verify.py} and
\path{code/04-deficit-asymptotics-independent_check.py}; $25$ sizes, $3{,}717$
candidates, BFS counts $2125$, $6452$, $19550$, $58654$ for $n=7,\ldots,10$,
$12$ direct coloring checks, and the cutoff margin $2702601/64$.
\end{itemize}

\emph{Manuscript~06.} The exactness theorem for $n\ge N(k)$ has a conventional
proof written in full, apart from the explicitly imported published lower
construction. The extra finite portions for $4\le k\le7$ are
computer-assisted. Neither the conventional proof nor the programs have been
checked in Lean, Rocq, or another proof assistant, and the manuscript has not
been independently refereed. The infinite theorem does not depend on a
generated plot or on numerical floating-point estimates. All mathematical
formulas defining the finite obligations appear in the article, and source
code and result files are kept separate so that reproduction does not require
trusting an existing success summary. The extension to larger $k$ is
mathematically permitted, but the required finite computation grows; no unrun
finite range is certified.

\emph{Manuscript~01.} Both programs exit unsuccessfully when a check fails.
The independent checker also rejects missing, duplicated, or unexpected
certificate rows, and it validates an existing CSV; rerunning the producer
regenerates the data before the independent audit.
\path{data/03-eventual-asymptotic_ratios.csv} contains illustrative
floating-point ratios of the exact candidate defects to $\lambda_k^n$; these
are diagnostic illustrations, not certified interval bounds and not part of
any proof. The finite checks do not prove the infinite tail; the tail has a
separate proof. Likewise an unspecified tail plus the finite region does not
by itself cover any possible intervening gap.

\emph{Manuscript~03.} The producer reports $25$ checked state sizes and
$3{,}717$ structural candidates. It separately performs literal breadth-first
orbit searches for the three-output witnesses with $n=7,8,9,10$, obtaining
respectively $2125$, $6452$, $19550$ and $58654$ states. These four searches
are sanity checks of attainment and composition conventions, not the
upper-bound proof. The independent checker reconstructs all $3{,}717$
candidate records using the alternative order recurrence
\eqref{evo:da:eq:ordersdp} and coloring calculation, additionally checks $12$
small complete-bipartite coloring counts by literal color enumeration, and
verifies the rational cutoff margin \eqref{evo:da:eq:evenmargin}. The cutoff
inequality's extension to every larger $n$ is justified in the mathematical
proof, not by iterating a finite loop. Both programs were also run with
\texttt{python -O} (the \texttt{optimized\_*} consoles). The producer supports
exploratory \texttt{--max-n} values through $45$ and \texttt{--bfs-max-n}
through $12$, but larger explorations are not needed for the proof and change
output files. The word ``independent'' refers to implementation strategy, not
to authorship by an independent research team.

For its sanity checks manuscript~03 uses zero-based states: with $a=a_n$,
$b=b_n$, $p$ is the two increasing cycles on $\{0,\ldots,a-1\}$ and
$\{a,\ldots,n-1\}$; $s$ starts as the identity, with $s(0)=a$, $s(n-1)=0$
and, when $a>2$ is odd, $s(1)$ and $s(2)$ swapped. For three outputs, color
$0$ is used throughout the first block, color~$1$ at the first point of the
second block, and color~$2$ on its remaining points; at $n=7$ this gives
Part~II's arrays $p=(1,2,0,4,5,6,3)$, $s=(3,2,1,3,4,5,0)$,
$\tau=(0,0,0,1,2,2,2)$ (\cref{tor:sec:witnesses}). The pullback action in the
code is \texttt{tuple(c[q] for q in t)}; starting at $\tau$ and repeatedly
applying it by $p$ and $s$ enumerates $\tau\langle p,s\rangle$, without an
inconsistent forward-composition convention. The package does not infer the
all-$n$ generation property from four BFS tests. Its certificate
(\path{data/04-deficit-asymptotics-finite_certificate.json}) is ordinary JSON
with exact decimal integers and no executable code: for each $n$ the producer
emits the two easy bounds and the records $(m,d,r,P,O,P-O)$ and
$(a,b,d,r,P,O,P-O)$ for all parameters of \cref{evo:da:prop:finitebound}; the
independent checker reconstructs these records, compares the entire sorted
record sets, not merely their minima, independently optimizes the
coprime-split expression, and raises an exception with a nonzero exit on any
mismatch. A future third verifier can ignore the producer, recompute
\eqref{evo:da:eq:ordersdp}, evaluate the displayed formulas and compare its
own minima. The claims need only the range through~$31$; expanding the
exploratory range does not replace the infinite proof.

\subsection{Commands and files}\label{evo:sec:commands}

The shipped programs are byte-identical to the delivery and still use the
delivered layout: each computes a package root as the parent of its own
directory and reads or writes \texttt{results/}\ldots\ there, and
\path{code/05-quartic-threshold-supplementary_checks.py} imports
\texttt{theorem\_checks}, which does not exist under its shipped name. Run in
this report's directory they would therefore write stray files into Part~I's
\texttt{results/}, manuscripts~01 and~03 would overwrite each other's
\texttt{results/verification\_summary.json}, the supplementary program would
stop at its import, and the two checkers of manuscripts~01 and~03 would fail
unless their producers had run there first. The report's README gives a recipe that restores the delivered names in
a scratch copy; there the commands are
\begin{lstlisting}
python code/theorem_checks.py        # manuscript 06
python code/independent_check.py
python code/supplementary_checks.py
python code/verify.py                # manuscript 01
python code/check_certificates.py
python code/verify.py                # manuscript 03
python code/independent_check.py
\end{lstlisting}
each group in its own copy. The write phase ran them so on 29 September 2026
(Python~3.14.4; about 30\,s, 5\,s and 1\,s): all passed, every CSV is
byte-identical to the shipped one, and every JSON, \TeX\ and console record
equals the shipped one apart from Windows line endings, except that
manuscript~01's \texttt{verification\_summary.json} (and its identical
console copy) records the Python version.

\subsection{What the checks do not establish}

No finite enumeration proves the universal quantifiers of
\cref{evo:thm:main,evo:et:thm:main,evo:da:thm:main3}. They are supplied by
the analytic arguments; the finite checks are exact integer comparisons of
explicitly reduced obligations, whose sufficiency is proved in the text
(\cref{evo:sec:finite,evo:et:prop:finite,evo:da:prop:finitebound}). No Lean,
Rocq, Isabelle or other proof-assistant verification was carried out for
Part~III, and none of its statements is formalized anywhere in ProveIt. The
published lower theorem is cited, not formally reconstructed. Agreement
between two programs written in one investigation is implementation
independence, not logical independence of the shared reduction, and not
external review. Successful compilation of a PDF is not mathematical
verification.

\section{Further research questions}\label{evo:sec:questions}

The three manuscripts close with ten, ten and nine questions. Where two or
three of them ask the same question it is printed once, with every source's
comments; questions that another source (or Part~II) has since answered are
printed with the answer, not as open. Part~II's questions in
\cref{tor:sec:questions} stay as printed there, with dated pointers where
Part~III bears on them. Manuscript~01 calls its questions proposed directions
arising from the argument, not assertions that each is a previously
published open problem; manuscript~03 calls its questions proposals, not
hidden assumptions in its proofs; manuscript~06 says they separate exact
optimization, structural classification, algorithms, and formalization.

\begin{question}[The complete two-parameter optimum; manuscripts~06, 01 and~03]\label{evo:q:complete}
Does $R_2(n,k)=k^n-\Bip_k(a_n,b_n)+G_k(a_n,b_n)$ hold for every $n\ge7$ and
$3\le k<n$?
\end{question}
This is Davies's Problem~2 in full, and Part~II's \cref{tor:q:four} for
$k\ge4$. \emph{Status after Part~III:} settled for $k=3$ (Part~II), for
$4\le k\le7$ (\cref{evo:thm:smallk}), for $k\ge8$ once $n\ge N(k)$
(\cref{evo:thm:eventual}), and for $8\le k\le12$, $n\le100$
(\cref{evo:et:prop:finite}) and every $k<n\le30$ (\cref{prop:finite}). The
remaining region is $k\ge8$ with $\max(7,k+1)\le n<N(k)$ outside those finite
ranges. Manuscript~06: a direct target is to replace the palette estimates by
a global comparison of every disconnected or isolated biclique graph with the
nearest full coprime biclique, including the period correction; finite
verification can settle each additional fixed output count, but it cannot by
itself close the unbounded two-parameter region. Manuscript~01 (writing
before manuscript~06): a concrete route is to replace the limiting palette
comparisons in \cref{evo:et:prop:separation} by finite inequalities strong
enough to cover every admissible collision graph; the certificate bound
already provides an exact target for a successful inequality, and small
exceptional graph types should be treated separately rather than concealed in
an asymptotic error. Manuscript~03 asked the same (``is Davies's optimized
coprime-split lower bound exact for every $n\ge7$ in its stated range?'').

\begin{question}[An explicit, and a substantially sharper, threshold; manuscripts~06 and~01]\label{evo:q:threshold}
Can one give an explicit, reasonably small upper bound for the least
threshold, and how does it grow with $k$ (manuscript~01)? Can one prove an
effective linear or near-linear condition in $k$, rather than the sufficient
condition $n\ge3k^4$ (manuscript~06)?
\end{question}
Manuscript~01's first part is answered by manuscript~06: $N(k)\le3k^4$ is
explicit (\cref{evo:cor:quartic}); in manuscript~01's words, even a very
large explicit bound turns the theorem into a finite verification program for
any fixed $k$, which is what \cref{evo:sec:finite} carries out for $k\le7$.
Manuscript~01 had noted that the union bound \eqref{evo:et:eq:ontoestimate}
and the elementary order bound are both quantitative, and that the missing
work was a usable uniform separation between the finite palette terms, with a
controlled treatment of components whose smaller part has not reached the
asymptotic regime. The sharper form stays open. [Added 29 September 2026,
batch~43: at $k=4$ Part~IV lowers the analytic threshold from $N(4)=212$ to
$26$ (\cref{fo:rem:threshold}); for general $k$ the question stays open.]
Manuscript~06: the current
losses have identifiable sources, namely the crude multinomial remainder
$k^23^k\mu^n$, the conversion of many small graph components into a factor
$2^{d+r}$, and the elementary permutation-order bound; sharper palette tails
and a direct comparison preserving the component sizes could improve the
threshold without changing the collision method. The case $k$ comparable to
$n$ is especially important, since fixed-$k$ asymptotics are least
informative there.

\begin{question}[Explicit cutoffs for odd outputs; manuscript~03. Answered]\label{evo:q:oddcutoff}
Give an explicit computable bound for $N_\exists(k)$ in
\cref{evo:da:thm:odd}, and determine the smallest cutoff for $k=5$.
\end{question}
Answered by manuscript~06: $N(k)$ of \eqref{evo:eq:Ndef} is such a bound for
every $k\ge4$, and for $k=5$ the formula holds for every $n\ge7$
(\cref{evo:thm:smallk}), so no cutoff beyond Davies's range $n\ge7$ is
needed. Manuscript~03 had observed that its proof already provides exponential
upper errors, a uniform period error, and finitely many strict coefficient
gaps, that turning the compactness reduction into inequalities with numerical
thresholds should produce a rigorous algorithm, and that the exclusion of
small reduced parts must be bounded as well as the asymptotic palette errors;
manuscript~06's \cref{evo:lem:badcross} does exactly that.

\begin{question}[Exact even-output optimality; manuscript~03. Answered eventually]\label{evo:q:evenexact}
For fixed even $k\ge4$, is $R_2(n,k)=k^n-\Bip_k(a_n,b_n)+a_nb_n$ true for
every sufficiently large $n$?
\end{question}
Answered: yes, by manuscript~01 (\cref{evo:et:thm:main}, non-effective) and by
manuscript~06 (\cref{evo:thm:eventual}, for $n\ge N(k)$). Manuscript~03's
stronger form (every $n\ge7$) is \cref{evo:q:complete}. Manuscript~03 had
noted that its argument forces an optimal permutation into two coprime cycles
but not their nearest balance for even $k$, and suggested analyzing the
second-largest terms in \eqref{evo:eq:signedpalette}, since first-order
asymptotics cannot discriminate the interval of \cref{evo:da:thm:plateau};
manuscripts~01 and~06 instead use exact strict balancing.

\begin{question}[Second-order asymptotics for even outputs; manuscript~03. Answered]\label{evo:q:evensecond}
After subtracting $\binom{2h}{h}h^n$, what are the next exponential scale and
the exact residue-dependent coefficient of $D_{2h}(n)$?
\end{question}
Answered by manuscript~06's \cref{evo:thm:evenasymptotic}: the next scale is
$(h^2-1)^{n/2}$ with coefficient
$2C_1\cosh(\delta_n\log\frac{h+1}{h-1})$, $C_1=(2h)!/((h-1)!(h+1)!)$.
Manuscript~03 had observed that for a prescribed near-balanced split the next
palette products are $h^2-1$, but that an extremal theorem must also show
that no different coprime split or singular generator improves the total by
changing the relative weights of those terms; exactness
(\cref{evo:thm:eventual}) supplies this.

\begin{question}[Rank and kernel structure of an extremal singular letter; manuscript~01. Answered]\label{evo:q:rank}
Must an eventual extremizer's singular generator have rank $n-1$? Can an
extremizer have two or more disjoint cross-cycle kernel pairs?
\end{question}
Answered: rank $n-1$, one cross pair, at $k=3$ for every $n\ge7$ (Part~II's
\cref{tor:thm:rigidity}) and at $k\ge4$ for every $(n,k)\in\mathcal E$
(\cref{evo:thm:rigidity}), hence for every fixed $k\ge3$ eventually.
Manuscript~01 had noted that its \cref{evo:et:thm:extremizers} only rules out
within-cycle pairs and kernel classes of size three, and that resolving the
question requires exploiting full improper saturation rather than reusing a
single-pair chromatic obstruction; both rigidity proofs do exactly that.

\begin{question}[Which rank-$(n-1)$ singular letters saturate the bound? Manuscripts~06, 03 and~01]\label{evo:q:classify}
For the forced two-cycle permutation $p$, characterize those singular maps $s$
for which some proper full-period coloring reaches every improper coloring
(manuscript~06); at $k=3$, characterize the singular $s$ that attain
$R_2(n,3)$ with a permissible output coloring (manuscript~03, Part~II's
\cref{tor:q:singular}). Are all eventual extremal pairs, up to relabeling and
generator changes, associated with the monoids $U_{a_n,b_n}$, or do smaller
monoids have the same projected coloring orbit (manuscript~01)?
\end{question}
Manuscript~06: \cref{evo:thm:rigidity} gives necessary structure, but
\cref{evo:ex:nonsaturation} rules out sufficiency of cycle shape, rank, and
output period. A useful intermediate goal is a classification in terms of the
image of $s$, the location of the omitted point, and how $s$ moves the two
cycles. The published lower monoids provide positive examples, not a proof
that every extremizer has the same transition monoid. Manuscript~03:
\cref{evo:da:thm:rigid3} reduces the problem to maps injective on each block
and to the reachability of every improper coloring; a useful answer would
replace that reachability condition by conditions on the cross-block
matching, image omissions, and the permutation action, and need not require
that $\langle p,s\rangle$ equal the entire standard witness monoid.
Manuscript~01: the theorem identifies necessary cycle geometry and a
necessary and sufficient orbit condition, not a normal form for the
semigroup; different transformations can induce the same output coloring, so
the projection may erase monoid-level differences.

\begin{question}[Recognition without enumerating $k^n$ colorings; manuscripts~06 and~01]\label{evo:q:recognize}
Can saturation of a two-cycle candidate be recognized in time polynomial in
$n$ and the bit length of its description (manuscript~06)? Can the saturation
condition of \cref{evo:et:thm:extremizers} be replaced by an efficiently
checkable algebraic criterion (manuscript~01)?
\end{question}
This is Part~II's \cref{tor:q:recognize} for every $k$. Manuscript~06: a
breadth-first search in the coloring space is exponential. The rigidity
theorem reduces the input shape, but not automatically the decision
complexity. A useful certificate would describe generation of all improper
colorings by finitely many local transformations. Conversely, a missing
coloring might admit a short invariant-based certificate like the
invariant-block obstruction in \cref{evo:ex:nonsaturation}.

\begin{question}[The second-largest value; manuscript~06]\label{evo:q:second}
What is the largest reverse complexity strictly below $R_2(n,k)$, and which
structural defect produces it?
\end{question}
This is Part~II's \cref{tor:q:second} for $k\ge4$. The nonfull collision graph
and the multiple-kernel-pair obstruction produce different quantitative
losses (\cref{evo:cor:nonfullgap,evo:prop:firstmatching}), but neither proves
that its bound is attained. A complete answer must compare a wrong cycle
split, extra kernel pairs, a shortened pure orbit, and incomplete coverage of
the improper family even when the coarse structure is correct.

\begin{question}[Stability of nearly extremal automata; manuscripts~01 and~03]\label{evo:q:stability}
If an orbit misses at most $(1+\varepsilon)D_k(n)$ colorings, which cycle
structures, output periods, and incomplete-saturation patterns are possible,
and can exact or asymptotic gap theorems distinguish these mechanisms
(manuscript~01)? Find an explicit positive function $c(k)$ such that every
sufficiently large automaton outside the required two-cycle structure has
deficit at least $(1+c(k))D_k(n)$, and optimize that constant, with the
smallest associated state threshold (manuscript~03).
\end{question}
Manuscript~01: for odd $k$, the hyperbolic-cosine factor already penalizes
imbalance at leading order; for even $k$, subleading palette terms are
essential, and the explicit balancing gap offers a way to quantify that
setting. Manuscript~03: its subsequence arguments imply such a separation for
the excluded structural types at fixed $k$, but do not extract its best value
or the smallest associated state threshold; odd imbalance gives additional
residue-dependent gaps that can be treated separately. \emph{Partial answer
(manuscript~06; the reading is the merge's):} for $n\ge N(k)$ every nonfull
cross-cycle graph costs at least $(\hat C/4-1)\lambda_k^n$ more than
$D^*_k(n)$ (\cref{evo:cor:nonfullgap}), and every same-cycle or unmixed case
has deficit above $(3\hat C/2)\lambda_k^n$ (\cref{evo:sec:eventual}). Since
$D^*_k(n)<\Bip_k(a_n,b_n)<(3\hat C/2)\lambda_k^n$, these give an explicit
$c(k)>0$ for those structural types, for instance $c(k)=(\hat C-4)/(6\hat C)$
for nonfull graphs. The wrong-split, extra-kernel-pair and
incomplete-saturation mechanisms are not quantified in this form (for extra
kernel pairs see \cref{evo:prop:firstmatching}). [Added 29 September 2026,
batch~43: at $k=4$ Part~IV quantifies the wrong-split and extra-kernel-pair
mechanisms (\cref{fo:as:thm:stability,fo:fo:thm:stability}); incomplete
saturation stays unquantified.]

\begin{question}[Sharpness of the matching hierarchy; manuscript~06]\label{evo:q:matching}
For fixed $a,b,k,t$, when can the bound $k^{a+b}-W_{k,t}(a,b)+ab$ be attained?
\end{question}
The matching statistic is necessary because every singular word carries a
rotated kernel matching. It forgets the relative displacement pattern of that
matching and all later constraints imposed by the word. Refining $W_{k,t}$ by
displacement type may reveal additional obstructions, while constructing large
orbits from prescribed matchings would test how much information the
statistic loses.

\begin{question}[The attainable near-maximum spectrum; manuscripts~06 and~03]\label{evo:q:spectrum}
Which integers immediately below the optimum occur as reverse state
complexities, and which are absent (manuscript~06)? Which orbit sizes in a
neighborhood of $R_2(n,3)$ are attainable, and does the rigidity theorem imply
gaps in the spectrum immediately below the maximum (manuscript~03)?
\end{question}
This is Part~II's \cref{tor:q:spectrum}. Manuscript~06: a family of singular
maps with controlled defects could connect the matching hierarchy with the
spectrum of attainable orbit sizes. This is not determined by knowing the
maximum: the orbit cannot generally be modified by adding or deleting one
coloring independently of the others. Manuscript~03: a perturbation of the
larger cycle's output word changes its period and can lose a polynomial number
of proper orbit states; a change in collision graph type loses exponentially
many. Understanding the intervening scales could connect extremal structure
to the DFAO magic-number question.

\begin{question}[The diagonal and near-diagonal regimes; manuscripts~06 and~01]\label{evo:q:diagonal}
What is the exact binary reversal maximum when every original state has a
distinct output, $k=n$ (manuscript~06)? How does the extremal defect behave
for $k=n$ or $k=n-c$ with $c$ fixed, and can output projection expose a
different optimal split than monoid-size maximization in these regimes
(manuscript~01)?
\end{question}
Manuscript~06: the lower witness assumes $k<n$, so the exactness theorem does
not apply at the boundary. The problem then relates directly to orders of
two-generated transformation monoids, a classical direction associated with
Holzer and K\"onig \cite{HolzerKoenig2004}. It requires a separate
attainability analysis, not a substitution of $k=n$ into the formula.
Manuscript~01: the witness uses a redundant output in the larger cycle; at
$k=n$ that resource disappears completely, and at fixed $n-k$ it is scarce.
This is a structural change, not merely a limiting value of the fixed-$k$
formulas. (Part~I's \cref{sec:finite} shows the collision bound already has a
gap at $(n,k)=(5,5)$.)

\begin{question}[Growing output alphabets; manuscripts~01 and~03]\label{evo:q:growing}
For which functions $k=k(n)\to\infty$ is the closest-coprime two-cycle
construction eventually optimal? Is there a transition when $k$ is a positive
proportion of $n$ (manuscript~01)? For which growth rates do the fixed-output
formulas retain a uniform version, and where does the transition toward the
diagonal monoid-size regime occur (manuscript~03)?
\end{question}
This is Part~II's \cref{tor:q:growing}. \emph{Partial answer
(manuscript~06):} whenever $4\le k\le(n/3)^{1/4}$ the construction is exactly
optimal (\cref{evo:cor:quartic}), and the extremizer structure of
\cref{evo:thm:rigidity} persists, and the same holds whenever $n\ge N(k)$;
for $k\ge8$ the range $n<N(k)$, in particular $k$ a positive proportion of
$n$, is open outside the certified finite ranges, and manuscript~06's
asymptotic expansions are fixed-$k$ statements. Manuscript~01: the fixed-$k$
proof does not answer this by substitution; onto-map errors, binomial palette
multiplicities, and the number of possible palette sizes all change with $k$,
and a uniform occupancy estimate is a natural replacement for the
fixed-alphabet palette lemma. Manuscript~03: the number of palette terms, the
onto-map approximation, and the strict gap \eqref{evo:da:eq:oddgap} all depend
on $k$; treating these dependencies explicitly is necessary.

\begin{question}[Minimal recurrences and closed numerators; manuscript~01]\label{evo:q:recurrence}
What is the minimal recurrence for $D_k(4t+r)$ for general fixed $k$? Which
bases in $\mathcal P_k$ cancel, and can the exact rational generating
function, including its finite initial correction, be computed?
\end{question}
The annihilator of \cref{evo:et:thm:recurrence} is explicit but can contain
unnecessary factors. Determining cancellations is a finite symbolic problem
once the exact threshold question is separated from the tail formula. (For
$3\le k\le7$ the values are now known from $n=\max(7,k+1)$ on, by Part~II and
\cref{evo:thm:smallk}, so for those $k$ only the minimality question
remains; at $k=3$ Part~II's \cref{tor:cor:recurrence} gives the denominator
$(1-4z^4)(1-z^4)^2$.) [Added 29 September 2026, batch~43: answered at $k=4$
by Part~IV: the order-$15$ recurrence of \cref{fo:as:thm:recurrence} is minimal
in $n$, and the annihilator of \cref{evo:et:thm:recurrence} is minimal on each
residue class (\cref{fo:rem:residueminimal}).]

\begin{question}[Formal verification of the proof chain; manuscripts~06, 01 and~03]\label{evo:q:formal}
Can the graph reduction, strict balancing, effective estimates, finite
closure, structural separation and witness saturation be formalized in the
repository's Lean development (or Rocq), with a transparent statement of the
imported lower-construction theorem?
\end{question}
This is Part~II's \cref{tor:q:formal} for $k\ge4$. Manuscript~06: the proof
breaks naturally into finite graph colorings, Stirling-number inequalities,
real-power estimates, and exact integer arithmetic; an especially useful
first target is a small verified checker for
\eqref{evo:eq:finiteperm}--\eqref{evo:eq:finitesame}, together with a proved
compression lemma; such a checker should report its imported axioms and its
evaluation trust boundary, and replaying a large table without proving that
the table covers every graph type would not formalize the theorem.
Manuscript~01: the most direct first milestone is a checked
finite-certificate theorem, proving \cref{evo:et:prop:finite} once and
certifying the finite table by a small verified evaluator; the analytic tail
can then be added independently, with no reason to delay finite checking
until every asymptotic lemma has been formalized. Manuscript~03: formalize
\cref{evo:da:prop:finitebound}, the order recurrence \eqref{evo:da:eq:ordersdp}
and the certificate evaluation for $7\le n\le31$; an attractive division is to
formalize the symbolic graph classification and let a verified integer
evaluator discharge the $3{,}717$ inequalities; the real-power estimates of
the analytic tail can be replaced by suitably powered rational comparisons;
and the lower construction should retain an explicit dependency declaration
until its own generation theorem is formalized.

\begin{question}[Beyond one cyclic permutation action; manuscripts~06, 01 and~03]\label{evo:q:groups}
Which parts of the argument survive when the available permutation
transitions generate a noncyclic group, or when there are several singular
letters (manuscript~06)? What exact defect laws arise when permutation letters
generate a prescribed abelian or nonabelian group, together with one singular
letter, and can orbital chromatic polynomials identify a sharp threshold
between near-maximal and full reversal (manuscript~01)? Extend the deficit
method when the alphabet contains one permutation and several singular
letters, or when all invertible letters generate a fixed abelian group
(manuscript~03).
\end{question}
This is Part~II's \cref{tor:q:groups}. Manuscript~06: the
rightmost-singular-letter argument still produces orbit graphs, but those
graphs need not be cycles or unions of bicliques; a replacement for the
classification would connect group actions, proper-coloring counts, and the
number of colorings supplied by the permutation subgroup; the cyclic
classification is precisely what makes the present binary problem tractable.
Manuscript~01: the single cyclic group is unusually rigid (one pair orbit is a
union of cycles or complete bipartite graphs); more general groups introduce
orbital graphs whose chromatic behavior may not be controlled by palette
splits; this is a natural extension of Part~I's abelian-permutation
obstruction (\cref{sec:abelian}), but its exact asymptotics require new graph
input. Manuscript~03: the forbidden proper colorings are then governed by a
union of edge orbits; an exact graph-class description may lead to new
alphabet-versus-output thresholds, and it is not enough merely to count the
colors needed for one selected collision graph.

\section{Conclusion of Part~III}\label{evo:sec:conclusion}

\emph{Manuscript~06.} The collision-orbit reduction, strict balancing and
explicit palette estimates determine $R_2(n,k)$ exactly for every $k\ge4$ once
$n\ge N(k)\le3k^4$, uniformly enough to let $k$ grow like $n^{1/4}$, and a
finite exact-integer closure extends this to every $n\ge\max(7,k+1)$ for
$4\le k\le7$. Equality forces one permutation with the two nearest coprime
cycles and a singular letter of rank $n-1$; a matching hierarchy prices
additional kernel pairs; and the optimal deficit has one leading constant
for even $k$ and three for odd $k$. The proved results narrow the problem
rather than eliminate every remaining direction.

\emph{Manuscript~01.} The collision-orbit method does more than guarantee that
a binary reversal omits some states: with fixed output alphabet size, it
identifies the exact eventual maximum. The only competitive permutation
geometry is two coprime cycles; strict balancing selects the closest such
pair; and the published monoid construction realizes every coloring
permitted by the resulting sharp bound. The final defect has exponential base
$\sqrt{\lfloor k^2/4\rfloor}$ and its residue subsequences satisfy explicit
eventual linear recurrences. Three points deserve emphasis: the cyclic
permutation order is negligible compared with the defect scale only because
$k$ is fixed and $\lambda_k>1$; the strict balancing theorem works at the
exact integer level and resolves ties that the even-output leading term
cannot see; and a single isolated vertex is not automatically negligible, the
positive margin \eqref{evo:et:eq:smallmargin} being what excludes that
possible competitor. The proof also uses the witness's saturation of every
improper coloring: a low chromatic count alone is only an upper bound on an
arbitrary orbit, and it becomes an exact construction because the imported
monoid theorem and the representative-selection argument prove
reachability. The remaining gap, as manuscript~01 stated it, was the uniform
threshold seven and growing output alphabets; manuscript~06 has since
narrowed both.

\emph{Manuscript~03.} The decisive object for binary reversal is not the
entire transformation monoid but the colorings excluded by a collision orbit.
With three outputs, properness forces a monochromatic block and sharply
restricts the residual cyclic orbit, which turns the repository's finite
structural evidence into an exact formula for every $n\ge7$ (Part~II's
theorem, proved a second way). For arbitrary fixed output size, balanced
disjoint palettes determine the exponential deficit scale, and uniformly
controlling the cyclic correction makes this local coloring calculation a
global extremal statement. Odd output size supplies a strict imbalance
penalty, yielding eventual exact optimality; even output size lacks that
penalty at first order and admits a nontrivial interval of asymptotically
optimal proportions. The distinction explains the specific obstruction that
remained for exact even-output optimality, which manuscripts~01 and~06 remove
by exact balancing.

\section{The finite verification kernel and threshold data}\label{evo:sec:kernel}

This section prints manuscript~06's two appendices. The following pseudocode
expresses the primary finite comparison, after precomputing chromatic values
and residual-order bounds. The implementation adds typed interfaces, exact
cube roots, exception handling, per-row margins, and output records.

\begin{lstlisting}
for n in range(max(7, k + 1), N(k)):
    a, b = nearest_coprime_split(n)
    D = P(k, a, b) - a*b
    check(k**n - k**(n-1) > D)
    check((k-1)**2 * k**(n-2) - 1 > D)

    for component_size in range(2, n + 1):
        u, v = nearest_coprime_split(component_size)
        for d in range(1, n // component_size + 1):
            r = n - d*component_size
            gap = P(k, u, v)**d * k**r - d*u*v*g[r]
            if d == 1 and r == 0:
                check(gap == D)
            else:
                check(gap > D)

    for m in range(2, n + 1):
        r = n - m
        for ell in divisors(m):
            if ell >= 2:
                cycle = (k-1)**ell + (-1)**ell * (k-1)
                gap = cycle**(m//ell) * k**r - m*g[r]
                check(gap > D)
\end{lstlisting}
(Here \texttt{P} is $\Bip_k$ and \texttt{g[r]} is $\gamma_r$.) The three
nontrivial coverage facts are worth restating. First, every mixed pair yields
a graph in the classification after choosing a collision pair. Second, fixing
the component total and multiplicity permits the nearest-coprime compression
because the coloring term and the period term are optimized in the same
direction. Third, every same-cycle displacement has a component length
dividing its active cycle length. These facts, not just the loop bounds, turn
the finite calculation into a universal bound.

The next table records further effective thresholds. It does not claim finite
closure below them. In particular, a small value of $N(k)$ relative to $3k^4$
is only an improvement of a sufficient bound.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}rrrrrr@{}}
