\toprule
$k$ & $\hat H$ & $\hat C$ & $\hat L$ & $\hat S$ & $N(k)$\\
\midrule
8 & 16 & 70 & 8 & 335 & 2,688\\
9 & 20 & 252 & 9 & 206 & 1,863\\
10 & 25 & 252 & 9 & 595 & 5,364\\
11 & 30 & 924 & 11 & 352 & 3,883\\
12 & 36 & 924 & 11 & 952 & 10,483\\
13 & 42 & 3,432 & 13 & 548 & 7,137\\
14 & 49 & 3,432 & 13 & 1,416 & 18,421\\
15 & 56 & 12,870 & 15 & 799 & 12,000\\
16 & 64 & 12,870 & 15 & 1,999 & 30,000\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Threshold data beyond the closed slices (manuscript~06; the
remaining constants are in
\texttt{data/05-quartic-threshold-thresholds.json}).}
\label{evo:tab:thresholds}
\end{table}

The difference between odd and even thresholds reflects the gap from the
largest to the next palette product: $\hat H-1$ for even $k$ and $\hat H-2$
for odd $k$. These values are bounds from a deliberately uniform argument, not
measurements of when the conjectured formula first becomes true.

\subsection{Primary published sources}

All three inspected Davies's arXiv version~2 of 17 October 2017
\cite{Davies2017} in HTML and PDF. Manuscript~06: the coloring-orbit
formulation (Proposition~4), Theorem~3, Corollary~3, the $k\ge4$ period
contribution, the output-map existence lemma, and the concluding optimality
question (Problem~2); printed pages~12 and~17 were inspected as page images.
The published statement supplies an accessible (trim) witness; manuscript~06
verifies original-state minimality separately. Manuscript~01: Proposition~4;
Section~3's definition of $U_{a,b}$, its two-generation attribution and the
explicit generator; the PDF page with the concluding open problems (printed
page~17), where Problem~2 asks about the exact binary lower bound for
$n\ge7$. Manuscript~03: Proposition~4 (printed page~6), the $U_{a,b}$
definition, two-generation attribution and generators (page~7), Theorem~3
(pages~9--12), Corollary~3 (page~12), and Section~5, Question~2 (page~17);
the image of page~17 was inspected to check the original question and to
distinguish bold exhaustive values from the paper's other numerical entries,
and no numerical entry of that table is treated as a proof.

Holzer and K\"onig \cite{HolzerKoenig2004}: manuscript~06 checked the
bibliographic record and treats the paper as background for the $k=n$
monoid connection, citing the lower-bound dependency through Davies.
Manuscript~01 imports its Theorem~8 (two-generation of $U_{a,b}$) in the form
quoted by Davies; the entire original 2004 proof was not independently
reconstructed. Manuscript~03 checked the publisher metadata and abstract; the
Theorem~8 attribution and its applicability were checked in Davies's paper,
the full proof was not reproduced, and the much stronger extremal-monoid
assertions are not imported. Krawetz's thesis \cite{Krawetz2003} is cited by
manuscript~01 for the attribution of the generator through Davies's account,
not as a claim that the thesis was read in full.

\subsection{Literature-search scope}

Manuscript~06 searched the exact Davies title and combinations of DFAO,
reversal, optimality, three and four outputs, and recent-year terms; broad
acronym searches returned many unrelated results, which were not used as
mathematical evidence. Manuscript~01 searched binary DFAO reversal, the exact
lower bound, closest coprime cycles, and eventual or asymptotic optimality.
Manuscript~03 searched ``DFAO'' ``reversal'' with ``optimal'',
``asymptotic'', ``2025''; the exact Davies title, also with 2025 and 2026; and
the exact Holzer--K\"onig title. No later solution was located by any of
them. None is an exhaustive citation-index review, a survey of theses or
unpublished work, or correspondence with specialists; such searches cannot
exclude an unindexed paper, thesis, later variant, or equivalent result under
different terminology. Non-discovery establishes no priority, and neither
absence of search results nor the 2017 paper's open-problem wording
establishes that a problem remained unsolved in later literature. The
manuscripts present their proofs for review, without declaring a verified
first solution; their extension claims are relative to the inspected sources.

\subsection{Dependency ledgers}

\emph{Manuscript~06} (novelty boundary). The collision-graph mechanism, the
orbit formulation, and the published lower construction are antecedents. The
results it develops are the all-$k$ effective eventual comparison, the uniform
quartic threshold, the four finite-slice closures, the corresponding $k\ge4$
extremizer constraints, and the matching and asymptotic consequences.
Standard identities (Stirling recurrences, Rolle's theorem, graph coloring,
the Chinese remainder criterion, the published lower monoids) are proved or
explained, not advertised as new. Its research-status record lists as proved
by conventional arguments: strict balancing for $k\ge3$; nearest-coprime
optimization of the Davies expression for every $k\ge4$, $n\ge7$, $k<n$, with
no asymptotic hypothesis; the matching upper bound for every $k\ge4$ and
$n\ge N(k)$; exactness there with the published lower construction; the
necessary extremizer structure, including rank $n-1$; the matching hierarchy
for arbitrary coprime cycle sizes and cross-pair kernels; the first matching
correction; and the even/odd asymptotics. The finite inequalities below $N(k)$
for $k=4..7$ are computer-assisted and extend both the exact optimum and the
rigidity to every $n\ge\max(7,k+1)$ in these slices.

\emph{Manuscript~01.}
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright}p{.36\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{.57\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Ingredient or conclusion & Provenance and role\\
\midrule
Reversal as a coloring orbit & Davies; rederived to fix composition and
accessibility conventions.\\[3pt]
Two-generator monoids $U_{a,b}$ & Published Holzer--K\"onig theorem; explicit
generator recorded by Davies from Krawetz. Imported dependency.\\[3pt]
Collision-orbit graphs and chromatic bounds & Inherited from Part~I; their
proofs and graph classification are supplied.\\[3pt]
Strict balancing with an explicit positive gap & Derived and proved, with an
elementary proof of the required Stirling positivity.\\[3pt]
Exclusion of all non-two-cycle competitors for fixed $k$ & Derived and
proved; the odd-output, one-isolated-vertex case is checked explicitly.\\[3pt]
Eventual exact optimum and extremizer criterion & Conclusions of the new
argument; no explicit threshold and no assertion for growing $k$.\\[3pt]
Parity-sensitive constants and recurrence laws & Consequences derived from
the exact eventual formula.\\[3pt]
The $919$ finite equalities & Exact integer certificates, checked by two
implementations; not kernel-checked proofs.\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The phrase ``derived here'' in manuscript~01 describes its own work; it does
not establish that the statement has never appeared elsewhere. Manuscript~01
names as the most useful independent checks the strict balancing proof, the
one-isolated-vertex inequality for odd $k$
(\eqref{evo:et:eq:smallmargin}), and the use of the imported $U_{a,b}$
generation theorem.

\emph{Manuscript~03} (theorem-by-theorem boundary). (1)~Exact three-output
optimum, all $n\ge7$: analytic upper proof for $n\ge32$, computer-assisted
upper step for $7\le n\le31$, attainment by the imported $U_{a,b}$ witness and
Davies's output construction; two local implementations agree on all $3{,}717$
inequalities. (2)~Necessary structure of every three-output optimizer: follows
from the strict inequalities and the unique minimizing parameters of the
certificate; not a classification of all singular generators. (3)~Sharp
deficit asymptotics for every fixed $k\ge3$: mathematical proof, no
computational range assumption, fixed $k$ essential. (4)~Eventual exact
formula for every fixed odd $k$: strict-separation proof plus the lower
witnesses; no numerical $N_\exists(k)$ for odd $k\ge5$. (5)~Near-optimal
rigidity: necessary structure, not asserted sufficient. (6)~Even-output
interval criterion: an if-and-only-if criterion for first-order optimality
within the $U_{a,b}$ witness family, not an exact even-output optimum. Its
new upper arguments require only finite transformations, elementary graph
coloring, least common multiples, exact integer arithmetic, and fixed-parameter
asymptotic estimates; they do not assume the conjecture that the largest
two-generated monoid has a particular form, which is unnecessary because a
large coloring orbit is not the same as a large monoid.

\subsection{Claims and nonclaims}

The principal new contribution of Part~III is the exact value of $R_2(n,k)$
for $k\ge4$ in the range $\mathcal E$ of \eqref{evo:eq:exactrange}, with the
effective threshold $N(k)\le3k^4$ (manuscript~06), together with the
non-effective eventual routes, finite certificates, recurrences, sharp
asymptotics and the even-$k$ interval criterion of manuscripts~01 and~03. The
following are \emph{not} claimed:
\begin{itemize}
\item Davies's Problem~2 in full: the formula for $k\ge8$ with
 $\max(7,k+1)\le n<N(k)$ outside the certified finite ranges; small $n$
 outside the stated ranges; the boundary $k=n$ (manuscript~06; none of the
 three treats it) and the near-diagonal regime.
\item Any other open question of Davies's Section~5, the
 largest-two-generated-transformation-monoid problem, or a classification
 of all extremal transformation monoids (orbit extremality is weaker than
 monoid-size extremality).
\item A necessary-and-sufficient classification of the singular transitions
 attaining the optimum (manuscripts~06 and~03); \cref{evo:ex:nonsaturation}
 is included to prevent an overstatement of \cref{evo:thm:rigidity}.
\item The second-best attainable value or the near-optimal spectrum
 (manuscript~06).
\item Uniform asymptotic error terms for growing $k$: the main threshold of
 manuscript~06 is uniform in $k$, but every displayed asymptotic expansion,
 and every theorem of manuscripts~01 and~03, is a fixed-$k$ statement; no
 uniform result is given for $k=k(n)\to\infty$ beyond
 $k\le(n/3)^{1/4}$.
\item An exact value or asymptotic estimate for the least threshold: $N(k)$
 is a sufficient threshold, and manuscripts~01 and~03 prove only the
 existence of $N_\exists(k)$ (for odd $k\ge5$ in manuscript~03, with no
 numerical value).
\item Exact even-output optimality from manuscript~03 alone (its plateau
 theorem is first-order and concerns the witness family; exact even-output
 optimality comes from manuscripts~01 and~06), and exact optimality of every
 split in the plateau interval.
\item That a finite interval plus an unspecified infinite tail covers every
 intervening integer (manuscript~01), or that the finite tables and
 breadth-first searches prove any infinite statement.
\item A new proof of Davies's lower construction or of the Holzer--K\"onig
 two-generation theorem; \cref{evo:sec:witness} proves the saturation step
 given generation.
\item Independent refereeing, worldwide or historical priority beyond the
 inspected sources, or machine-checked formalization. None of the new
 material has been checked by a proof assistant or an external referee.
\item No novelty for the orbit reduction, the collision-graph method, the
 lower witness, standard Stirling and palette identities, the small values
 (the $n=8$, $k=5$ value $369020$ and the $k=3$ values are in Part~I, Part~II
 or Davies's experiments), or the finite table beyond extending its range.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{What remains open}

The $k\ge8$ window below $N(k)$; a sharper (near-linear) threshold; exact
values for growing $k$ beyond $n^{1/4}$ and the diagonal $k=n$; a
classification of saturating singular letters and efficient recognition of
saturation; second-best values, stability constants and the near-maximum
spectrum; sharpness of the matching hierarchy; minimal recurrences; formal
verification; noncyclic permutation groups and several singular letters.
\Cref{evo:sec:questions} formulates the corresponding questions. Davies's
Problem~1, the nonattainment question of Part~I, is not affected by Part~III.
[Added 29 September 2026, batch~43: at $k=4$ Part~IV settles the minimal
recurrence and quantifies stability (\cref{fo:sec:audit}).]

\noindent\emph{Part~IV was added on 29 September 2026 in batch~43 of

\section{Provenance, conventions and the statement}\label{fo:sec:intro}

\subsection{Two manuscripts, one addition}\label{fo:sec:source}

Part~IV merges two AI-assisted research manuscripts prepared for Vladimir
Reshetnikov and dated 29 September 2026. They prove the same theorem with the
same architecture and in the same order (coloring orbits, collision reduction,
four-color counts and balancing, the upper bound, a finite certificate,
attainment, rigidity, stability, the missing-orbit inventory, asymptotics and
a recurrence), so they form one part. In this report's local file sequence
(Part~I's unprefixed files count as~01, Part~II's are~02, Part~III's are~03
to~05) they are~06 and~07. Both archives arrived in \texttt{06bcc37a8} and
were placed in \texttt{faef2ed2a}; despite their similar archive names they
are different packages with no file in common.

\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Manuscript~05 of batch~43}, the \emph{base}, called \emph{AS}
(``all states'') below; file prefix \texttt{07-all-state-}. \emph{Exact
Binary Reversal with Four Outputs: An all-state formula, quantitative
rigidity, and the structure of the missing reversal states}, 21 pages as
delivered. Archive
\texttt{ProveIt\_\allowbreak Four\_\allowbreak Output\_\allowbreak Reversal\allowbreak\ (1).zip}
(inner directory \texttt{four\_\allowbreak output\_\allowbreak reversal/}).
Pinned to ProveIt commit \texttt{8315d24e3}, with this report's
\texttt{article.tex} blob \texttt{84923bc5} and the blob \texttt{f93f98bb} of

AS is the base because it has the weaker hypothesis and proves more: its
theorem covers every $n\ge4$ (FO's every $n\ge7$), it proves the recurrence
minimal (FO only proves annihilation), and it counts the extremal output maps
on the standard transition pair. FO keeps, under its own hypotheses, a
second-collision penalty that needs no properness assumption, a stability
theorem at the scale $3^{n/2}$ with an explicit imbalance bound, three exact
residue formulas, a literal C++ breadth-first witness search through $n=12$,
and a regression sweep through $n=200$. ``Manuscript~03'' and
``manuscript~06'' keep their Part~III meanings (batch~40's manuscript~03 and
batch~41's manuscript~06); the two batch-43 sources are always called AS and
FO.

\emph{What the two manuscripts knew.} At both pins this report's
\texttt{article.tex} had blob \texttt{84923bc5}: Parts~I and~II were printed,
Part~III was not (it was written in \texttt{18634ca66}, after both pins),
although its files were already staged. FO read Part~I, Part~II (including
\cref{tor:q:four}, which it answers for $k=4$), the README and
\texttt{code/reversal.py}. AS read Part~I, Part~II, the README and Part~III's
staged \path{03-eventual-SOURCE_AUDIT.md}, which records ``no established
explicit fixed-$k$ threshold''. Neither knew Part~III's explicit threshold
$N(k)$ or its all-$n$ closure of $4\le k\le7$. Both therefore re-derive
Part~III's $k=4$ case; this part credits Part~III wherever that happens.

\begin{resultbox}
\textbf{Status (both manuscripts).} AI-assisted, unrefereed research
manuscripts with complete proposed proofs and reproducible finite
certificates, not refereed or proof-assistant-checked publications. Nothing in
Part~IV is checked in Lean, Rocq or any other proof assistant, no Lean or
Rocq file is supplied, and the repository's formal developments in other
directories confer no formal status on these results. The upper bound is
analytic for $n\ge26$ and computer-assisted below (AS: $2{,}082$ integer
comparisons for $4\le n\le25$; FO: $2{,}059$ for $7\le n\le25$), with the
ordinary execution and integer-arithmetic trust boundary. Attainment for
$n\ge7$ imports the Holzer--K\"onig two-generation theorem in the form stated
by Davies; it is cited, not reproved. The claimed resolution is the
four-output slice of Davies's exact binary reversal problem, not the
variable-output problem or the largest two-generated transformation-monoid
problem. A targeted literature check found no later resolution, but does not
establish priority; publication priority has not been established.
\end{resultbox}

\subsection{Notation of Part~IV}\label{fo:sec:notation}

Parts~I--III's symbols keep their meanings; in particular $\Bip_k(a,b)$,
$(a_n,b_n)$, $\delta_n$, $D_k(n)=k^n-R_2(n,k)$, $D^*_k(n)$ and $\defi$ are
Part~III's (\cref{evo:tab:notation}), $P_G(k)$, $C_L(k)$, $H_r(t)$, $M(n,k)$
and $L(n,k)$ are Part~I's, and $J(r)$ and $P_2(d)$ are Part~II's. The two
manuscripts use Part~II's letters $A,B$ and $D_n$ for four-output objects, and
several letters that Parts~I--III already use. Part~IV therefore fixes the
convention of \cref{fo:tab:notation}, used throughout, including in FO's
material. No normalization changed. As in the manuscripts, the output
alphabet is $\D=\{0,1,2,3\}$ and states are numbered from zero.

Where the manuscripts write $M_n$ for the balanced multinomial coefficient
(FO) it is Part~I's $M(n,4)$; AS's residual-order function $H_r(t)$ is Part~I's
\eqref{eq:H}; both manuscripts' $P_G$ is Part~I's $P_G(4)$. AS's letters $s,t$
for an arbitrary pair and $p,s$ for a permutation and a singular map keep
their meaning, as in Parts~II and~III. A \emph{primitive cyclic word} of length
$m$ is one whose least positive rotation period is $m$.

\subsection{The statement}\label{fo:sec:answer}

Both manuscripts write the split directly: for $n\ge7$, with
$h=\lfloor n/2\rfloor$,
\begin{equation}\label{fo:eq:split}
 (a_n,b_n)=
 \begin{cases}
 (h,h+1),&n=2h+1,\\
 (h-1,h+1),&n=2h,\ h\text{ even},\\
 (h-2,h+2),&n=2h,\ h\text{ odd};
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
this is Part~III's \eqref{evo:eq:split} and Part~II's \eqref{tor:eq:split}.
Then $3\le a_n<b_n$, $a_n+b_n=n$, $\gcd(a_n,b_n)=1$, $b_n\ge4$,
$a_nb_n\ge12$ and $b_n-a_n\le4$. At $k=4$, Part~III's palette formula
\eqref{evo:eq:k4P} is
\begin{equation}\label{fo:eq:B4}
 \Bip_4(a,b)=6\cdot2^{a+b}+4(3^a+3^b)-12(2^a+2^b)+12\qquad(a,b\ge1),
\end{equation}
and $D^*_4(n)=\Bip_4(a_n,b_n)-a_nb_n$ for $n\ge7$.

AS defines $R_2(n,4)$ as the maximum reversal complexity of a minimal
$n$-state DFAO with at most two input letters and four used output values,
FO as the maximum over minimal $n$-state binary DFAOs with four output
symbols. These are the readings of $R_2$ discussed in Part~III's
\cref{evo:sec:answer}; both manuscripts prove the upper bound for the
unrestricted maximum of $|\tau\langle s,t\rangle|$ over all transformation
pairs and output maps, without accessibility, minimality or surjectivity, and
witnesses that are accessible, minimal and use all four outputs.

\begin{theorem}[Four outputs at every $n\ge4$; Part~III and Part~I, second route by AS and FO]\label{fo:thm:main}
For every $n\ge4$, all maps $s,t:Q\to Q$ and every $\tau:Q\to\D$,
\begin{equation}\label{fo:eq:upper}
 |\tau\langle s,t\rangle|\le
 \begin{cases}
 176,&n=4,\\
 826,&n=5,\\
 3526,&n=6,\\
 4^n-D^*_4(n),&n\ge7.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
For each $n\ge4$ equality is attained by an accessible, minimal $n$-state
DFAO using two input letters and all four output values. Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{fo:eq:main}
 R_2(n,4)=4^n-6\cdot2^n-4(3^{a_n}+3^{b_n})+12(2^{a_n}+2^{b_n})-12+a_nb_n
 \qquad(n\ge7),
\end{equation}
and $R_2(4,4)=176$, $R_2(5,4)=826$, $R_2(6,4)=3526$; equivalently
$D_4(n)=80,198,570$ for $n=4,5,6$ and $D_4(n)=D^*_4(n)$ for $n\ge7$.
\end{theorem}

\emph{Status of the statement.} For $n\ge7$ this is Part~III's
\cref{evo:thm:main} at $k=4$ (analytic for $n\ge N(4)=212$,
computer-assisted for $7\le n\le211$ in \cref{evo:thm:smallk}), with the
closed form of \cref{evo:ex:k4formula}. For $n=4,5,6$ the three values are
Part~I's: its table of small cases in \cref{sec:finite} records equality of
the structural upper bound and an explicit witness at $176$, $826$ and
$3526$, with the witnesses of \cref{app:witnesses}; the value $176$ is also
among Davies's previously verified maxima (\cref{sec:instances}). AS's
witnesses for $n=4,5,6$ (\cref{fo:sec:attainment}) are exactly Part~I's arrays.
What AS and FO add to the theorem is its proof: a second, self-contained route
whose analytic part starts at $n=26$ and whose finite part is a certificate of
about two thousand integer comparisons, printed in
\cref{fo:sec:reduction,fo:sec:tail,fo:sec:finite,fo:sec:attainment}. AS's
contribution at $n=4,5,6$ is the uniform certificate, not the numbers.

\section{The reduction at four outputs}\label{fo:sec:reduction}

\subsection{What Part~IV takes from Parts~I--III}\label{fo:sec:imported}

Both manuscripts reprove the orbit reduction and the collision machinery,
with Part~I's composition convention ($(u\circ v)(q)=u(v(q))$, letters act on
colorings by $c\mapsto c\circ t$, and the monoid contains the identity).
These statements are printed once, in Part~I, and recalled with credit.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Orbit reduction.} For an accessible DFAO with transitions $s,t$,
 initial state $q_0$ and output $\tau$, the reversal has
 $|\tau\langle s,t\rangle|$ states (\cref{prop:orbit}; Davies's
 Propositions~3--4). AS (Proposition~2.1) and FO (Proposition~2.1) reprove
 it by the same argument, to fix the composition convention. FO notes that
 only the lower witness uses accessibility, and calls a map $Q\to\D$ a
 coloring without requiring that all four colors occur.
\item \emph{Collision obstruction.} For a permutation $p$, a singular $s$ and
 a collision pair $\{x,y\}$ of $s$, let $G=G(p;x,y)$ have edge set
 $\{\{p^j(x),p^j(y)\}:j\in\Z\}$. Writing a word with its rightmost
 occurrence of $s$ as $u\circ s\circ p^j$ shows that it identifies
 $p^{-j}(x),p^{-j}(y)$, the endpoints of an edge of $G$, so only pure powers
 of $p$ can reach a proper coloring (\cref{lem:rightmost}). Since $p$ is an
 automorphism of $G$, its pure orbit on $\tau$ is either wholly proper or
 wholly improper, and
 \begin{equation}\label{fo:eq:sharpgraph}
 |\tau\langle p,s\rangle|\le4^n-P_G(4)+
 \begin{cases}|\tau\langle p\rangle|,&\tau\text{ proper for }G,\\
 0,&\tau\text{ improper for }G,\end{cases}
 \end{equation}
 with $|\tau\langle p\rangle|\le\ord(p)$ (\cref{prop:chromatic}; AS's
 Lemma~2.2, FO's Lemma~3.2). AS stresses that the lemma does \emph{not}
 assert that every improper coloring is reachable; that is needed for an
 equality witness and is proved separately (\cref{fo:sec:attainment}).
 FO's proof, like AS's, uses the \emph{rightmost} occurrence; FO's audit
 notes that this direction of composition is essential and that the
 collapsed edge uses $p^{-j}$, which is legitimate because $G$ contains all
 translates.
\item \emph{Classification.} A same-cycle pair on an $m$-cycle with
 displacement $e$ gives $d=\gcd(m,e)$ cycles of length $L=m/d\ge2$ (a
 $2$-cycle is one edge) and $r=n-m$ isolates,
 $P_G(4)=C_L(4)^d4^r$ with $C_L(4)=3^L+(-1)^L3$ (\eqref{eq:samecount},
 \eqref{eq:cyclepoly}); a cross pair on cycles of lengths $a,b$ gives
 $d=\gcd(a,b)$ copies of $K_{a/d,b/d}$ and $r=n-a-b$ isolates,
 $P_G(4)=\Bip_4(a/d,b/d)^d4^r$ (\eqref{eq:diffcount}). AS (Lemma~2.3) and FO
 (Lemma~3.3) reprove this with the Chinese remainder criterion. AS proves
 the cycle count as the trace of the $L$th power of the $4\times4$ matrix
 with zero diagonal and ones elsewhere (eigenvalues $3,-1,-1,-1$), which is
 Part~I's argument; both manuscripts also give the path recurrence of
 \cref{fo:sec:algorithms}, which Part~II's \cref{tor:sec:small} spells out
 for three colors. FO remarks that the classification does not make the
 monoid small; it gives a short list of obstructions whose proper-coloring
 counts can be compared exactly.
\item \emph{The four-color biclique count.} Formula \eqref{fo:eq:B4} is
 Part~III's \eqref{evo:eq:k4P} (manuscripts~06 and~01 of Part~III), and
 Part~I's $B_4(a,b)$. AS (Lemma~3.1) and FO (Lemma~4.1) prove it from the
 palette sum $\sum_j\binom4jj!\,S(a,j)(4-j)^b$, substituting
 $2!\,S(a,2)=2^a-2$ and $3!\,S(a,3)=3^a-3\cdot2^a+3$; the contributions of
 $j=1,2,3$ are $4\cdot3^b$, $6(2^a-2)2^b$ and $4(3^a-3\cdot2^a+3)$, the
 $j=4$ term vanishes because $b>0$, and the result is symmetric in $a,b$,
 as it must be. FO notes that it is also the four-color specialization of
 the count in Davies's Theorem~3.
\item \emph{Residual orders.} AS's $H_r(t)=\max_{\lambda\vdash r}\lcm(t,\lambda_1,\ldots)$,
 with $H_0(t)=t$, is Part~I's \eqref{eq:H}, an exact maximum; FO uses Part~II's
 product bound $J(r)$ (\eqref{tor:eq:J}, \cref{tor:lem:product}).
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Unmixed letters and permutation orders}\label{fo:sec:unmixed}

\begin{lemma}[Unmixed and nonsurjective cases; AS and FO]\label{fo:lem:easy}
Let $n\ge2$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item If both letters are permutations, the deficit is at least
$4^n-M(n,4)\ge3\cdot4^{n-1}$.
\item If both letters are singular, the deficit is at least $9\cdot4^{n-2}-1$.
\item \textup{(FO)} If $\tau$ is not surjective, the deficit is at least
$4^n-3^n\ge4^{n-1}$.
\end{enumerate}
For $n\ge7$ each of these bounds exceeds $7\cdot2^n$ \textup{(FO)}.
\end{lemma}

These are Part~III's \cref{evo:lem:easy} at $k=4$ and Part~I's
\eqref{eq:Dnon}--\eqref{eq:Dsing}; (iii) is also Part~III's
\cref{evo:et:rem:unmixed}. The manuscripts' proofs of the multinomial bound
differ from Part~III's restriction argument and are kept.

\begin{proof}[Proofs of the bound $M(n,4)\le4^{n-1}$]
\emph{AS.} Permutations preserve the four color-class sizes, so an orbit lies
in one class of size at most $M(n,4)$; the multinomial recurrence gives
$M(n,4)\le4M(n-1,4)$ and $M(1,4)=1$. \emph{FO.} The maximum occurs when the
multiplicities differ by at most one (replacing $u,v$ by $u-1,v+1$ when
$u\ge v+2$ reduces the factorial denominator by the factor $(v+1)/u<1$).
Normalize all multinomial coefficients at level $n$ by $4^n$: each
normalized coefficient is the average of four at level $n-1$ (nonexistent
entries read as zero), so the maximum is nonincreasing; it is $1/4$ at level
one.

For (ii), choose a collision pair of each letter; a nonidentity word ends on
the right with one of them and makes its pair monochromatic, so no proper
coloring of the graph formed by these one or two edges is reachable except
possibly $\tau$ itself; two distinct edges have exactly $9\cdot4^{n-2}$ proper
four-colorings whether or not they meet, one edge has $12\cdot4^{n-2}$.
For (iii), a color absent from $\tau$ is absent from every orbit element.
FO's comparison with $7\cdot2^n$: $4^n-3^n\ge4^{n-1}$ and
$4^n-M(n,4)\ge3\cdot4^{n-1}$, and all three exceed $7\cdot2^n$ at $n=7$ and
remain larger as $n$ grows.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Permutation orders]\label{fo:lem:order}
\textup{(AS)} A permutation on $n\ge1$ points has order at most $2^{n-1}$.
\textup{(FO)} A permutation on $r$ points has order at most $J(r)$, and
\begin{equation}\label{fo:eq:period}
 J(r)\le3^{r/3}<(3/2)^r<2^r\qquad(r>0).
\end{equation}
In the same-cycle case $\ord(p)\le mJ(r)$, and in the cross-cycle case
$\ord(p)\le\lcm(a,b)J(r)=\tfrac{ab}{d}J(r)$ \textup{(FO)}; exactly,
$\ord(p)\le H_{n-m}(m)$, respectively $H_{n-a-b}(\lcm(a,b))$ \textup{(AS)}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\emph{AS.} If the cycle lengths are $m_1,\ldots,m_c$, the order divides
$\prod m_i$, and $m\le2^{m-1}$ for every positive integer $m$ by induction;
so $\ord(p)\le2^{n-c}\le2^{n-1}$. \emph{FO.} The first two inequalities are
Part~II's \cref{tor:lem:product} and Part~III's \cref{evo:lem:order}. FO
derives the closed form of $J$ as Part~II does and proves $m\le3^{m/3}$ by
checking $m=1,\ldots,4$ and using $(m+1)^3/m^3<3$ for $m\ge4$; the strict
comparisons follow from $\sqrt[3]3<3/2<2$. Keeping the one or two active
cycles separate and bounding the order on the remaining $r$ states gives the
local bounds. FO calls the Landau function unnecessary here: the simpler
$J(r)$ bound makes the proof and the certificate easier to audit.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Uniform four-color bounds}\label{fo:sec:bounds}

Put $\psi(t)=3^t-3\cdot2^t$ (AS's $h(t)$; FO's $g(t)=4\psi(t)$). Then
\begin{equation}\label{fo:eq:psi}
 \Bip_4(a,b)=6\cdot2^{a+b}+4\bigl(\psi(a)+\psi(b)\bigr)+12,\qquad
 \psi(1)=\psi(2)=-3,\ \psi(3)=3,
\end{equation}
and $\psi$ is increasing from $t=2$ on, since $\psi(t+1)-\psi(t)=2\cdot3^t-3\cdot2^t>0$
for $t\ge2$; in FO's form $\Delta g(t)=8\cdot3^t-12\cdot2^t$ and
$\Delta^2g(t)=16\cdot3^t-12\cdot2^t>0$, and $\psi\ge-3$ throughout.

\begin{lemma}[Uniform exponential lower bounds; AS and FO]\label{fo:lem:counts}
For positive integers $a,b$ with $w=a+b$ and every $L\ge2$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item $\Bip_4(a,b)\ge6\cdot2^w-12\ge3\cdot2^w$ \textup{(FO; AS proves the outer bound)};
\item if $w\ge5$, then $\Bip_4(a,b)\ge6\cdot2^w+12>6\cdot2^w$ \textup{(AS; FO states the strict form)};
\item $C_L(4)=3^L+(-1)^L3\ge(8/3)^L$ \textup{(FO)}, hence $\ge(5/2)^L$ \textup{(AS)}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(a) By \eqref{fo:eq:psi} and $\psi\ge-3$; $w\ge2$ gives the second inequality.
AS's proof of $\Bip_4\ge3\cdot2^w$ is different: choose a two-color palette for
the left side and the complementary palette for the right; the six choices
give $6\cdot2^w$ palette-coloring pairs, and a proper coloring occurs in at
most two of them (two exactly when both sides are monochromatic).
(b) If $w\ge5$ one side is at least three, so its $\psi$-value is at least
$3$ while the other is at least $-3$; hence $\psi(a)+\psi(b)\ge0$.
(c) \emph{FO:} $C_2=12>(8/3)^2$, and for $L\ge3$,
$C_L\ge3^L-3\ge\frac89\,3^L\ge(\frac89)^L3^L$. \emph{AS:} $L=2$ is direct,
$3^3-3=24>(5/2)^3$, and $3^{L+1}-3=3(3^L-3)+6$ gives $3^L-3\ge(5/2)^L$ for
$L\ge3$ by induction; the even-$L$ expression is larger still.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Balancing at four outputs}\label{fo:sec:balance}

The closest coprime split \eqref{fo:eq:split} is the unique coprime pair
$a<b$, $a+b=n$, with least $b-a$: both manuscripts prove this as Part~II
(\cref{tor:lem:optimize}) and Part~III (\cref{evo:cor:split}) do (for odd
$n$ consecutive integers; for $n=2h$ with $h$ even the odd pair $h\pm1$ has
gcd dividing two; for odd $h\ge5$ that pair is even, and the odd pair $h\pm2$
has gcd dividing four; the equal split is not coprime).

\begin{lemma}[Balancing the period-corrected count; AS and FO]\label{fo:lem:balance}
Put $\mathcal F_4(a,b)=\Bip_4(a,b)-ab$. For positive integers $b\ge a+2$,
\begin{equation}\label{fo:eq:balance}
 \mathcal F_4(a,b)-\mathcal F_4(a+1,b-1)
 =8(3^{b-1}-3^a)-12(2^{b-1}-2^a)+(b-a-1)>0.
\end{equation}
Hence, among coprime pairs with sum $n\ge7$, $\mathcal F_4$ is uniquely
minimized at $(a_n,b_n)$, with value $D^*_4(n)$. \textup{(AS)} Every other
coprime pair $a<b$ with sum $n$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{fo:as:eq:balancegap}
 \mathcal F_4(a,b)>D^*_4(n)+a_nb_n.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

The first assertion is the case $k=4$ of Part~III's \cref{evo:cor:split}
(via \cref{evo:thm:balance}, or quantitatively \cref{evo:et:thm:balance});
the manuscripts' proof is a direct $k=4$ identity.

\begin{proof}
Subtract two instances of \eqref{fo:eq:B4}; the product term contributes
$b-a-1$. In FO's form the difference is $\Delta g(b-1)-\Delta g(a)+(b-a-1)$.
The function $8\cdot3^t-12\cdot2^t$ is strictly increasing for $t\ge1$ (its
increment is $16\cdot3^t-12\cdot2^t>0$), which gives strict balancing; the
intermediate splits need not be coprime, since strict balancing holds for
all positive splits. So the closest admissible split is the unique minimum.

\emph{The gap (AS).} Order the other pair as $a<b$; then $a<a_n$, and the
inward moves from $(a,b)$ to $(a_n,b_n)$ include the last move from
$(a_n-1,b_n+1)$, whose loss is at least
\[
 16\cdot3^{a_n-1}-12\cdot2^{a_n-1}+1>4\cdot3^{a_n-1}>(a_n+2)^2\ge n^2/4\ge a_nb_n.
\]
The first strict inequality uses $a_n-1\ge2$; the second holds at $a_n=3$ and
persists by induction, since multiplying by three dominates the ratio
$(a_n+3)^2/(a_n+2)^2$; finally $n\le2a_n+4$. Summing the positive inward
losses proves \eqref{fo:as:eq:balancegap}.
\end{proof}

Both manuscripts explain why four outputs are especially tractable. A proper
three-coloring of a biclique is constant on one side (Part~II); with four
colors both sides can carry primitive nonconstant words on disjoint two-color
palettes, so the proper orbit can have length $ab$ rather than $\max(a,b)$
(FO). The leading term of $\Bip_4(a,b)$ is $6\cdot2^{a+b}$, independent of the
imbalance of the split; a nonspanning or disconnected graph has a strictly
larger leading coefficient, a same-cycle graph a larger exponential base, and
the optimal split is selected by a smaller, strictly convex correction (AS
and FO).

\section{An effective tail: every \texorpdfstring{$n\ge26$}{n >= 26}}\label{fo:sec:tail}

This section proves the upper bound of \cref{fo:thm:main} for $n\ge26$ twice,
by AS's and by FO's elementary estimates. Neither uses asymptotic notation;
both reduce to one integer inequality at $n=26$ and a decreasing ratio.

\subsection{AS's exclusion}

\begin{lemma}[An upper estimate on the witness deficit; AS]\label{fo:as:lem:target}
For all $n\ge26$, $D^*_4(n)<7\cdot2^n$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write $a_n=n/2-\delta_n$, $b_n=n/2+\delta_n$ with $0<\delta_n\le2$. Since
$a_nb_n\ge12$,
\[
 D^*_4(n)\le6\cdot2^n+4(3^{\delta_n}+3^{-\delta_n})3^{n/2}
 \le6\cdot2^n+\frac{328}{9}3^{n/2}.
\]
At $n=26$ the last term is strictly less than $2^n$, by the integer inequality
\begin{equation}\label{fo:as:eq:threshold26}
 328\cdot3^{13}<9\cdot4^{13}.
\end{equation}
The ratio $3^{n/2}/2^n=(\sqrt3/2)^n$ decreases, so the inequality persists.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Structural exclusion beyond the threshold; AS]\label{fo:as:prop:tail}
Let $n\ge26$. In the complete case split of \cref{fo:sec:imported} and
\cref{fo:lem:easy}, every case except one spanning coprime biclique has
deficit greater than $8\cdot2^n$. A spanning coprime biclique with cycle
lengths $a,b$ has deficit at least $\mathcal F_4(a,b)$. Consequently the upper
bound \eqref{fo:eq:upper} holds for every $n\ge26$, and every structural case
other than the spanning biclique with cycle lengths $(a_n,b_n)$ has deficit
strictly greater than $D^*_4(n)+a_nb_n$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
A cross-cycle collision with $d=\gcd(a,b)\ge2$ and $r=n-a-b$ has, by
\cref{fo:lem:counts}(a), $P_G(4)=\Bip_4(a/d,b/d)^d4^r\ge3^d2^{a+b}4^r=3^d2^{n+r}\ge9\cdot2^n$;
subtracting $\ord(p)\le2^{n-1}$ (\cref{fo:lem:order}) leaves more than
$(17/2)2^n>8\cdot2^n$. If $d=1$ but $r\ge2$, the same estimate gives
$P_G(4)\ge3\cdot2^{n+r}\ge12\cdot2^n$; if $r=1$, then $a+b=n-1\ge5$ and
\cref{fo:lem:counts}(b) gives $P_G(4)\ge(6\cdot2^{n-1}+12)4>12\cdot2^n$; the
deficit is at least $(23/2)2^n$. A same-cycle collision has
$P_G(4)\ge(5/2)^{n-r}4^r\ge(5/2)^n$ by \cref{fo:lem:counts}(c); already at
$n=10$, $(5/4)^n>17/2$ (that is, $2\cdot5^{10}>17\cdot4^{10}$), and this
persists, so $P_G(4)-\ord(p)>8\cdot2^n$ for $n\ge26$. The two bounds of
\cref{fo:lem:easy}(i)--(ii) also exceed $8\cdot2^n$. This leaves $d=1$,
$r=0$: $p$ has exactly two cycles of coprime lengths $a,b$, its order is
$ab$, and the graph bound is $\Bip_4(a,b)-ab=\mathcal F_4(a,b)$, minimized
at $(a_n,b_n)$ by \cref{fo:lem:balance}.

For the margin, the excluded cases have deficit
$>8\cdot2^n>D^*_4(n)+2^n>D^*_4(n)+a_nb_n$, since
$a_nb_n\le n^2/4<2^n$ for $n\ge26$ (true at $26$ and preserved by induction);
every other spanning coprime split has the margin by
\eqref{fo:as:eq:balancegap}.
\end{proof}

AS: the threshold $26$ is sufficient for this particular collection of simple
estimates, not a claimed optimal analytic threshold; no conclusion about
smaller $n$ is drawn by continuity or extrapolation, and the next section
handles them exactly.

\subsection{A second route: FO's exclusion}

\begin{lemma}[Eliminating nonoptimal graph types; FO]\label{fo:fo:lem:eliminate}
For $n\ge7$, a same-cycle collision forces $\defi>7\cdot2^n$, and a
cross-cycle collision with $\gcd(a,b)\ge2$ or $a+b<n$ forces
$\defi>8\cdot2^n$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For a same-cycle collision, $P_G(4)=C_{m/d}(4)^d4^r\ge(8/3)^n$ by
\cref{fo:lem:counts}(c), and $\ord(p)<(3/2)^n$ by \eqref{fo:eq:period}, so
$\defi/2^n>(4/3)^n-(3/4)^n>7$ for $n\ge7$; the middle expression increases
with $n$, and at $n=7$ the claim is the integer inequality
$4^{14}-3^{14}>7\cdot12^7$. For a cross-cycle collision with
$d=\gcd(a,b)\ge2$, $P_G(4)=\Bip_4(a/d,b/d)^d4^r\ge3^d2^{n+r}\ge9\cdot2^n$, and
$\ord(p)<2^n$. If $d=1$ and $r\ge1$: when $a+b\ge5$,
$P_G(4)>6\cdot2^{a+b}4^r=6\cdot2^{n+r}\ge12\cdot2^n$; when $a+b\le4$, $n\ge7$
forces $r\ge3$ and $P_G(4)\ge3\cdot2^{n+r}\ge24\cdot2^n$. Again subtracting an
order below $2^n$ suffices.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The optimal candidate is below the separating threshold; FO]\label{fo:fo:lem:threshold}
For every $n\ge26$,
\begin{equation}\label{fo:fo:eq:threshold}
 D^*_4(n)<6\cdot2^n+40\cdot3^{n/2}<7\cdot2^n.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
With $a_n=n/2-\delta_n$, $b_n=n/2+\delta_n$, $0\le\delta_n\le2$, one has
$3^{\delta_n}+3^{-\delta_n}\le9+1/9<10$, so $4(3^{a_n}+3^{b_n})<40\cdot3^{n/2}$;
the remaining correction $-12(2^{a_n}+2^{b_n})+12-a_nb_n$ is negative. For
the second inequality the ratio $40\cdot3^{n/2}/2^n$ decreases, and at $n=26$
the comparison is $40\cdot3^{13}=63{,}772{,}920<67{,}108{,}864=2^{26}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Analytic upper bound and strictness; FO]\label{fo:fo:prop:large}
For $n\ge26$, every orbit has deficit at least $D^*_4(n)$. Equality requires
one permutation and one singular generator, the permutation having exactly
two cycles, of lengths $a_n,b_n$, and every collision pair of the singular
generator joins those two cycles.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The easy regimes have deficit greater than $7\cdot2^n$
(\cref{fo:lem:easy}), so they cannot attain the candidate of
\cref{fo:fo:lem:threshold}. In the mixed regime choose any collision pair;
\cref{fo:fo:lem:eliminate} rules out a same-cycle collision and every
cross-cycle graph with $d>1$ or $r>0$. Only two full coprime cycles of
lengths $a,b$ remain; their order is $ab$, so $\defi\ge\Bip_4(a,b)-ab\ge D^*_4(n)$,
with equality in the second step only at $(a_n,b_n)$
(\cref{fo:lem:balance}). Since the argument applies to \emph{any} collision
pair, every collision joins the two cycles in an equality case.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Comparison with Part~III's threshold; added in the merge]\label{fo:rem:threshold}
Part~III's analytic threshold at $k=4$ is $N(4)=212$ (\cref{evo:eq:Ndef},
\cref{evo:tab:closure}); below it, Part~III closes $7\le n\le211$ by
$180{,}964$ exact comparisons in $205$ rows per implementation. AS and FO
reach $26$ because at $k=4$ the leading term $6\cdot2^n$ of every spanning
coprime biclique does not depend on the split, so the competing graph types
are separated by a whole multiple of $2^n$; Part~III's $N(k)$ comes from an
argument uniform in $k$, which the manuscripts' estimates are not. Neither
$26$ nor $212$ is claimed to be least: both are sufficient thresholds, and the
formula already holds from $n=7$ by the finite closures. The first part of
\cref{evo:q:threshold} (a smaller explicit threshold) is thereby improved at
$k=4$ only. (For comparison: $D^*_4(n)<7\cdot2^n$ first holds at $n=15$ and
fails again at $n=16$, $18$ and $22$; FO's intermediate bound
$6\cdot2^n+40\cdot3^{n/2}<7\cdot2^n$ first holds at $n=26$. The write phase
checked both by exact arithmetic.)
\end{remark}

\section{Finite certificates below the threshold}\label{fo:sec:finite}

\subsection{Two complete finite lists}

For each $n$ both manuscripts form a finite list of integers, one for every
structural case left by the reduction, and check that its minimum is the
claimed deficit. Neither enumerates transformation monoids or automata; the
finite objects are cycle lengths, divisors and residual integer partitions.
AS calls this the essential reduction that makes the finite proof small and
independently checkable.

\emph{AS's list.} For $n\ge4$, $\mathcal K^{\mathrm{AS}}_n$ consists of
\begin{align}
 &3\cdot4^{n-1},\qquad 9\cdot4^{n-2}-1,\label{fo:as:eq:listeasy}\\
 &C_{m/d}(4)^d\,4^{n-m}-H_{n-m}(m),
 &&2\le m\le n,\ d\mid m,\ d<m,\label{fo:as:eq:listsame}\\
 &\Bip_4(a/d,b/d)^d\,4^{n-a-b}-H_{n-a-b}(\lcm(a,b)),
 &&1\le a\le b,\ a+b\le n,\ d=\gcd(a,b).\label{fo:as:eq:listcross}
\end{align}

\emph{FO's list.} For $n\ge7$, $\mathcal K^{\mathrm{FO}}_n$ consists of the
three easy entries $4^n-3^n$, $4^n-M(n,4)$, $9\cdot4^{n-2}-1$, the same-cycle
entries $C_{m/d}(4)^d4^{n-m}-mJ(n-m)$ over the same range, and the cross-cycle
entries $\Bip_4(a/d,b/d)^d4^r-\frac{ab}{d}J(r)$, $r=n-a-b$, over the same
range. Entries keep their structural tags even when numerical values
coincide.

\begin{lemma}[Completeness; AS and FO]\label{fo:lem:complete}
Every pair of transformations on $n$ points and every output map have
deficit at least $\min\mathcal K^{\mathrm{AS}}_n$, and at least
$\min\mathcal K^{\mathrm{FO}}_n$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The easy entries handle two permutations, two singular letters and (FO) a
nonsurjective output (\cref{fo:lem:easy}). In the mixed case choose any
collision pair. For a same-cycle pair the containing cycle has length $m$ and
the other cycles partition $n-m$, so $\ord(p)\le H_{n-m}(m)\le mJ(n-m)$; for a
cross pair on cycles of lengths $a,b$ the other cycles partition $n-a-b$, so
$\ord(p)\le H_{n-a-b}(\lcm(a,b))\le\lcm(a,b)J(n-a-b)$
(\cref{fo:lem:order}). The classification covers every possible pair, and
taking the minimum over the list only weakens the bound.
\end{proof}

Both lists are variants of Part~I's structural collection
\eqref{eq:Dnon}--\eqref{eq:Dbip} at $k=4$: AS's drops the nonsurjective entry
(not needed, since the collision bound holds for every $\tau$) and weakens the
multinomial entry to $3\cdot4^{n-1}$, keeping Part~I's exact order sets; FO's
keeps all three easy entries and replaces the exact order sets by Part~II's
product bound $J$. At $k=4$ Part~I's period term $T_{r,4}(a,b)$ is
$H_r(\lcm(a,b))$ (\eqref{eq:periodimprove}), so AS's cross entries are
Part~I's.

\begin{proposition}[Finite certificates; AS and FO]\label{fo:prop:finite}
\textup{(AS)} For every $4\le n\le25$, $\min\mathcal K^{\mathrm{AS}}_n=D_4(n)$ of
\cref{fo:thm:main}, attained by a unique entry: the same-cycle entry
$(m,d,r)=(4,1,0)$ at $n=4$, the cross-cycle entry $(a,b,r)=(2,3,0)$ at $n=5$,
the same-cycle entry $(6,2,0)$ at $n=6$, and the cross-cycle entry
$(a_n,b_n,0)$ for $7\le n\le25$, where every other entry exceeds
$D^*_4(n)+a_nb_n$. \textup{(FO)} For every $7\le n\le25$, the unique minimizing
tagged entry of $\mathcal K^{\mathrm{FO}}_n$ is the cross-cycle entry
$(a,b,d,r)=(a_n,b_n,1,0)$, with value $D^*_4(n)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Finite computational proofs]
\emph{AS.} Enumerate the ranges and all integer partitions exactly, and
evaluate all powers and least common multiples as integers. The resulting
$2{,}082$ entries, their parameters and the $22$ minima are in
\path{data/07-all-state-finite_certificate.json};
\cref{fo:tab:finite} displays the minimum and the next-smallest entry, so
uniqueness and the gap can be checked from the summary
(\path{data/07-all-state-finite_summary.csv}). The producer
\path{code/07-all-state-verify.py} enumerates nondecreasing partitions and
uses \eqref{fo:eq:B4}; the independent checker
\path{code/07-all-state-independent_check.py} imports no producer code,
enumerates partitions in the reverse direction, evaluates biclique counts by
the Stirling-number formula and cycle counts by the path recurrence, and
re-enumerates every structural entry instead of trusting the reported
minimum. Both completed successfully on all $2{,}082$ entries.

\emph{FO.} Evaluate the list with the integer formulas \eqref{fo:eq:B4},
$C_L(4)$, $M(n,4)$ and the closed form of $J$. There are $2{,}059$ entries in
total; the complete rows, including the runner-up tag, are in
\path{data/06-four-output-base_certificate.csv}. The primary implementation
\path{code/06-four-output-verify.py} uses the closed forms; the independent
implementation \path{code/06-four-output-independent_check.py} computes
biclique counts by Stirling numbers, cycle counts by the transfer recurrence
and $J$ by the dynamic program $J(r)=\max_{1\le j\le r}jJ(r-j)$, enumerates
cross-cycle cases by total occupied size rather than by first cycle length,
and verifies every delivered table field. Both returned the stated unique
minima; all arithmetic is exact, and all checks remain active under Python's
optimization flag.

Each is a finite computational proof, with the ordinary execution and
integer-arithmetic trust boundary (\cref{fo:sec:verification}).
\end{proof}

FO's comments on the table: at $n=7$ the minimizing entry is
$\Bip_4(3,4)-12=924-12=912$ and the closest competitor is
