one isolated state. So the finite check includes the potentially dangerous
arithmetic exceptions instead of assuming that all best candidates are
balanced full-support bicliques.

\begin{corollary}[Universal upper bound and structural margin; AS and FO]\label{fo:cor:universal}
The upper bound \eqref{fo:eq:upper} holds for every $n\ge4$. For every $n\ge7$,
all structural entries other than the spanning coprime biclique with cycle
lengths $(a_n,b_n)$ have deficit strictly greater than $D^*_4(n)+a_nb_n$
\textup{(AS)}; in particular, if equality is possible, every selected
collision yields the unique minimizing graph type, and the cycle structure and
cross-collision restrictions of \cref{fo:fo:prop:large} hold for every $n\ge7$
\textup{(FO)}.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Combine \cref{fo:prop:finite} with \cref{fo:as:prop:tail} (AS), respectively
with \cref{fo:fo:prop:large} (FO); the intervals $4\le n\le25$ (or
$7\le n\le25$) and $n\ge26$ cover the domain.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Comparison with Parts~I and~III; added in the merge}\label{fo:sec:rowcheck}

The write phase compared the closed form \eqref{fo:eq:main} with the recorded
data of every part, in exact integers: it equals Part~III's
$4^n-\Bip_4(a_n,b_n)+a_nb_n$ with the closest-coprime split for $7\le n\le400$;
the \texttt{upper} and \texttt{lower} columns of all $24$ rows $k=4$
($7\le n\le30$) of Part~I's \path{data/finite_range_bounds.csv}; all $94$
rows $k=4$ of \path{data/03-eventual-finite_certificates.csv}; the split and
the target deficit of all $205$ rows of
\path{data/05-quartic-threshold-finite_k4.csv} ($7\le n\le211$); all $213$
rows of FO's \path{data/06-four-output-base_certificate.csv} and
\path{..._structural_sweep.csv}; and AS's $22$ rows, including $176$, $826$ and
$3526$ at $n=4,5,6$. There were no mismatches. On $7\le n\le25$ the two
certificates have identical margins (FO's \texttt{margin} equals AS's
\texttt{gap}); FO has exactly one more entry per $n$ (its nonsurjective
entry), $2{,}059$ against AS's $2{,}040$ on the common range. Part~III's
margins at $k=4$ are different numbers (its smallest excluded margin is
$1265$ at $n=7$), because its list compresses component sizes and bounds
residual orders by $\lfloor3^{r/3}\rfloor$. So the range $7\le n\le25$ of
the two new certificates was already covered by Part~I's
\cref{prop:finite} ($n\le30$) and by Part~III's closure ($n\le211$); what is
new is that, with the threshold $26$, a certificate of about two thousand
comparisons suffices for the whole four-output slice.

\section{Attainment and the small witnesses}\label{fo:sec:attainment}

\subsection{The imported generation theorem}

Both manuscripts import, for attainment only, the Holzer--K\"onig
two-generation theorem \cite[Theorem~8]{HolzerKoenig2004} in the form stated
by Davies \cite[Section~3, printed p.~7]{Davies2017}, who attributes the
displayed generators to Krawetz \cite{Krawetz2003}. This is Part~III's
\cref{evo:et:def:U} and the imported theorem of \cref{evo:sec:witness}: for
$1<a<b$, $\gcd(a,b)=1$, $Q=X\sqcup Y$ with $X=\{0,\ldots,a-1\}$,
$Y=\{a,\ldots,n-1\}$ and
\begin{equation}\label{fo:eq:generators}
 p=(0\ 1\ \cdots\ a-1)(a\ a+1\ \cdots\ n-1),\qquad
 s(0)=a,\ s(n-1)=0,\ \text{and, if $a$ is odd, }s(1)=2,\ s(2)=1,
\end{equation}
with $s$ fixing every other state, the monoid $U_{a,b}$ of powers of $p$ and
maps $t$ with $t(X)\cap t(Y)\ne\varnothing$, $Y\not\subseteq t(Q)$ is
generated by $p$ and $s$. (AS calls the parity rule the four-output
specialization of Davies's displayed generators; FO writes it as
$(s(1),s(2))=(2,1)$ for odd $a$ and $(1,2)$ for even $a$.) The map $s$ has the
unique collision pair $\{0,a\}$, omits $n-1$ from its image and has rank $n-1$
(FO); in the range $a\ge3$ all listed points exist (AS). Neither manuscript
reproves the generation theorem, and both say that finite breadth-first
searches do not replace it; AS: all universal lower-bound claims depend on
this published theorem, and neither a few searches nor the repository's
presence replaces that dependency. The upper bound does not use it (FO).

\subsection{Saturating the improper colorings}

The saturation step is Part~III's \cref{evo:et:lem:saturation}, and the
witness Part~III's \cref{evo:et:prop:witness} at $k=4$ (which gives $X$ and $Y$
two colors each). AS and FO prove it for their own output words.

\begin{proposition}[All-state lower witness; AS]\label{fo:as:prop:attainment}
For $n\ge7$, $a=a_n$, $b=b_n$ and the generators \eqref{fo:eq:generators}, the
output word $\tau=0^{a-1}1\,2^{b-1}3$ has orbit size
$4^n-\Bip_4(a,b)+ab$. The DFAO with initial state $0$ is accessible and
minimal.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The graph of the cross collision is $K_{a,b}$, and $\tau$ is proper for it
(disjoint palettes). Each block word has full cyclic period, because it
contains a symbol occurring exactly once, so the combined period is
$\lcm(a,b)=ab$ and exactly $ab$ proper colorings lie in $\tau\langle p\rangle$.
Let $c$ be any improper coloring, so $c(x)=c(y)$ for some $x\in X$, $y\in Y$.
Choose $y_0\in Y$ labelled $2$ by $\tau$; since $b\ge4$ this label occurs at
another point of $Y$, so there are representatives $r_i\in Q\setminus\{y_0\}$
with $\tau(r_i)=i$ for every $i\in\D$. The map $t(q)=r_{c(q)}$ identifies
$x,y$ and omits $y_0$, so $t\in U_{a,b}$, and $\tau\circ t=c$. By the imported
theorem every improper coloring is reachable; there are $4^n-\Bip_4(a,b)$ of
them, and by \eqref{fo:eq:sharpgraph} the only reachable proper colorings are
the $ab$ pure powers. The $p$-cycle reaches $X$ from $0$, $s(0)=a$ enters $Y$,
and powers of $p$ reach $Y$, so the DFAO is accessible. Points on different
cycles have different palettes, and two points on one cycle are separated by
a power of $p$ because the cyclic output word is primitive; so the DFAO is
minimal, and \cref{prop:orbit} converts the orbit count into reversal
complexity.
\end{proof}

\noindent\emph{Second proof, with another output (FO).} For $3\le a<b$
coprime, FO takes $\tau|_X=(0,1,\ldots,1)$ and $\tau|_Y=(2,3,\ldots,3)$ and
proves $|\tau U_{a,b}|=4^{a+b}-\Bip_4(a,b)+ab$. Properness and the period
$ab$ are as above (a unique occurrence of the first color prevents a
nonidentity rotation from fixing a word). Every nonpermutation in $U_{a,b}$
has a cross-block collision, so no other proper coloring occurs. Conversely,
for an improper $c$ choose, for each color, one state carrying it under
$\tau$, and send each $q$ to the representative of $c(q)$: this map $u$ has
$\tau\circ u=c$ and a cross-block collision, and its image contains at most
two states of $Y$ (the representatives of colors $2$ and $3$), so, as
$b\ge4$, it omits a state of $Y$ and lies in $U_{a,b}$. Accessibility and
minimality are proved as above. FO remarks that although its count is a
specialization of Davies's lower construction, the representative-map proof
makes the four-color count and the accessibility requirements explicit.

\begin{example}[Seven states; FO]\label{fo:fo:ex:n7}
The optimal split is $(3,4)$. In zero-based image-list notation
$p=(1,2,0,4,5,6,3)$, $s=(3,2,1,3,4,5,0)$, $\tau=(0,1,1,2,3,3,3)$. Here
$\Bip_4(3,4)=924$, $\ord(p)=12$ and $R_2(7,4)=4^7-924+12=15{,}472$. (These $p$
and $s$ are Part~II's arrays of \cref{tor:sec:witnesses}.) FO: this lower-bound
value already appears in Davies's table (it is also Part~I's, \cref{sec:finite});
the new assertion is the general upper bound and the all-state theorem.
\end{example}

\subsection{The three small cases}

For $n=4,5,6$ AS gives the following zero-based arrays (images of
$0,\ldots,n-1$, not cycle notation); every row uses all four output labels.
They are exactly Part~I's witnesses of \cref{app:witnesses}.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}cllll@{}}
\toprule
$n$ & $p$ & $s$ & $\tau$ & Reverse states\\
\midrule
4 & $(1,2,3,0)$ & $(0,0,3,2)$ & $(0,1,2,3)$ & $176$\\
5 & $(1,0,3,4,2)$ & $(2,1,2,3,0)$ & $(0,1,2,3,3)$ & $826$\\
6 & $(1,2,3,4,5,0)$ & $(0,3,0,4,5,1)$ & $(0,0,1,1,2,3)$ & $3526$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
At $n=4$ the collision graph is $C_4$, with $84$ proper colorings and a
four-element proper orbit; at $n=5$ it is $K_{2,3}$, with $204$ and a
six-element proper orbit; at $n=6$ it is \emph{two triangles}, not a single
six-cycle: the collision pair $\{0,2\}$ under the six-cycle has $d=2$,
$L=3$, giving $24^2=576$ proper colorings and a six-element proper orbit
(Part~I makes the same observation after its table in \cref{sec:finite}).
These give the upper deficits $80$, $198$, $570$. Full breadth-first
enumeration of the coloring orbits gives the counts in the table, once with
tuples of colors and once, independently, with integer base-four encodings;
both exhaust the reachable set under both transitions. These are finite
lower-bound certificates, not samples of words. Accessibility is immediate
from the cycles (and the cross transition at $n=5$); minimality follows from
the primitive cyclic words (and the disjoint palettes at $n=5$) and is also
checked by partition refinement. The arrays and results are in
\path{data/07-all-state-bfs_witnesses.json}.

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{fo:thm:main}]
\Cref{fo:cor:universal} supplies the upper bound, \cref{fo:as:prop:attainment}
(or FO's variant) minimal equality witnesses for $n\ge7$, and the three
arrays above equality for $n=4,5,6$. So both the algebraic orbit maximum and
the automaton reversal maximum are $4^n-D_4(n)$ for every admissible $n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What the small values establish; AS]\label{fo:as:rem:small}
The values displayed by Davies and the finite repository comparisons are not
claimed as new numerical discoveries. Their role is to complete a single
rigorously specified all-state statement and to make its lower witnesses
directly reproducible. In particular, the special behaviour at $n=6$ is kept
separate rather than forced into the coprime-split formula.
\end{remark}

\section{Rigidity, a second collision and stability}\label{fo:sec:rigidity}

Throughout this section $n\ge7$, $a=a_n$, $b=b_n$ and $R=R_2(n,4)$.

\begin{theorem}[Necessary structure of every extremizer; AS and FO]\label{fo:thm:rigidity}
If $|\tau\langle s,t\rangle|=R$ for arbitrary maps $s,t$ and output $\tau$,
then after interchanging the letters:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item one letter $p$ is a permutation with exactly two cycles $X,Y$, of
lengths $a,b$;
\item the other letter $s$ has rank $n-1$; its only nonsingleton fiber has
size two, with one point in each cycle (in particular $s$ is injective on
each cycle);
\item $\tau$ is proper for $K_{a,b}$, uses exactly two colors on each cycle,
with disjoint palettes, and both cyclic output words are primitive;
\item the reachable coloring set consists of every improper coloring of
$K_{a,b}$ together with one proper $p$-orbit of length $ab$.
\end{enumerate}
Conversely, \textup{(i)--(iv)} imply that the orbit has size $R$ \textup{(AS)}.
Conditions \textup{(i)--(iii)} alone are not asserted to imply \textup{(iv)} for
an arbitrary singular generator.
\end{theorem}

This specializes Part~III's \cref{evo:thm:rigidity} at $k=4$, whose (iii)
says $\tau(X)\cap\tau(Y)=\varnothing$ with pure orbit of size $ab$ and whose
(iv) says every improper coloring is reached; at $k=4$ these force the
$2+2$ palette split and the primitive words, and (iv) becomes an equality of
sets. It is the four-output counterpart of Part~II's
\cref{tor:thm:rigidity}.

\begin{proof}[Proof (AS)]
The structural margin of \cref{fo:cor:universal} rules out two permutations
and two singular letters. Write the mixed pair as $p,s$. Any collision pair of
$s$ must give the unique optimal graph entry, so $p$ has precisely two cycles
of lengths $a,b$, and $s$ is injective on each: a same-cycle collision would
give a strictly worse bound. Fix a cross-cycle collision. If $\tau$ were
improper for $K_{a,b}$, \eqref{fo:eq:sharpgraph} would give an orbit at most
$R-ab$; so $\tau$ is proper, its $p$-orbit has size at most $ab$, and equality
in $4^n-\Bip_4(a,b)+ab$ forces a full proper orbit of length $ab$ and
reachability of every improper coloring. With word periods $u\mid a$, $v\mid b$
(coprime), the combined period is $uv$, so $u=a$, $v=b$; both words are
nonconstant, the palettes are disjoint, and four colors split as two and two.
This proves (iii) and (iv), given that there is only one collision pair.

All nonsingleton fibers of $s$ are cross pairs, and distinct ones are
disjoint. If there were at least two, consider a coloring with exactly one
cross equality: choose $x\in X$, $y\in Y$ and distinct colors
$\alpha,\beta,\gamma$, color $x,y$ by $\alpha$, $X\setminus\{x\}$ by $\beta$ and
$Y\setminus\{y\}$ by $\gamma$. A composition with a rightmost $s$ collapses
at least two distinct cross pairs after a common rotation by $p$, so it cannot
produce this coloring; nor can a pure power of $p$, which is proper. This
contradicts reachability of every improper coloring. So there is exactly one
cross double fiber and $s$ has rank $n-1$. Finally (iv) supplies
$4^n-\Bip_4(a,b)+ab=R$ distinct reachable colorings, and the upper bound
excludes more; this is the converse.
\end{proof}

\noindent\emph{Second proof (FO).} The strict comparisons, including the
finite certificate, give (i) and show that every collision of $s$ joins the
two cycles, so $s$ is injective on each cycle, every nonsingleton fiber has
size two and is a cross pair. If there were two such pairs,
\cref{fo:fo:prop:penalty} below would make the orbit strictly smaller than
$R$; since $s$ is singular there is at least one, which proves (ii). By
\eqref{fo:eq:sharpgraph} the reachable proper colorings lie in the $p$-orbit
of $\tau$, of size at most $ab$; equality requires all improper colorings and
exactly $ab$ proper ones, and since $p$ preserves properness, $\tau$ is proper
with orbit length $ab$. The periods $u\mid a$, $v\mid b$ are coprime with
$uv=ab$, so $u=a$, $v=b$; each primitive word of length at least two uses at
least two colors, properness makes the palettes disjoint, and four colors
force exactly two each. This proves (iii), and the equality count proves (iv).

AS states the equality conditions in two layers on purpose: the cycle, fiber
and output conditions are concrete necessities, while full improper
saturation is a separate dynamical property of the generated monoid. The
standard pair of \cref{fo:sec:attainment} has that property by the published
generation theorem and the representative argument; a complete
classification of the other transition pairs with this property is not
provided.

\begin{remark}[Necessary is not sufficient; FO]\label{fo:fo:rem:notsufficient}
Statements (i)--(iii) alone do not characterize extremizers. At $n=7$ take
FO's $p$ and $\tau$ of \cref{fo:fo:ex:n7}, but let $s$ send $0$ to $3$ and fix
every other state: $s$ has rank six and the unique cross collision $\{0,3\}$,
the machine is accessible and minimal, and the output words are primitive on
disjoint palettes. Yet on the four-state cycle $Y$, $s$ acts as the identity
and $p$ only rotates, so the restriction of every reachable coloring to $Y$
has at most four possibilities, and its restriction to the other three states
at most $4^3$; the orbit has at most $4\cdot4^3=256$ states, far fewer than
$15{,}472$. FO's breadth-first search finds exactly $212$
(\path{data/06-four-output-bfs.json}); the bound $256$ already proves the
failure of sufficiency. This parallels Part~III's
\cref{evo:ex:nonsaturation} ($564$ states, with the invariant block on the
shorter cycle) and Part~II's \cref{tor:ex:nonsufficient}.
\end{remark}

\subsection{A second collision}\label{fo:sec:penalty}

Part~III prices extra kernel pairs by its matching hierarchy
(\cref{evo:thm:matching}): if $p$ has two coprime cycles and the nontrivial
kernel classes of $s$ are $t\ge2$ disjoint cross pairs, then
$|\tau\langle p,s\rangle|\le k^{a+b}-W_{k,2}(a,b)+ab$ with
$W_{k,2}=\Bip_k+\Omega_{k,1}$ and $\Omega_{k,1}(a,b)\ge kab\,\Bip_{k-1}(a-1,b-1)$
(\cref{evo:prop:firstmatching}, \eqref{evo:eq:oneedgepenalty}), the last term
counting exactly the colorings with one monochromatic cross edge. At $k=4$ FO
proves the same penalty with a direct count, under a weaker hypothesis on $s$,
and AS proves a weaker form.

\begin{lemma}[Exactly one monochromatic cross edge; FO]\label{fo:fo:lem:oneedge}
For $a,b\ge2$, the number of four-colorings of $K_{a,b}$ with exactly one
monochromatic edge is
\begin{equation}\label{fo:fo:eq:oneedge}
 \Omega^{\mathrm e}_4(a,b)=12ab\bigl(2^{a-1}+2^{b-1}-2\bigr)=4ab\,\Bip_3(a-1,b-1).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Choose the edge in $ab$ ways and its color in four. No other vertex can have
that color (it would create a second monochromatic edge with the opposite
chosen endpoint), so the remaining vertices form a proper three-coloring of
$K_{a-1,b-1}$. For positive $u,v$ the three-color biclique count is
$3(2^u+2^v-2)$: if the left side uses one color there are $3\cdot2^v$
possibilities, and if it uses two, $3(2^u-2)$, the right side then being
constant in the remaining color (Part~II's $\Bip$). Each coloring has a unique
counted edge.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[A second-collision penalty; FO]\label{fo:fo:prop:penalty}
Let $p$ consist of two coprime cycles of lengths $a,b\ge2$. If $s$ has at
least two distinct cross-cycle collision pairs, then for every output map
$\tau$,
\begin{equation}\label{fo:fo:eq:penalty}
 |\tau\langle p,s\rangle|\le4^{a+b}-\Bip_4(a,b)-\Omega^{\mathrm e}_4(a,b)+ab.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
A word containing $s$ is $u\circ s\circ p^j$ after isolating its rightmost
singular letter. Its coloring has a monochromatic edge for each of the two
distinct translated collision pairs, and these remain distinct cross edges.
So neither a proper coloring nor a coloring with exactly one monochromatic
cross edge arises from such a word. There are $\Bip_4(a,b)+\Omega^{\mathrm e}_4(a,b)$
colorings of those two types, and at most $ab$ of them can be reached by the
remaining words, the powers of $p$. No properness assumption on $\tau$ is
used.
\end{proof}

This is Part~III's bound at $k=4$, $t=2$ with the one-edge term; FO's
hypothesis allows $s$ to have further collisions of any kind, whereas
\cref{evo:thm:matching} assumes that all nontrivial kernel classes are
disjoint cross pairs.

\begin{proposition}[A $24ab$ penalty; AS]\label{fo:as:prop:penalty}
Suppose $p$ has cycles $X,Y$ of lengths $a,b$, $s$ is injective on each cycle
and has at least two cross-cycle double fibers, and $\tau$ is proper for
$K_{a,b}$. Then $|\tau\langle p,s\rangle|\le R-24ab$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Use the colorings of the rigidity proof with exactly one cross equality:
$ab$ choices of the equal pair and $4\cdot3\cdot2=24$ ordered choices of its
three colors give $24ab$ distinct colorings (the equal pair and the colors of
the two nonempty complements can be recovered). Each is unreachable by the
rightmost-singular-factor argument, and, $\tau$ being proper, not a pure
power. So at most $4^n-\Bip_4(a,b)-24ab$ improper colorings and at most $ab$
proper ones are reachable.
\end{proof}

AS: the properness hypothesis matters, because without it pure permutation
powers need not avoid the particular improper family counted, and the
proposition is not claimed to be sharp; it supplies a uniform explicit loss
sufficient for the stability theorem. (Added in the merge: since
$\Bip_3(a-1,b-1)\ge6$ for $a,b\ge2$, $\Omega^{\mathrm e}_4(a,b)\ge24ab$, so
\cref{fo:fo:prop:penalty} implies \cref{fo:as:prop:penalty} without its
properness and injectivity hypotheses; the family AS counts consists of
the one-edge colorings whose two complements are monochromatic.)

\subsection{Stability near the maximum}

\begin{theorem}[Quantitative stability; AS]\label{fo:as:thm:stability}
If $|\tau\langle s,t\rangle|>R-ab/2$, then after interchanging the letters
conditions \textup{(i)--(iii)} of \cref{fo:thm:rigidity} hold: the permutation
has the optimal two-cycle split, the singular letter has one cross-cycle
double fiber, and the output words are primitive on disjoint two-color
palettes. No assertion of complete improper saturation is required or
inferred.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
An excluded letter type or collision graph has deficit $>D^*_4(n)+ab$
(\cref{fo:cor:universal}), hence orbit $<R-ab$. So one letter is a permutation
with cycles of lengths $a,b$, the other is singular, and it has no
within-cycle collision. If $\tau$ is improper the orbit is at most $R-ab$. For
proper $\tau$, a nonfull $p$-orbit has size a proper divisor of $ab$, at most
$ab/2$, and the chromatic bound gives at most $R-ab/2$; so the period is full,
forcing the primitive disjoint $2+2$ palettes. If $s$ had more than one cross
double fiber, \cref{fo:as:prop:penalty} would give the loss $24ab$. So it has
exactly one, and rank $n-1$.
\end{proof}

AS: this is a stability statement with a certified, but not necessarily
optimal, radius; it concerns the structure of an individual nearly maximal
automaton, not merely the asymptotic size of the maximum.

\begin{theorem}[Stability at the $3^{n/2}$ scale; FO]\label{fo:fo:thm:stability}
Let $n\ge26$ and let $\defi$ be an orbit deficit. If $\defi<7\cdot2^n$, then,
after naming the generators appropriately, one is a permutation $p$ with
exactly two full coprime cycles of lengths $a'\le b'$, $a'+b'=n$, the other is
singular, and all its collisions are cross-cycle collisions. Moreover
\begin{equation}\label{fo:fo:eq:stabilitylower}
 \defi\ge6\cdot2^n+3^{b'}-\frac{n^2}{4}.
\end{equation}
Consequently, if $\kappa>0$ and
\begin{equation}\label{fo:fo:eq:stabilityhyp}
 \kappa\,3^{n/2}<2^n,\qquad \defi\le6\cdot2^n+\kappa\,3^{n/2},
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}\label{fo:fo:eq:imbalance}
 b'-a'\le2\log_3\Bigl(\kappa+\frac{n^2}{4\cdot3^{n/2}}\Bigr).
\end{equation}
For fixed $\kappa$ the cycle imbalance of such machines is therefore bounded
independently of sufficiently large $n$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\Cref{fo:lem:easy,fo:fo:lem:eliminate} eliminate every structure except two
full coprime cycles, using only $\defi<7\cdot2^n$, and the argument applies to
every collision pair. Now $b'\ge n/2\ge13$, and for $b'\ge4$,
$4\psi(b')=4\cdot3^{b'}-12\cdot2^{b'}\ge3^{b'}$ because $(3/2)^{b'}\ge4$; also
$4\psi(a')\ge-12$. So $\Bip_4(a',b')\ge6\cdot2^n+3^{b'}$ by \eqref{fo:eq:psi};
subtracting the period bound $a'b'\le n^2/4$ gives
\eqref{fo:fo:eq:stabilitylower}. The hypotheses \eqref{fo:fo:eq:stabilityhyp}
imply $\defi<7\cdot2^n$; combining the two estimates gives
$3^{b'}\le\kappa3^{n/2}+n^2/4$; divide by $3^{n/2}$, take base-three logarithms,
and use $b'-n/2=(b'-a')/2$.
\end{proof}

FO: this is a necessary structural stability statement, not a distance
estimate between transition tables; a nearly optimal orbit might still be
generated by many different rank-$(n-1)$ maps on the same two cycles, and
describing that freedom is a separate problem.

\emph{Relation to Parts~II and~III (added in the merge).} Part~III's
\cref{evo:q:stability} asks for explicit separations of nearly extremal
automata and records that the wrong-split, extra-kernel-pair and
incomplete-saturation mechanisms are not quantified there. At $k=4$,
\cref{fo:as:thm:stability} quantifies the first two for every $n\ge7$ (radius
$a_nb_n/2$ below the maximum, via the margin $>a_nb_n$ and the $24ab$ loss), and
\cref{fo:fo:thm:stability} bounds the split imbalance on the scale
$3^{n/2}$ for $n\ge26$. Incomplete saturation remains unquantified, and
neither manuscript determines the second-largest value
(\cref{evo:q:second}, Part~II's \cref{tor:q:second}).

\section{The missing orbits and the extremal output maps}\label{fo:sec:inventory}

\Cref{fo:thm:rigidity} identifies the missing states set-theoretically: they
are the proper colorings of $K_{a,b}$ with one full cyclic orbit removed. This
permits an exact orbit inventory, not just a cardinality (AS); an extremizer
misses $D^*_4(n)$ colorings, all proper, and their organization into cyclic
orbits can be determined exactly (FO). Part~II's \cref{tor:thm:inventory} is
the three-output counterpart. Throughout, $a,b$ are coprime and $p$ rotates
the two blocks $X,Y$.

\subsection{Primitive words and least periods}

For $q,m\ge1$ let $P_q(m)=\sum_{d\mid m}\mu(m/d)q^d$ (AS), the number of words
of length $m$ over a fixed $q$-letter alphabet with least cyclic period $m$
(Part~II's $P_2$ is \eqref{tor:eq:primitive}): every word of length $m$ has a
unique least period dividing $m$, so $q^m=\sum_{d\mid m}P_q(d)$, and divisor
inversion gives the formula. For $m>1$ such words are nonconstant; a primitive
binary word of length greater than one uses both symbols. FO counts instead,
for a fixed set of $j$ colors, the words using all of them,
$\sur_j(m)=\sum_{i=0}^j(-1)^i\binom ji(j-i)^m$ (Part~III's $\sur_j$), and the
primitive ones among them,
$\operatorname{prim}_j(m)=\sum_{d\mid m}\mu(m/d)\sur_j(d)$: every surjective
word is a repetition of a unique primitive word with the same palette. For
$m>1$, $\operatorname{prim}_2(m)=P_2(m)=\sum_{d\mid m}\mu(d)2^{m/d}$.

For $u\mid a$ and $v\mid b$ let $\mathcal C_{u,v}$ be the number of proper
colorings of $K_{a,b}$ whose restrictions to $X,Y$ have least periods $u,v$,
and $o_{u,v}=\mathcal C_{u,v}/(uv)$.

\begin{lemma}[Counting exact component periods; AS and FO]\label{fo:lem:periods}
Every coloring counted by $\mathcal C_{u,v}$ has $p$-orbit length $uv$, and
\begin{equation}\label{fo:as:eq:C}
 \mathcal C_{u,v}=
 \begin{cases}
 12,&u=v=1,\\
 4P_3(v),&u=1<v,\\
 4P_3(u),&v=1<u,\\
 6P_2(u)P_2(v),&u>1,\ v>1
 \end{cases}
 \qquad\textup{(AS)},
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}\label{fo:as:eq:Cmobius}
 \mathcal C_{u,v}=\sum_{d\mid u}\sum_{e\mid v}\mu(u/d)\mu(v/e)\Bip_4(d,e)
 \qquad\textup{(AS)},
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}\label{fo:fo:eq:Cpalette}
 \mathcal C_{u,v}=
 \sum_{\substack{i,j\ge1\\i+j\le4}}\frac{4!}{i!\,j!\,(4-i-j)!}\,
 \operatorname{prim}_i(u)\operatorname{prim}_j(v)
 \qquad\textup{(FO)}.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\emph{AS.} If both periods are one, choose two distinct constant colors
($4\cdot3=12$). If only the left side is constant, choose its color in four
ways and a primitive length-$v$ word from the remaining three colors; the
repetition determines the length-$b$ word. If both sides are nonconstant,
properness and the four-color limit force a disjoint $2+2$ palette split: six
choices of the left palette and $P_2(u)P_2(v)$ primitive words. Since
$\gcd(a,b)=1$, $\gcd(u,v)=1$ and the simultaneous rotation has period
$\lcm(u,v)=uv$. Alternatively, colorings with periods dividing $u,v$ are the
repetitions of proper colorings of $K_{u,v}$, so there are $\Bip_4(u,v)$ of
them; inverting this divisor sum in both variables gives
\eqref{fo:as:eq:Cmobius}.
\emph{FO.} Choose disjoint used palettes of sizes $i,j$ in
$4!/(i!\,j!\,(4-i-j)!)$ ways, then a primitive surjective $i$-color word of
length $u$ and a primitive surjective $j$-color word of length $v$, and repeat
them to lengths $a,b$; this counts every proper coloring with the specified
periods once.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact missing-orbit inventory; AS and FO]\label{fo:thm:inventory}
For coprime $a,b$, the proper colorings of $K_{a,b}$ form $o_{u,v}$ orbits of
length $uv$ for each $u\mid a$, $v\mid b$, and
\begin{equation}\label{fo:eq:inventorysum}
 \sum_{u\mid a}\sum_{v\mid b}uv\,o_{u,v}=\Bip_4(a,b)\qquad\textup{(FO)}.
\end{equation}
For any extremizer at $n\ge7$, with $(a,b)=(a_n,b_n)$, the number of missing
$p$-orbits of length $uv$ is $o_{u,v}-\bbone_{(u,v)=(a,b)}$; every missing
orbit occurs this way, and
\begin{equation}\label{fo:eq:inventoryD}
 \sum_{u\mid a}\sum_{v\mid b}uv\bigl(o_{u,v}-\bbone_{(u,v)=(a,b)}\bigr)=D^*_4(n)
 \qquad\textup{(AS)}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The proper-coloring set is invariant under $p$, and \cref{fo:lem:periods}
partitions it by exact component periods, all orbits in one part having size
$uv$; summing orbit sizes counts all proper colorings. \Cref{fo:thm:rigidity}(iv)
removes exactly one reachable proper orbit, of full size $ab$, and no others.
Since $a,b$ are coprime, the length $uv$ determines the two divisors, so no
length is counted ambiguously between different pairs (AS).
\end{proof}

For $a,b\ge3$ full periods need at least two colors on each side, so only
$i=j=2$ contributes to $o_{a,b}$ (FO):
\begin{equation}\label{fo:fo:eq:fullinventory}
 o_{a,b}=\frac{6\,P_2(a)P_2(b)}{ab}.
\end{equation}
FO's audit adds that the inventory assumes coprime cycle lengths (so that
each joint orbit has size $uv$), and that the full-period count loses one
\emph{orbit}, not one coloring.

\begin{example}[Seven states; AS and FO]\label{fo:ex:inventory7}
At $n=7$, $(a,b)=(3,4)$ and $D^*_4(7)=912$:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrrrrrr@{}}
\toprule
$(u,v)$ & $(1,1)$ & $(1,2)$ & $(3,1)$ & $(1,4)$ & $(3,2)$ & $(3,4)$ & Total\\
Orbit length $uv$ & $1$ & $2$ & $3$ & $4$ & $6$ & $12$ & \\
\midrule
Proper orbits $o_{u,v}$ (FO) & 12 & 12 & 32 & 72 & 12 & 36 & 176\\
Missing orbits (AS) & 12 & 12 & 32 & 72 & 12 & 35 & 175\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Weighting by orbit lengths gives $12+24+96+288+72+432=924=\Bip_4(3,4)$
proper colorings, of which an extremizer keeps one orbit of length $12$ and
misses $924-12=912$. FO: this is more information than the missing-state count
alone. AS: complete four-color orbit enumeration at $n=7$ and $n=8$
reproduces the corresponding inventories independently of the period-counting
formulas (\path{data/07-all-state-bfs_witnesses.json}); AS's
\path{data/07-all-state-orbit_inventories.json} records the algebraic
inventories for $7\le n\le100$, FO's \path{data/06-four-output-inventory.json}
those for $(a,b)=(3,4)$ and $(3,5)$.
\end{example}

\subsection{All extremal outputs on the standard pair}

\begin{theorem}[Extremal output maps for the published witness; AS]\label{fo:as:thm:outputmaps}
Fix the transition pair \eqref{fo:eq:generators} at $n\ge7$ with
$(a,b)=(a_n,b_n)$. Exactly $6P_2(a)P_2(b)$ output maps $\tau:Q\to\D$ attain
$R_2(n,4)$; modulo simultaneous rotation by $p$ there are
$6P_2(a)P_2(b)/(ab)$ of them.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Necessity follows from \cref{fo:thm:rigidity}: the palettes split as $2+2$
and both block words are primitive, giving six left palettes and
$P_2(a)P_2(b)$ pairs of words. Conversely, such a map is proper, surjective and
has full $p$-period $ab$; since $b\ge4$ and only two colors are used on $Y$,
some color on $Y$ has at least two representatives, so a redundant point
$y_0\in Y$ can be chosen, and the representative argument of
\cref{fo:as:prop:attainment} (Part~III's \cref{evo:et:lem:saturation})
realizes every improper coloring by a map in $U_{a,b}$ omitting $y_0$, which
the imported theorem makes reachable by the fixed pair. The action on these
maps has full orbit size $ab$.
\end{proof}

FO makes the same observation: there are $6P_2(a)P_2(b)$ full-period proper
output maps on the fixed labelled cycles, and in the monoid $U_{a,b}$ each
attains the same orbit size as its witness, because each uses two colors on
$Y$, whose size exceeds two. At $n=7$ there are $6\cdot6\cdot12=432$ such maps
in $36$ rotation classes, the $36$ full-period orbits of
\cref{fo:ex:inventory7}. AS: the theorem classifies output maps for one fixed,
known saturating pair; it does not classify all singular generators that work
with the optimal permutation, nor identify output maps under arbitrary state
or color relabelings, which are different equivalence problems.

\section{Exact sequences, asymptotics and a minimal recurrence}\label{fo:sec:recurrence}

The following are consequences of the all-state formula, not independent
claims that residue-class asymptotics or primitive-word techniques are new
methods (AS). For $n\ge7$ they concern $D_4(n)=D^*_4(n)$.

\subsection{Exact residue formulas}

Substituting \eqref{fo:eq:split} into $D^*_4(n)$ gives, with
$h=\lfloor n/2\rfloor$ (FO),
\begin{equation}\label{fo:fo:eq:residue}
 D^*_4(n)=
 \begin{cases}
 6\cdot2^n+16\cdot3^h-36\cdot2^h+12-h(h+1),&n=2h+1,\\
 6\cdot2^n+40\cdot3^{h-1}-60\cdot2^{h-1}+13-h^2,&n=2h,\ h\text{ even},\\
 6\cdot2^n+328\cdot3^{h-2}-204\cdot2^{h-2}+16-h^2,&n=2h,\ h\text{ odd},
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
and, in AS's form without square roots, for $4j+r\ge7$,
\begin{align}
 D^*_4(4j)&=6\cdot16^j+\tfrac{40}{3}9^j-30\cdot4^j+13-4j^2,\label{fo:as:eq:res0}\\
 D^*_4(4j+1)&=12\cdot16^j+16\cdot9^j-36\cdot4^j+12-4j^2-2j,\label{fo:as:eq:res1}\\
 D^*_4(4j+2)&=24\cdot16^j+\tfrac{328}{3}9^j-102\cdot4^j+15-4j^2-4j,\label{fo:as:eq:res2}\\
 D^*_4(4j+3)&=48\cdot16^j+48\cdot9^j-72\cdot4^j+10-4j^2-6j.\label{fo:as:eq:res3}
\end{align}
These are exact identities for $n\ge7$, not asymptotic expansions (FO); the
write phase checked all seven against the closed form for $7\le n\le399$.

\begin{proposition}[Exact separated-scale expression; AS and FO]\label{fo:prop:scales}
Let $c_n,d_n,e_n$ have period four, given in residue order $0,1,2,3$ by
\[
 (c_n)=\Bigl(\tfrac{40}{3},\tfrac{16}{\sqrt3},\tfrac{328}{9},\tfrac{16}{\sqrt3}\Bigr),\quad
 (d_n)=\bigl(-30,-18\sqrt2,-51,-18\sqrt2\bigr),\quad
 (e_n)=\bigl(13,\tfrac{49}{4},16,\tfrac{49}{4}\bigr).
\]
Then for every $n\ge7$
\begin{equation}\label{fo:eq:scales}
 D^*_4(n)=6\cdot2^n+c_n3^{n/2}+d_n2^{n/2}-\frac{n^2}{4}+e_n,
\end{equation}
with $c_n=4(3^{\delta_n}+3^{-\delta_n})$, $d_n=-12(2^{\delta_n}+2^{-\delta_n})$
and $e_n=12+\delta_n^2$. Consequently
\begin{gather}
 \frac{D_4(n)}{2^n}\to6,\qquad
 \frac{R_2(n,4)}{4^n}=1-6\cdot2^{-n}+O\bigl((\sqrt3/4)^n\bigr),\notag\\
 \frac{D_4(n)-6\cdot2^n}{3^{n/2}}\to
 \begin{cases}16/\sqrt3,&n\text{ odd},\\40/3,&n\equiv0\pmod4,\\328/9,&n\equiv2\pmod4.\end{cases}
 \label{fo:eq:asymptotic}
\end{gather}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Substitute $a_n=n/2-\delta_n$, $b_n=n/2+\delta_n$ into $D^*_4(n)$, using
$a_nb_n=n^2/4-\delta_n^2$ (AS's constants $C_r=c_n$, $E_r=-d_n$). Divide by
the indicated scales; the remaining exponential bases and polynomial terms
are smaller, and $3^{n/2}=o(2^n)$.
\end{proof}

In particular the leading deficit is $6\cdot2^n$ with no leading residue-class
oscillation; the oscillation first appears at the smaller scale $3^{n/2}$,
and the fraction of the coloring space that is missed is asymptotic to
$6\cdot2^{-n}$ (AS). The oscillation is arithmetic, not a numerical artifact:
odd $n$ permits adjacent cycle lengths, while for even $n$ the closest
coprime split alternates between offsets one and two from the midpoint, and
these bounded offsets have no effect on $6\cdot2^n$ but survive in the
$3^{n/2}$ correction (FO). Despite the radicals in \eqref{fo:eq:scales}, the
integer formulas are preferable for exact computation (FO).

\emph{Relation to Part~III (added in the merge).} This is Part~III's
\cref{evo:thm:evenasymptotic} at $h=2$, made exact: there $C_0=6$, $C_1=4$,
$\mu_1=\sqrt3$ and $2C_1\cosh(\delta_n\log3)=4(3^{\delta_n}+3^{-\delta_n})=c_n$,
with an error $O(\xi_1^n)=O(2^{n/2})$, which \eqref{fo:eq:scales} replaces by
the explicit term $d_n2^{n/2}-n^2/4+e_n$. Part~III's
\cref{evo:ex:k4formula} already notes that the first residue-dependent term
has base $\sqrt3$.

\subsection{A minimal recurrence and the generating function}

Let $\mathsf S$ be the forward shift in $n$ and
\begin{equation}\label{fo:eq:chi}
 \chi_4(x)=(x-2)(x^4-9)(x^4-4)(x-1)^2(x^4-1)
 =(x-2)(x^4-9)(x^4-4)(x-1)^3(x+1)(x^2+1).
\end{equation}
The first form is AS's $\chi_D$, the second FO's operator $\mathcal A(E)$; they
are the same polynomial of degree~$15$ (checked in the write phase).

\begin{theorem}[Minimal constant-coefficient recurrence; AS, annihilation also FO]\label{fo:as:thm:recurrence}
The sequence $(D_4(n))_{n\ge7}$ satisfies $\chi_4(\mathsf S)D_4=0$ from every
starting index $n\ge7$, and $\chi_4$ is its minimal characteristic
polynomial: the minimal order is $15$. The tail $(R_2(n,4))_{n\ge7}$ has
minimal characteristic polynomial $(x-4)\chi_4(x)$ and minimal order $16$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\emph{Annihilation (AS and FO).} In \eqref{fo:eq:scales}, $2^n$ is annihilated by
$\mathsf S-2$, a period-four coefficient times $3^{n/2}$ by $\mathsf S^4-9$, and
times $2^{n/2}$ by $\mathsf S^4-4$; the quadratic polynomial and the remaining
period-four term are jointly annihilated by $(\mathsf S-1)^2(\mathsf S^4-1)$,
which has a triple root at $1$ and simple roots $-1,\pm i$ (FO: a quadratic by
$(\mathsf S-1)^3$, a period-four sequence by $\mathsf S^4-1=(\mathsf S-1)(\mathsf S+1)(\mathsf S^2+1)$).
Since $R_2(n,4)=4^n-D_4(n)$, one more factor $\mathsf S-4$ suffices.

\emph{Minimality (AS).} Expand each period-four coefficient in its four
Fourier modes. For a coefficient vector $(c_0,c_1,c_2,c_1)$ the two
imaginary-frequency coefficients equal $(c_0-c_2)/4$, the alternating one is
$(c_0+c_2-2c_1)/4$ and the constant one $(c_0+c_2+2c_1)/4$. For both $(c_n)$
and $(d_n)$ all four are nonzero: for $c$ the alternating numerator is
$448/9-32/\sqrt3>0$ and $40/3\ne328/9$; for $-d$ it is $81-36\sqrt2>0$ and
$30\ne51$; the constant coefficients are positive. So all four roots of
$x^4-9$ and of $x^4-4$ are needed. The period-four vector
$\delta_n^2=(1,\frac14,4,\frac14)$ has alternating coefficient $9/8$ and
imaginary-frequency coefficients $-3/4$, all nonzero; the term $-n^2/4$ needs
multiplicity three at the root $1$; the coefficient of $2^n$ is $6\ne0$.
Exponential-polynomial sequences with distinct bases are linearly independent,
also on an infinite tail (apply shift differences $\mathsf S-\lambda$ to
eliminate all but one base, then use the nonvanishing leading coefficient of
the remaining polynomial), so no factor or multiplicity can be removed. The
base $4$ enters $R_2(n,4)$ with coefficient one and needs exactly one more
factor.
\end{proof}

FO proves the annihilation and says that no minimal-order claim is needed;
its audit states that no minimal recurrence order is claimed. Its supplementary
checks use exact integers. The write phase confirmed, independently of both
manuscripts, that $\chi_4(\mathsf S)$ annihilates the closed form from
every starting index $7\le n\le384$ and that removing any single linear factor of $\chi_4$ (over
$\mathbb C$) destroys the annihilation.

\begin{corollary}[Rational generating function; AS and FO]\label{fo:as:cor:gf}
\begin{equation}\label{fo:eq:gf}
 \sum_{n\ge7}D_4(n)z^{n-7}=\frac{\mathcal N_4(z)}{\mathcal Q_4(z)},\qquad
 \mathcal Q_4(z)=(1-2z)(1-9z^4)(1-4z^4)(1-z)^2(1-z^4),
\end{equation}
where
\begin{align*}
 \mathcal N_4(z)={}&912-1515z-188z^2+7064z^3-36544z^4+52766z^5-19986z^6
 -29624z^7\\
 &+146416z^8-210575z^9+89558z^{10}+22752z^{11}-111168z^{12}
 +159804z^{13}-69624z^{14}.
\end{align*}
The displayed denominator is reduced \textup{(AS)}; FO shows that the
denominator divides $\mathcal Q_4(z)$, written as
$(1-2z)(1-9z^4)(1-4z^4)(1-z)^3(1+z)(1+z^2)$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The recurrence gives a denominator of this form, and multiplication by the
initial series determines $\mathcal N_4$: with the ascending coefficients
\[
(q_0,\ldots,q_{15})=(1,-4,5,-2,-14,56,-70,28,49,-196,245,-98,-36,144,-180,72)
\]
of $\mathcal Q_4$, $n_i=\sum_{j\le i}q_jD_4(7+i-j)$ for $0\le i<15$, and all
later coefficients vanish by the recurrence (FO). If numerator and denominator
shared a nonconstant factor, cancellation would give a smaller-order eventual
recurrence, contrary to \cref{fo:as:thm:recurrence} (AS).
\end{proof}

Explicitly, $\sum_{j=0}^{15}q_jD_4(n-j)=0$ for $n\ge22$ (AS), equivalently
$\sum_{j=0}^{15}\alpha_jD_4(n+j)=0$ for $n\ge7$ with FO's advance coefficients
\[
(\alpha_0,\ldots,\alpha_{15})=(72,-180,144,-36,-98,245,-196,49,28,-70,56,-14,-2,5,-4,1),
\]
the $q_j$ in reverse order (FO's $a_j$, renamed because of $a_n$). The first
fifteen values, from $D_4(7)$, are
\[
\begin{aligned}
&912,\ 2133,\ 3784,\ 13359,\ 15006,\ 32353,\ 58482,\ 171447,\ 226948,\ 472965,\\
&882132,\ 2264023,\ 3442146,\ 7047969,\ 13490734.
\end{aligned}
\]
These coefficients and values are also in
\path{data/07-all-state-recurrence.json} and
\path{data/06-four-output-recurrence.json}; they can be recomputed from the
closed formula, and were not used to infer the theorem from a guessed
recurrence (AS). Providing a factored operator and exact coefficients avoids
any reliance on numerical root identification (FO). FO adds that the
recurrence permits evaluation modulo a supplied integer with
$O(\log n)$ ring operations by polynomial powering; this is not a claim of
logarithmic bit complexity for the exact integer $D_4(n)$, whose binary length
grows linearly with $n$.

\begin{remark}[Residue classes; added in the merge]\label{fo:rem:residueminimal}
Part~III's \cref{evo:et:thm:recurrence} annihilates each residue sequence
$F_r(t)=D_4(4t+r)$ by $(\mathsf E-1)^3(\mathsf E-4)(\mathsf E-9)(\mathsf E-16)$
in the step $t$, and does not assert minimality. By
\eqref{fo:as:eq:res0}--\eqref{fo:as:eq:res3}, every residue sequence is a
combination of $16^t$, $9^t$, $4^t$ and a quadratic in $t$ with leading
coefficient $-4$, all with nonzero coefficients; by the linear-independence
argument above, that annihilator is therefore minimal for each of the four
residue classes, from the first $t$ with $4t+r\ge7$. (The write phase also
checked this by exact arithmetic.) Its order-$24$ interleaving in $n$ is not
minimal: $\chi_4$ has order $15$. This answers Part~III's
\cref{evo:q:recurrence} at $k=4$.
\end{remark}

\section{Reproducible checks and their limits}\label{fo:sec:verification}

\subsection{What each manuscript ran}

Both packages use Python's standard library with exact integer arithmetic;
FO adds a standalone C++17 program. The recorded runs of FO used
Python~3.13.5. Both packages ship their recorded outputs, not merely code that
has not been run (AS).

\emph{AS.} The checks have different logical roles:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright}p{0.30\textwidth}>{\raggedright}p{0.25\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.38\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Check & Executed range & Role in the argument\\
\midrule
Complete structural collection & $4\le n\le25$; $2{,}082$ entries & Finite
upper-bound proof and strict structural margin; re-enumerated by an
independent implementation.\\[2pt]
Reachable coloring orbits & $4\le n\le9$ (producer); $4\le n\le8$ (checker) &
Finite lower-bound proof for $n=4,5,6$; regression tests at larger sizes.\\[2pt]
Accessibility and minimality & six witnesses, $4\le n\le9$ & Confirms the
original machines have the stated number of states.\\[2pt]
Missing-state orbit inventory & complete coloring space, $n=7,8$ & Checks the
orbit formulas against actual reachable and missing sets.\\[2pt]
Primitive-period formulas & $7\le n\le100$ & Compares the palette formula with
double M\"obius inversion and checks all weighted inventory sums.\\[2pt]
Order-15 recurrence & $22\le n\le500$ & Regression test, not the proof of the
recurrence or of its minimality.\\[2pt]
Threshold inequalities & exact integers at $26$ and $10$ & Checks the numerical
anchors of the infinite-tail induction.\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The producer and checker use different data representations and independent
implementations of the principal counting formulas; that protects against
coding errors, but both rely on the same graph classification, so their
agreement is not logical independence of two proofs, nor a replacement for
auditing \cref{fo:lem:complete} (AS).

\emph{FO.} The following checks completed successfully:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright}p{0.55\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.38\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Check & Completed scope\\
\midrule
Finite proof certificate & $n=7,\ldots,25$; $2{,}059$ entries\\
Primary structural regression & $n=7,\ldots,200$; $752{,}291$ entries\\
Independent structural implementation & $n=7,\ldots,100$; $101{,}568$ entries\\
Brute-force graph-coloring checks & 17 graphs; $180{,}128$ colorings\\
Inventory and unique-edge counts & $(3,4)$, $(3,5)$; $81{,}920$ colorings\\
Explicit witness accessibility and minimality & $n=7,\ldots,200$\\
Split and residue-formula comparisons & $n=7,\ldots,1000$\\
Order-15 recurrence checks & starting indices $n=7,\ldots,300$\\
Full witness-orbit breadth-first searches & $n=7,\ldots,12$\\

\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The largest complete breadth-first search reached $16{,}744{,}863$ colorings;
the six exact witness counts were
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}rrrrrrr@{}}
\toprule
$n$ & 7 & 8 & 9 & 10 & 11 & 12\\
\midrule
$|\tau\langle p,s\rangle|$ & 15\,472 & 63\,403 & 258\,360 & 1\,035\,217 & 4\,179\,298 & 16\,744\,863\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
(\path{data/06-four-output-bfs.json}, with the $212$-state non-extremal example
of \cref{fo:fo:rem:notsufficient}). They also check the interpretation of
composition and the odd-$a$ swap in the published witness; they do not verify
its generation theorem for unbounded $a,b$. (FO's regression count $752{,}291$
is the size of the structural enumeration for $7\le n\le200$, which does not
depend on the number of outputs; Part~II's verifier reports the same count at
$k=3$.) FO on independence: two programs derived from the same recurrence are
not two proofs of it; here the two certificate implementations share the
mathematically proved reduction but use distinct formulas for each principal
count (the symmetric closed form, the cycle chromatic formula and the explicit
$J$, against Stirling surjection counting, the two-coordinate transfer
recurrence and a partition-product dynamic program), and the independent code
imports no primary code. This detects transcription and implementation errors;
it does not certify the graph reduction, which is argued in
\cref{fo:sec:reduction}, and the agreement of the residue formulas and of the
recurrence checks is arithmetic consistency, not the extremal theorem from
which the sequence is derived. The Python checks use explicit error
conditions, not removable \texttt{assert} statements, and neither program
downloads code or data. The C++ program restricts its endpoint to at most $12$
(its memory guard); the largest run needs roughly $85$\,MB for its coloring
mask and queue.

\subsection{The dependency boundary}

AS: the graph classification, coloring inequalities, balancing theorem,
threshold-$26$ exclusion, rigidity, orbit inventories and recurrence arguments
are proved in the manuscript. The finite comparison is a computer-assisted
proof with an explicitly bounded domain; trusting it requires the correctness
of the short enumeration algorithms, the Python runtime and integer
arithmetic, and the recorded execution. The lower construction for all $n\ge7$
additionally uses the published two-generator theorem, which the package does
not prove. No independent referee has reviewed the arguments. These
qualifications do not alter the mathematical scope of the proposed theorem:
the proof addresses every $n\ge4$, with no unbounded computational search left
implicit. FO: the certificate is part of the proof for nineteen specified
indices; the larger sweeps and breadth-first searches are supplementary checks,
not arguments for an infinite range.

\subsection{The finite algorithms}\label{fo:sec:algorithms}

This subsection prints AS's appendix on finite algorithms and FO's compact
enumerator.

\emph{Partitions and residual orders (AS).} To enumerate nondecreasing
partitions of $r$, choose a first part $i$ between a lower bound and $r$ and
recurse on partitions of $r-i$ with parts at least $i$; $r=0$ contributes the
empty partition. Every partition has a unique first part and a unique
remaining partition with this constraint, so induction proves completeness and
absence of duplicates. Least common multiples of the parts are exactly the
possible orders of permutations on $r$ points; repeated values may be
deduplicated because only their maximum after combination with $t$ is needed.
The independent checker uses nonincreasing partitions (choose the largest part
up to a bound and recurse with that bound equal to the chosen part; the same
induction proves completeness) and computes $\lcm(t,\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_j)$
directly for each partition.

\emph{The structural enumerator (AS).} All variables are integers and all
endpoints inclusive:
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily
For n = 4,...,25:\\
\hspace*{1em}Insert the two homogeneous-letter bounds.\\
\hspace*{1em}For m = 2,...,n:\\
\hspace*{2em}For each proper positive divisor d of m:\\
\hspace*{3em}Insert the same-cycle bound with r = n-m.\\
\hspace*{1em}For a = 1,...,n-1:\\
\hspace*{2em}For b = a,...,n-a:\\
\hspace*{3em}Insert the cross-cycle bound with d = gcd(a,b).\\
\hspace*{1em}Compare every entry with the proposed minimum.\\
\hspace*{1em}Check uniqueness and, for n >= 7, the strict AB margin.
\end{quote}
The checker instead groups cases by the residual size $r$, then enumerates
$m=n-r$, divisors $L\mid m$ and splits $a+b=m$, and compares the entire
multiset of triples (case type, parameters, integer value) with the supplied
certificate. This detects missing, extra and repeated cases and incorrect
integer evaluations, not just an incorrect displayed minimum.

\emph{A second derivation of the cycle count (AS and FO).} Fix the first color
of a path, and let $S_L$ and $T_L$ count proper length-$L$ words ending,
respectively, in the first color and in a different color. Then
$(S_1,T_1)=(1,0)$, $S_{L+1}=T_L$, $T_{L+1}=3S_L+2T_L$; closing the path into a
cycle requires a different last color, so $C_L(4)=4T_L$. Solving this
two-term transfer recurrence gives $3^L+(-1)^L3$ (FO, whose $x_L,y_L$ are
$S_L,T_L$); it is the independent formula used by both checkers.

\emph{Breadth-first orbit closure (AS).} Starting with $\{\tau\}$, repeatedly
add $c\circ p$ and $c\circ s$ for each newly discovered coloring $c$. Every
inserted coloring is reachable by induction on its discovery, and every
reachable coloring is eventually inserted by induction on word length; since
there are only $4^n$ colorings the algorithm terminates with exactly the orbit.
The tuple implementation stores $(c(0),\ldots,c(n-1))$; the independent one
stores $\sum_ic(i)4^i$, extracts colors by bit shifts and marks visited integers
in a byte array. Both use the same mathematical transition; the different
encodings protect against implementation mistakes but are not different
mathematics.

\emph{FO's compact enumerator.} FO prints the whole finite comparison in
deliberately small form (its production checker additionally writes the full
CSV, reports coverage and performs the supplementary checks). The code keeps
FO's variable names ($A$ is $a_n$, $B(a,b)$ is $\Bip_4(a,b)$):
\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
from math import gcd, factorial

def B(a, b):
    return (6*2**(a+b) + 4*(3**a + 3**b)
            - 12*(2**a + 2**b) + 12)

def J(r):
    if r < 2:
        return 1
    q, t = divmod(r, 3)
    if t == 0:
        return 3**q
    return 4*3**(q-1) if t == 1 else 2*3**q

for n in range(7, 26):
    A = max(a for a in range(1, n//2 + 1)
            if gcd(a, n-a) == 1)
    target = B(A, n-A) - A*(n-A)
    q, z = divmod(n, 4)
    M = factorial(n)//(factorial(q)**(4-z)
                       * factorial(q+1)**z)
    entries = [(4**n-3**n, None), (4**n-M, None),
               (9*4**(n-2)-1, None)]
    for m in range(2, n+1):
        for d in range(1, m):
            if m % d == 0:
                L, r = m//d, n-m
                P = (3**L + (-1)**L*3)**d * 4**r
                entries.append((P-m*J(r), None))
    for a in range(1, n):
        for b in range(a, n-a+1):
            d, r = gcd(a, b), n-a-b
            P = B(a//d, b//d)**d * 4**r
            entries.append((P-(a*b//d)*J(r),
                            (a, b, d, r)))
    least = min(value for value, tag in entries)
    tags = [tag for value, tag in entries
            if value == least]
    if least != target or tags != [(A, n-A, 1, 0)]:
        raise RuntimeError((n, least, target, tags))
print("PASS: all nineteen finite comparisons")
\end{lstlisting}
The loops exhaust $\mathcal K^{\mathrm{FO}}_n$, including two-vertex edges,
singleton cycles, arbitrary residual states and $J(0)=1$; none of these
boundary cases may be dropped (FO).

\subsection{Commands and files}\label{fo:sec:commands}

The shipped programs are byte-identical to the deliveries and still use the
delivered layout: each computes its package root as the parent of its own
directory and reads and writes \texttt{data/}\ldots\ there under the
delivered, unprefixed names. Run in this report's directory,
\path{code/07-all-state-verify.py} would therefore \emph{overwrite Part~I's}
\path{data/bfs_witnesses.json} and \path{data/verification_summary.json}, and
\path{code/07-all-state-independent_check.py} would then read Part~I's file;
\path{code/06-four-output-verify.py} would write five stray files, and
\path{code/06-four-output-independent_check.py} would fail on the missing
\path{data/base_certificate.csv}; both Makefiles name the delivered scripts,
which do not exist here, and their \texttt{pdf} and \texttt{clean} targets act
on the merged \texttt{article.tex} in place. The report's README gives a recipe
that restores the delivered names in a scratch copy; there the commands are
\begin{lstlisting}
python code/verify.py                # AS (07-all-state-)
python code/independent_check.py
python -O code/verify.py             # FO (06-four-output-)
python -O code/independent_check.py
g++ -O3 -std=c++17 -Wall -Wextra -pedantic code/witness_bfs.cpp -o witness_bfs
./witness_bfs 12 > data/bfs.json
\end{lstlisting}
each group in its own copy (FO's scripts are meant to run under
\texttt{python -O}; they use no assertions, so the flag does not disable any
check). The write phase ran them so on 29 September 2026 (Python~3.14.4, and
the WinLibs \texttt{g++}; about $2$\,s each): all passed. Every CSV is
byte-identical to the shipped one; every JSON record equals the shipped one
apart from Windows line endings, except that FO's \texttt{verification.json}
and \texttt{independent\_check.json} record the Python version
(\texttt{3.14.4} instead of the delivered \texttt{3.13.5}); the C++ output
equals \path{data/06-four-output-bfs.json} apart from line endings.

\subsection{What the checks do not establish}

No finite enumeration proves the universal statements; they rest on the
analytic tail of \cref{fo:sec:tail}, whose sufficiency for the finite
reduction is proved in the text. Breadth-first searches are not exhaustive
enumerations of all $n$-state automata; universality comes from the
structural bounds (AS). Agreement between two programs written in one
investigation is implementation independence, not logical independence of the
shared reduction, and not external review. No Lean, Rocq or other
proof-assistant verification was carried out for Part~IV, and none of its
statements is formalized anywhere in ProveIt.

\subsection{Routes to formal certification}\label{fo:sec:formal}

\emph{AS.} A formalization decomposes into a small finite part and a reusable
graph-theoretic part: define coloring orbits on a finite state type and prove
the rightmost-singular-factor lemma by induction on words; prove the two graph
descriptions with finite cyclic groups and the generalized Chinese remainder
theorem; check the four-color formulas by surjection counting or short
recurrences. For the finite proof no sophisticated solver is needed: a
verified integer-partition enumerator, a verified divisor enumerator and closed
arithmetic evaluation recompute the $2{,}082$ inequalities, and the same
framework would serve larger output alphabets. The small lower witnesses can be
certified by a verified reachability closure on $\{0,1,2,3\}^Q$: a list of
states closed under both transitions, with predecessor words, supplies both
directions of the equality between the certificate set and the reachable set.
The infinite tail needs only elementary inequalities for powers, finite
permutation orders and exact polynomial identities. The substantial remaining
external formalization is the Holzer--K\"onig theorem; a useful intermediate
formal theorem would expose it as an explicit hypothesis while proving all
upper bounds, finite cases and conditional attainment without further
assumptions.

\emph{FO.} The proof separates into finite graph theory, integer arithmetic
and the external monoid-generation input. A Lean or Rocq development could
first encode transformations, the rightmost-singular factorization and the two
collision-graph forms; then prove the coloring counts by finite combinatorial
sums, including the special two-vertex cycle convention; the whole small-state
certificate can then be a finite reflection theorem over
$\mathcal K^{\mathrm{FO}}_n$, with an explicit checker whose soundness follows
from the reduction. The analytic comparison avoids most real analysis: the
threshold is one integer inequality propagated by a fixed ratio, balancing is a
strict integer finite difference, period estimates can be expressed with cubed
inequalities, and parity splitting removes the square roots of the
presentation. One can either formalize the $U_{a,b}$ generation theorem or
expose it as a named dependency while proving all upper-bound and
conditional-attainment statements. No such formalization is supplied, and no
kernel-checked status is claimed.

\section{Further research questions}\label{fo:sec:questions}

AS and FO close with eight questions each. Where both ask the same question it
is printed once, with both sources' comments; where Part~III (or Part~II)
already asks it, the counterpart is named; questions that Part~III has
answered are printed with the answer, not as open. AS states its questions as
directions arising from its manuscript (apart from Davies's cited
variable-output problem), not as claims that a comprehensive search has
verified their open status; FO says the first three concern the remaining
mathematical obstruction rather than a computational limit.

\begin{question}[The next fixed output alphabet; AS and FO. Answered by Part~III]\label{fo:q:next}
Can the exact binary reversal maximum with five outputs be determined for all
$n\ge7$, and can non-effective fixed-$k$ thresholds be replaced by explicit
thresholds and bounded certificates (AS)? For every fixed $k\ge5$, is Davies's
coprime two-cycle lower bound optimal for all $n\ge7$ with $k<n$, and can
equality be proved uniformly in $n$ and $k$ (FO)?
\end{question}
\emph{Answered, except for the uniform part:} Part~III proves the formula for
$k=5,6,7$ and every $n\ge\max(7,k+1)$ (\cref{evo:thm:smallk}) and, for every
$k\ge4$, with the explicit threshold $N(k)\le3k^4$ (\cref{evo:thm:eventual});
the full two-parameter statement is \cref{evo:q:complete}, open for $k\ge8$
below $N(k)$ outside the certified finite ranges. AS had observed that for four
outputs the dominant palette split $2+2$ contributes the split-independent
$6\cdot2^n$, whereas for five outputs the competing $2+3$ and $3+2$ terms
depend on the imbalance of the cycles, so the leading palette terms should be
separated from a uniformly controlled remainder before residual vertices and
multiple components are treated, without assuming that the $k=4$ estimates
transfer unchanged. FO had observed that its proof works because the
biclique count splits into a split-independent leading term and one strictly
convex correction, that for larger $k$ several palette splits compete, and that
a first step is a uniform balancing inequality for $\Bip_k(a,b)-ab$ with
structural gaps for repeated bicliques and isolated cycles; Part~III's
\cref{evo:thm:balance,evo:cor:split} supply that balancing.

\begin{question}[Uniformity when the number of outputs grows; AS]\label{fo:as:q:growing}
For which functions $k=k(n)$ can the published coprime two-cycle construction be
proved optimal, and what inequalities would settle Davies's full Problem~2?
\end{question}
This is Part~III's \cref{evo:q:growing}, partly answered there ($4\le
k\le(n/3)^{1/4}$). AS: it needs uniform control of proper-coloring counts and
available cyclic periods; an estimate valid for each fixed $k$ may lose all
strength as $k$ grows, so explicit dependence on $k$ is an essential part of
the question, not an afterthought.

\begin{question}[Which singular generators saturate the improper set? AS and FO]\label{fo:q:saturate}
Fix the optimal two-cycle permutation and a singular map with exactly one
cross-cycle double fiber; characterize the maps for which every improper
four-coloring is reachable from a primitive proper $2+2$ output map (AS). For
the optimal cycles and a primitive disjoint two-palette output map, which
rank-$(n-1)$ cross-collision transformations reach every improper coloring
(FO)?
\end{question}
This is Part~III's \cref{evo:q:classify} at $k=4$. AS: a complete answer would
upgrade the necessary conditions to a classification of transition pairs; it
is not enough to classify kernels, since the placement of the image and the
permutations induced away from the double fiber affect the generated monoid.
FO: \cref{fo:thm:rigidity} reduces the problem to this reachability question and
\cref{fo:fo:rem:notsufficient} shows why the reduction is not yet a
classification; one could first classify these transformations modulo the
centralizer of the two-cycle permutation, then seek a graph or semigroup
criterion; requiring the monoid to equal $U_{a,b}$ is sufficient but may be
stronger than necessary for its four-color projection.

\begin{question}[Stability radius, second maximum and the singular generator; AS and FO]\label{fo:q:stability}
What is the smallest deficit below $R_2(n,4)$ that can occur without the
structure of \cref{fo:as:thm:stability}, and what is the exact second-largest
orbit size (AS)? Does a deficit within $o(2^n)$ of the optimum force the
singular generator to have exactly one cross collision, and under stronger
near-optimality can one bound a natural distance from the family of
extremizing transition tables (FO)?
\end{question}
These are the $k=4$ cases of Part~III's \cref{evo:q:second,evo:q:stability}
(Part~II's \cref{tor:q:second}). AS: the radius $a_nb_n/2$ separates structural
failures from saturation failures but is not proved best possible;
determining the first failed saturation examples among pairs satisfying
(i)--(iii) could be more informative than optimizing the exponential
estimates. FO: the penalty $\Omega^{\mathrm e}_4(a,b)$ is of order
$ab\,2^{\max(a,b)}$ for balanced parts, so it isolates one intermediate scale
but does not settle an $o(2^n)$ criterion; a sharper hierarchy of obstruction
counts may distinguish the number and arrangement of several collisions.

\begin{question}[Several collisions; AS and FO]\label{fo:q:collisions}
If the singular letter is injective on both optimal cycles and has $q\ge2$
cross-cycle double fibers, what is the sharp loss in reachable colorings (AS)?
For $r\ge2$ distinct cross collisions, what is the sharp number of colorings
excluded beyond the one-collision obstruction (FO)?
\end{question}
Part~III's matching hierarchy (\cref{evo:thm:matching}) already gives, for $t$
disjoint cross kernel pairs, the upper bound $4^{a+b}-W_{4,t}(a,b)+ab$ with the
exact count \eqref{evo:eq:matchingegf}; its sharpness is \cref{evo:q:matching}.
AS: the $24ab$ obstruction counts only colorings with exactly one equal cross
pair; counting colorings whose equality pattern is a small bipartite graph
could give penalties depending on $q$ and on the relative rotation positions
of the pairs, and the rightmost-singular-factor argument gives the precise
compatibility condition to exploit. FO: counting colorings with fewer than $r$
monochromatic cross edges gives an immediate lower bound, but the orbit of the
whole kernel pattern under $p$ keeps positional information that an edge count
loses; exact formulas for small kernel patterns could improve both stability
and classification.

\begin{question}[Constructive word bounds; AS]\label{fo:as:q:words}
For the standard optimal pair, how long a word is needed to reach an arbitrary
improper coloring? Can such a word be found without exploring $4^n$ colorings
or the entire transition monoid?
\end{question}
AS: the representative proof gives a target transformation in $U_{a,b}$ but no
bound on its word length in the two generators; a constructive refinement of
the generation theorem would turn saturation into an efficient synthesis
algorithm and give stronger finite certificates. (Compare Part~III's
\cref{evo:q:recognize}.)

\begin{question}[Enumerating inequivalent extremizers; AS]\label{fo:as:q:enumerate}
How many extremal transition-output triples exist modulo state relabeling,
input-letter interchange and output relabeling?
\end{question}
AS: \cref{fo:as:thm:outputmaps} counts output maps on one fixed pair only;
a classification of saturating singular generators and their centralizer
actions would be needed before an orbit-counting formula could be applied.

\begin{question}[Removing the computational boundary; AS and FO]\label{fo:q:computation}
Can the structural comparisons be proved uniformly already for $n\ge7$, leaving
only the three small equality witnesses, or can the certificate be checked by
a small proof-assistant kernel (AS)? Can the inequalities of
\cref{fo:sec:tail} be strengthened enough to cover $7\le n\le25$ without a
finite certificate (FO)?
\end{question}
AS: either outcome would strengthen confidence; the first needs tighter
small-$n$ inequalities, the second is directly actionable because the
certificate has just $2{,}082$ integer comparisons, not a search over automata.
FO: this would simplify the trust boundary rather than change the theorem; the
difficulty is genuine, since small $n\equiv2\pmod4$ have an optimal split
farther from the midpoint and a graph with isolated states can become the
closest competitor, and the recorded runner-up tags identify the cases a
uniform proof must dominate.

\begin{question}[Four-color projections versus whole transformation monoids; FO]\label{fo:fo:q:monoids}
Can a two-generated monoid different from $U_{a_n,b_n}$ attain the maximum
four-color orbit, and how small can such a monoid be?
\end{question}
This is the last part of Part~III's \cref{evo:q:classify} (manuscript~01) at
$k=4$. FO: a positive answer would show explicitly that extremal coloring
orbits and extremal monoid sizes are different optimization problems; neither
\cref{fo:thm:main} nor \cref{fo:thm:rigidity} settles the
largest-two-generated-transformation-monoid question.

\begin{question}[Attainable near-maximum sizes; FO]\label{fo:fo:q:spectrum}
Which values immediately below $R_2(n,4)$ occur as reversal complexities of
minimal $n$-state binary DFAOs with four outputs?
\end{question}
This is Part~III's \cref{evo:q:spectrum} at $k=4$. FO: the missing proper-orbit
inventory describes one structured source of gaps, incomplete reachability
among improper colorings another; it would be valuable to distinguish the two
mechanisms and decide whether they force intervals of unattainable sizes near
the maximum.

\begin{question}[A complete formal theorem; FO]\label{fo:fo:q:formal}
Can the collision classification, certificate checker, strict comparison and
the two-generator monoid theorem be formalized together, so that both sides of
\eqref{fo:eq:main} are kernel-checked without admitted inputs?
\end{question}
This is Part~III's \cref{evo:q:formal} at $k=4$; the two manuscripts' plans are
in \cref{fo:sec:formal}. FO: the short upper-bound reduction is a natural first
milestone, and the published generation theorem likely the more substantial
algebraic component; separating the tasks allows a formally verified universal
upper bound before the attainment formalization is complete.

\section{Conclusion of Part~IV}\label{fo:sec:conclusion}

\emph{AS.} The four-output problem separates completely into a chromatic upper
bound and an existing monoid construction that attains it. The identity for
$\Bip_4(a,b)$ makes the universal comparison explicit: beyond $n=25$ every
collision structure except a spanning coprime biclique has a strictly larger
exponential deficit, and strict balancing selects the closest coprime split;
the finite certificate covers every smaller state count. Equality forces not
only the best cycle sizes but also a single cross collision and primitive
disjoint binary output words. The result is more than a collection of matching
numerical values: an all-state formula, an explicit proof boundary, a
quantitative structural theorem for near-extremizers and an exact description
of the missing states. Its claims are confined to four outputs; the uniform
variable-output conjecture and the classification of all saturating
transition pairs remain separate tasks.

\emph{FO.} The four-output problem has an exact all-state answer because one
collision already forces a very restricted graph, and four-color biclique
counts separate its possible shapes sharply enough; after this reduction only
the closest coprime two-cycle split survives. Published generation results
attain the bound, while strictness yields necessary extremizer rigidity and a
meaningful stability theorem. The result resolves the four-output slice of the
optimality problem, relative to the proof and finite certificate given; it does
not resolve the variable-output question or classify every extremizing
singular map, and these remaining issues are isolated as explicit structural
and reachability problems rather than an undifferentiated search over automata.

\emph{The merge.} Both conclusions were written before Part~III: the
four-output slice of Davies's Problem~2 is Part~III's (\cref{evo:thm:main}), and
what Part~IV contributes to it is the second route with threshold $26$, the
stability theorems, the orbit inventory, the output count and the minimal
recurrence.

\section{Sources, dependency boundaries and provenance}\label{fo:sec:audit}

This section follows the two manuscripts' audits, dated 29 September 2026 and
shipped verbatim: AS's \path{07-all-state-SOURCE_AUDIT.md} and
\path{07-all-state-PROOF_AUDIT.md} (delivered in \texttt{notes/}), and FO's
\path{06-four-output-SOURCE_AUDIT.md} and \path{06-four-output-PROOF_AUDIT.md}.

\subsection{Repository evidence the manuscripts inspected}

Both read this report through the GitHub connector, at the pins

instead of treating an unrefereed repository statement as an axiom.

\emph{Stale text in the shipped audits and the unshipped manuscripts.} FO
writes that Part~II ``explicitly leaves the four-or-more-output cases open''
and that the contribution relative to the snapshot is the all-state
four-output upper bound; its source audit lists the exact result for $k\ge4$
as ``explicitly left open there''. AS writes that the eventual audit records

[the repository's] fixed-four-output eventual analysis effective at every state
count''. Both were true of the text at the pins; Part~III, written in
